The demo is a table of attention gaps. Night school mines the same misses as moments and rewrites the debugging skill against them, with proof.

### Ledger-built compaction at the cost-optimal reset point [R1 · Tokens and speed; build; escalated; value 5, L]
Today every session runs until Claude Code compacts on its own at about 686k tokens, so each model call re-sends about 372k tokens on average. Foreman would choose the reset point from the project's measured costs and fire it at step boundaries. It would also answer the engine's compaction hook with a compaction it builds itself from the brief and ledger, with no summariser call, carrying the live working set over word for word and freshly read from disk.
- mechanism: (1) Per-project cost numbers from transcripts (fmcost.scan): b = context just after a reset (47-70k), g = context growth per call (~2.0-2.6k), K = cost of one reset (the summary call, the cache rewrite, and the re-reads after it, R). Mean cost per call is about b + (T-b)/2 + K*g/(T-b), so the best reset point is T* = b + sqrt(2*K*g). (2) foreman-ui freshen() today fires only on a task-done toast, and only above a fixed 40%. Extend it to step-done, gate it on context >= T*, and call $.session.compact({instructions}). (3) Add a foreman-ui 'session.compact' hook for the plugin, auto and precompute triggers. It answers {messages} itself: the last N turns and every user message kept by engine handle, plus one built message from a new `fm handoff --json`. That message holds the active brief (step, Resume here, criteria, evidence state), open asks and decisions, the files touched this segment (ledger 'touched' events) re-read from disk now at the fm outline ranges in use, and the last failing output. If the handoff can't be built, it falls through to next(e), the model summary. (4) As a backstop, set CLAUDE_CODE_AUTO_COMPACT_WINDOW in the settings env (through fm ask, because settings are guarded) so compaction can't drift back to 686k.
- first slice: M: `fm cost --resets` prints b, g, K, R, T* and the simulated saving for this project from its own transcripts. freshen() fires on step and task boundaries at T* instead of the fixed 40%, and each reset writes a ledger event with tokens before and after. Its silent `.catch(() => undefined)` becomes a logged failure. This alone roughly halves input-side tokens. The ledger-built session.compact answer is slice 2.
- bar/numbers: 1: no coding agent compacts deterministically from a task ledger. Claude Code, Cursor, Aider and OpenHands all summarise with the model. 2: I replayed 3 real sessions using their real per-call growth. Resetting at 200k with a 40k carry-over costs 0.45-0.47x the input-side tokens that actually ran, and still 0.68-0.72x with a 100k re-read penalty. The analytic optimum with a no-summary reset is about 0.3x.
- verdict: escalated to: Rebirth: sessions die at every milestone or thrash and are reborn from a capsule proven sufficient by a quiz. Picking when to compact is right, and its T* formula is reused. Rebirth starts a fresh session from a capsule proven by a quiz, and also resets the tree to green on a thrash.

### Edit-path latency router [R1 · Tokens and speed; skip; kill; value 5, M]
On this machine an edit made through a Bash heredoc or sed -i takes about a second at the median and two minutes at p90. The same edit through the Edit tool returns in about 20 ms. Foreman would learn, per project, which tool path is fast for each kind of action from its own timing log, and steer the agent onto the fast one by quoting the measured numbers.
- mechanism: (1) Add `fm latency`. It groups the tool rows in events.jsonl (each already has ms, tool, target, project and agent_type) by project, action class and path. Classes: write via Bash (python3 heredoc, sed -i, cat >), write via Edit/Write, read, test, ssh. For each it prints p50, p90 and hours lost. (2) In PreToolUse (fmhooks._pre_tool_use; fmguard's _heredocs()/_write_targets() already find what a Bash command writes): when the command is a single-file text edit, and this project's write-via-Bash p90 is over 10 s while Edit/Write p90 is under 1 s, refuse once per session with the numbers, e.g. 'here Bash edits p90 146 s, Edit p90 30 ms: use Edit'. A `# fm: bulk` marker lets real multi-file scripted rewrites through. (3) Record the steer and the outcome so fm latency shows hours saved.
- first slice: M: the fm latency report plus the PreToolUse steer. In week one it is a note; it becomes a one-time refusal once the per-project numbers hold. Tests use fixture events.
- bar/numbers: 2: on the volt repo each edit's p90 wall-clock goes from 146 s to about 0.03 s (about 1000x), and 18.7 h of tool waiting in 7 days goes away.
- verdict: A refusal quoting latency numbers is a nudge around a stall. Fix the stall at its root (Harness latency bisect, kept).

### Harness latency bisect [R1 · Tokens and speed; build; keep; value 4, M]
Foreman would trace every slow tool call to a cause, not just slow calls in its own hooks. It does this by comparing each slow call with near-identical fast ones and running controlled probes, then files the fix. Today fm doctor checks only Foreman's hook latency. Nothing explained the Bash-write stalls, or Foreman's own one-line bookkeeping commands taking up to 10 minutes.
- mechanism: (1) Per call, events.jsonl already has ms, tool, target, project, agent_type and session. Add whether the command wrote files (fmguard._write_targets) and the hook-chain timings Claude Code writes into transcripts (stop_hook_summary hookInfos durationMs). (2) Attribution: for every class whose p90 is more than 10x its p50, contrast slow calls with fast ones of the same class on repo, path, writes, plugins and concurrent work, and rank the suspects. (3) A detached `fm doctor --latency` runs probes: `true`, a one-line write in a scratch dir, the same write inside the repo, and `fm task log` on a scratch task, each with and without a suspect. It reproduces the real harness path through fm session or claude -p. (4) It outputs a culprit line plus a captured FIX item with the evidence, and fm friction tracks hours lost per week.
- first slice: M: `fm latency --why`, an attribution report over events.jsonl (classes, p50/p90, hours lost, top suspects by contrast), wired into fm doctor and fm friction. Probes are slice 2.
- bar/numbers: 2: it turns multi-minute stalls into explained, fixable findings. The measured stall budget is about 18.7 h/week of Bash writes plus Foreman bookkeeping commands that take minutes.
- verdict: Passes: it explains why a one-line write sometimes takes 2 minutes by contrasting slow and fast calls and running probes. A real root cause no agent finds for itself.

### The midpoint law: a four-number cost model of every session [R1 · Tokens and speed; build; escalated; value 4, M]
Foreman would explain and forecast each session's token bill from four measured numbers: calls N, post-reset baseline b, reset point T and reset cost K. It would price every lever before anyone builds it. Today fm cost prints totals by task and tool. It can't show that trimming outputs or Foreman's own notes barely moves the bill, while the call count and the reset point do.
- mechanism: fmcost.scan already walks the usage of every reply. Add: split the session at compact_boundary records and fit b, T, g and K (K = reset call + cache rewrite + post-reset re-reads of files read before the reset). Count N by call class: fm bookkeeping, read-run continuation, verify, edit via Bash vs Edit, agent. Predict cost = sum over cycles of N_c*(b+T)/2 + resets*K, and print the gap from actual. Print counterfactuals: reset at T*, bookkeeping in-text (fewer calls), baseline -Xk, effort routing (less output). Per task, show N and tokens in fm task finish and the digest. fm friction, fm evolve and fm bench use the model as their fitness function. Any Foreman change, such as a new mandatory fm call or a rules line, is priced in tokens per finished task before merge and re-measured after.
- first slice: M: `fm cost --model [--days N]` over the existing transcripts. It prints fitted b, T, g and K, N by class, predicted vs actual, and five counterfactual lines, tested on a synthetic transcript fixture.
- bar/numbers: 4: the user can see and predict what actually drives the bill; no agent shows this. 3: every later Foreman change is judged by measured cost, so efficiency gains add up with proof and regressions in Foreman's own process get caught.
- verdict: escalated to: Context PGO: Foreman profiles where every token goes and recompiles its own prompts, injections and output digests to the smallest form that still passes. A cost model alone is a table; it becomes Context PGO's pricing model for every lever.

### Zero-round-trip bookkeeping [R1 · Tokens and speed; build; keep; value 4, M]
Some state writes need no answer: log lines, captures, decisions, surprises, step notes. These would ride in the agent's message as `fm:` lines that Foreman applies from its hooks, so they cost no model round trip. Today each one is its own tool call that re-sends the whole context (about 372k tokens), and it goes through the Bash path, which sometimes stalls for minutes. Foreman writes what it can infer itself, such as advancing a step when its evidence passed.
- mechanism: Syntax: a line that starts with `fm: ` followed by a whitelisted command: task log, capture, decide, surprise, task hypo, task assume add, task step text. Two places apply them. (a) The Stop hook reads last_assistant_message, which fmhooks.stop already reads. (b) Mid-turn, a foreman-ui 'tool.call' hook reads the latest assistant message through $.session.messages() before the next tool runs. Lines are parsed with shlex into fmcli argv, never a shell. Each (message id, line) pair applies once. Results, new ids and errors come back in the next PreToolUse or UserPromptSubmit note and in the band. Never accepted in text: evidence, done/finish, ask, standing, trust, allow, check, so gates and consent stay tool calls. Inference: _auto_evidence already verifies the step a passing check ran in; extend it to advance the step.
- first slice: M: the Stop hook alone applies task log, capture and decide. Add one rules line, one output-style line, an 'inline' ledger event and a saved-calls counter in fm cost. Tests feed a fake last_assistant_message.
- bar/numbers: 1: no agent applies state writes from a side channel in its prose instead of tool calls. 2: bookkeeping round trips drop to about 0; today they are 11-17% of main-thread calls.
- verdict: Passes on numbers rather than spectacle: it removes whole round trips (each re-sends the full context) for state writes, with a whitelist that keeps gates and consent as tool calls. Cheap, and it multiplies with Rebirth.

### Hash-pinned code facts: answers that expire when the code changes [R1 · Tokens and speed; build; escalated; value 3, M]
Foreman would answer 'where/how does X work' from a local memo of what earlier sessions, lanes and subagents already established. Every fact is pinned to the content hash of the definition it describes and dropped as soon as that code changes. A miss goes to a cheap child that must cite lines, and its answer joins the memo. One `fm peek` call replaces a run of exploratory reads, and read-only subagents get the same answers over fm mcp.
- mechanism: A fact is (file, symbol from fmmap.outline/ast, sha1 of the symbol's source, claim text, file:line citation, source session or agent), stored in state/projects/<slug>/facts.jsonl. Sources: peek answers, and a nightly fm night job that mines transcripts. A read or grep of a symbol followed by assistant text or an edit that names it becomes a claim. Query `fm peek "question" [paths]` ranks facts by identifier overlap, keeps only those whose symbol hash matches the working tree now, and prints at most 12 cited lines. On a miss it runs a budgeted Haiku child (an fmideas child_cmd variant with --tools Read,Grep,Glob) under fmbudget and stores the answer. A `peek` tool in fmmcp.TOOLS lets fm-reviewer and fm-recon, which have no Bash, use it; sensitive repos expose nothing, as fmmcp already does.
- first slice: M: fm peek with the memo filled only from peek answers (the child path), hash invalidation, the MCP tool and budget records. Mining transcripts is slice 2.
- bar/numbers: 3: it compounds. Each session's reading makes later sessions, lanes and subagents cheaper, and hash pinning means it never serves stale code.
- verdict: escalated to: Consequence engine: executed answers to 'what breaks if...', computed before anyone asks. Hash-pinned facts are the consequence engine's memory layer; 'where is X' is the cheap end of 'what breaks if X'.

### Cache-safe per-request effort and model routing [R1 · Tokens and speed; build; escalated; value 3, M]
The foreman-ui mod would set the reasoning effort of each model request from what the step is: a bookkeeping follow-up, the next read in an exploration run, or the call after a passing check, as against planning, debugging or an edit. It would switch models only on the first request after a reset, when the prompt cache is rebuilt anyway. Routing happens per request inside an interactive session, with an A/B record showing whether quality held.
- mechanism: A turn.step hook can rewrite model and effort per request, on main or a subagent (TurnStepInput). It classifies each request from the previous tool result and Foreman's stage (fm ui --json carries task, stage and step). Mechanical requests get low or medium effort; judgement requests stay unchanged. The model switches only when the request follows a session.compact (seen by the compact hook) and the coming stretch is tagged mechanical (close-out, docs, changelog). Every routed request logs to the ledger: class, effort, model, output tokens, cache_creation on the next request, and any later reopen or failure. fm bench replays gate the policy, and a class reverts on its own if failures rise.
- first slice: M: effort-only routing for two classes (after an fm bookkeeping result; after a passing verify), behind a userConfig flag. Before widening, measure whether an effort change causes a cache rewrite (cache_creation on the next request).
- bar/numbers: 1: no coding agent re-routes effort or model per request by what the step is, with switch points chosen so the prompt cache survives.
- verdict: escalated to: Opus on call: the cheapest model attempts every step first, checked by machines not by Opus; the frontier model is paged only on failure. Per-request effort is a knob inside Opus's own loop; the cascade removes Opus from most steps.

### Push verification: affected tests run themselves and count as evidence [R1 · Tokens and speed; build; escalated; value 3, M]
After each edit, Foreman runs the linked tests in the background and delivers the result to the agent on its next turn. When the command is a criterion's verify command on the same tree, the result is recorded as evidence. The agent stops spending calls, and waiting, on 'now run the tests'.
- mechanism: The async PostToolUse hook watches Edit, Write and Bash writes and debounces for 2-3 s. If fm check --affected or the active criterion's verify command is cheap (stored run time under 60 s), it runs detached, keyed by worktree_id. The async hook response carries '✓ 14 linked tests pass (tree abc123)' or the failing tail; async_hook_response attachments already appear in transcripts. If the tree id still matches when the agent later runs the same command, fm check reuses the pass (existing tree-hash reuse) and _auto_evidence records it as [ran]. Stale results are dropped.
- first slice: M: for Python projects with fm check --affected set, run the affected tests in the background after edits and deliver them as an async note, with reuse through the tree id. Measure verify calls per task before and after with fm cost.
- bar/numbers: 1 (weak): test results are pushed without being asked for and count as evidence on the same tree. 2: verify calls and their waits drop for the common edit-then-test loop.
- verdict: escalated to: Shadow twin: every edit is executed in a twin worktree within seconds; only behaviour flips reach the model, and edits can be tried before they land. Background affected tests on the real tree. The twin adds per-edit blame, flips-only delivery (silence costs zero tokens) and simulate-before-edit.

### fm graph: one bitemporal work graph joining code to everything Foreman has done to it [R1 · Knowledge graph; build; escalated; value 5, L]
Foreman's memory today is six separate flat indexes: BM25 recall over briefs, tripwires.json (file to lessons), failures.jsonl signatures, map.json co-change, fm relate edges between open tasks, and vetoes.json words. MASTER.md section 6 says 'Knowledge graph | none installed'. This adds one typed, time-stamped graph that joins files, symbols and tests to tasks, criteria, evidence runs at real git trees, commits, lessons, decisions, steers, guard rules, failures and escapes. Every edge records where it came from and when it was true, so the graph can be queried as of any date. No coding agent keeps a symbol-level graph of its own work history joined to outcomes.
- mechanism: A new stdlib module, plugin/lib/fmgraph.py, stores state/projects/<slug>/graph.db (sqlite3 is stdlib; FTS5 and JSON1 are present; the import costs 5.7 ms). Tables: node(id, kind, key, label, attrs JSON, first_seen, gone_at); edge(src, dst, rel, valid_from, valid_to, ingested_at, weight, origin, conf in OBSERVED|DERIVED|INFERRED); cursor(source, pos). Ingestion is incremental and uses no LLM. (1) ledger.jsonl is read from a stored byte offset and inode. An inode change detects tidy's monthly roll. Malformed lines are skipped (volt's ledger has one). The events task_new, focus, touched, evidence, check_run, commit, escape, guard_block, decision, note 'steer:', research, assumption, surprise, prove, audit and subagent become nodes and edges keyed by task and timestamp. (2) git log -p -U0 runs from the last ingested sha. It gives commit-to-file edges, commit-to-symbol edges from the hunk headers (git's funcname line; Python post-images are re-read with ast for exact qualnames), and commit-to-task edges from T-ids in subjects plus the ledger's commit events. (3) Briefs whose mtime moved are re-read for criteria, steps, Lessons, Files touched, Hypotheses and Assumptions. (4) Evidence trees are diffed (see the fix-delta item). (5) graphify-out/graph.json is read when present (see the graphify item). A symbol id is 'path::qualname' and joins to graphify ids by (source_file, label). A detached `fm graph sync`, spawned like fmrelate.spawn, runs from fm task done (beside write_tripwires at fmcli.py:420), fm check and SessionStart; fm night does full rebuilds. The guard path never opens the DB: sync writes small JSON indexes (like tripwires.json) for PreToolUse. PostToolUseFailure may query sqlite (under 10 ms). Query verbs: fm graph risk PATH[:SYMBOL] | history SYMBOL | near ID | path A B | --as-of DATE, plus a read-only 'graph' tool in fmmcp.TOOLS so Codex, Gemini and opencode sessions get it too.
- first slice: fmgraph.py ingesting the ledger (task, touched, evidence, commit, escape, guard_block, decision, task_done lesson) and git hunks, with cursors, `fm graph sync`, and `fm graph risk PATH`. For each symbol, risk prints the tasks that changed it, the escapes against those tasks, the newest lesson and the last commit. plugin/tests/test_graph.py runs over a temp git repo and a synthetic ledger and checks that an incremental re-sync adds nothing twice and that a rolled ledger is re-read.
- bar/numbers: 1 and 4. It is a new kind of capability: the agent's own outcomes joined to code at symbol level, over time. Every other item in this file queries it. On its own it answers 'what happened to this function' with its tasks, escapes, lessons and decisions in one reply.
- verdict: escalated to: Consequence engine: executed answers to 'what breaks if...', computed before anyone asks. A graph store is substrate, and its demo is a query. The consequence engine uses it as the history layer and answers counterfactuals by running them.

### Fix-delta memory: every red-to-green Foreman already recorded becomes a labelled causal example [R1 · Knowledge graph; build; escalated; value 5, M]
Every evidence line already stores a real git tree (fmcore.worktree_tree writes '[tree d23e294f48ab]'), but nothing ever diffs those trees. The hunks between the last red tree and the first green tree of the same verify command are the observed fix. The hunks between a green tree and a later red one are the observed break. Ingested as failure/test -fixed_by-> symbol and symbol -broke-> test edges, they give debugging priors no agent has. Today the hint says 'a similar failure came up in T-x'. With this it says 'this signature last went green when 9 lines of fmguard._top changed, and the hypothesis that held was H2'.
- mechanism: Read each brief's Verification evidence lines with a regex ((step|ac N) `cmd` -> [failure mark] ... [tree X] (ts)) and group them by (task, cmd). For each red-to-green pair, run `git diff -U0 --no-renames TREE_RED TREE_GREEN` (the trees are real objects in the repo), map the hunks to enclosing symbols, and store a fixdelta node: task, cmd, files, symbols, lines added and removed, and an excerpt of at most 40 redacted lines. Links: the fixdelta -fixes-> the failure signatures that task met between the two runs (failures.jsonl rows, via fmrecall.failure_signature); -fixes-> test ids (failing test names fm check already parses into flakes.json via note_flakes); -confirmed_by-> the brief's confirmed hypothesis; -lesson-> the task's lesson. Green-to-red pairs become 'broke' edges from the changed symbols to the test. When a new failure happens, fmrecall.note_failure / seen_before (PostToolUseFailure) asks for fixdeltas on the same signature or test and prints the symbol, the size and the confirmed hypothesis. fm-debugger briefs get the top three as priors. `fm graph fixes SIG|TEST|SYMBOL` lists them.
- first slice: Standalone over briefs and git (it moves onto graph.db when that lands): `fm graph fixes` builds fixdeltas from the evidence lines, and seen_before's hint gains 'fixed by <symbol> (+a/-b lines, T-x; held: H2 ...)'. Test: a temp repo with a red and then a green evidence pair yields one fixdelta on the right function, and the hint names it.
- bar/numbers: 1: no coding agent mines its own red-to-green trajectories into causal fix examples. 2: time to root cause, because the first hint points at the symbol that fixed the same failure last time instead of at a task to re-read.
- verdict: escalated to: Bug nose: names the bug before a file is read, and smells bug-shaped hunks at edit time, graded on every red. The labelled red-to-green deltas are the nose's real-bug corpus.

### Since-last-green suspects: automatic hunk-level delta debugging inside the task [R1 · Knowledge graph; build; escalated; value 4, M]
When a check that passed earlier in the task fails, Foreman already holds the exact tree it last passed on. Diffing that tree against the working tree and ranking the changed hunks by graph priors (this test's broke edges, the symbol's escape and fix history) names the likely culprit before anything is re-read. When there are only a few hunks, Foreman then reverts each one alone in a scratch worktree and reports the one whose removal turns the check green. That is Zeller-style delta debugging, automated over the agent's own uncommitted trajectory.
- mechanism: On PostToolUseFailure, or when fm check has a red gate: for the failing command, find the newest evidence or check_run in this task that exited 0, and its tree. Run `git diff -U0 GREEN_TREE <worktree_tree now>` and map the hunks to symbols. Score each symbol from observed broke(symbol, test) edges, fixdelta overlap, escape rate, and how recently it was edited (touched events). Emit a 3-line note: 'green at 14:02 (tree ...); since then 5 hunks; most likely: fmguard._top (broke this test twice), _hint'. `fm task suspects ID --run CMD [--bisect]` reuses task_prove_hunks' scratch-worktree machinery to ask the inverse question. It reverts each hunk alone and runs CMD; if no single hunk is enough, it tries halves (ddmin). A hunk whose revert turns CMD green is recorded as evidence and as a 'broke' edge, which feeds the sensitivity map.
- first slice: `fm task suspects ID --run CMD` with the ranking and --bisect on up to 20 hunks. The PostToolUseFailure note appears only when the task holds a green tree for the same command. Test: a temp repo with two hunks, one of which breaks the test; --bisect names that one.
- bar/numbers: 2: regressions introduced mid-task are the most common FIX loop, and this finds the culprit hunk mechanically, without spending tokens on re-reading. 1: no agent bisects its own uncommitted hunks against a recorded green snapshot.
- verdict: escalated to: Shadow twin: every edit is executed in a twin worktree within seconds; only behaviour flips reach the model, and edits can be tried before they land. Same need as Why-red; per-edit execution in the twin answers it instantly.

### Observed test-sensitivity map: learned test selection, and 'dark code' no check has ever caught [R1 · Knowledge graph; build; escalated; value 4, M]
fm check --affected picks tests by matching file-name stems (fmmap.tests_for). The graph learns which tests actually react to which symbols from what happened: an edit followed by green-to-red (broke), red-to-green fixes (fixed_by), and prove --hunks kills (a test that fails when a hunk is reverted is sensitive to that hunk). Foreman then runs the observed-sensitive tests first. It also exposes 'dark symbols': code changed by many tasks that no check has ever been seen to detect, where a green result is probably vacuous.
- mechanism: Edges symbol -sensitive-> test, with counts and last-seen time, come from fixdelta and break pairs, prove events (data.tests and the proven hunks), suspects --bisect results, and the failing test names fm check already parses (note_flakes). _check_affected merges the observed tests (weighted and decayed) with the name links. `fm graph dark [PATH]` lists symbols changed by at least N tasks that have no sensitivity edge. When a brief's scope or blast radius contains a dark symbol, fm next adds 'write a test that fails without this change first'. Test-first is already the norm; this aims it where history says the checks are blind.
- first slice: Standalone over the ledger and briefs: turn prove events and red/green pairs into sensitivity edges, add `fm graph dark`, and merge the observed tests into --affected. Tests for both on a temp repo.
- bar/numbers: 2: fewer escapes and faster gates, because the tests that have actually caught regressions here run first. 1: predictive test selection exists only as CI-scale server systems; this learns it locally from one agent's own trajectory with no instrumentation.
- verdict: escalated to: Shadow twin: every edit is executed in a twin worktree within seconds; only behaviour flips reach the model, and edits can be tried before they land. Learned test selection is how the twin chooses what to run per edit, learned as a side effect of running.

### Blast radius at plan time: what this change reaches, and what broke the last times it was touched [R1 · Knowledge graph; build; escalated; value 5, M]
At focus, Foreman adds BM25-related briefs and name-linked tests. The tier is self-declared, and fm next only says 'outgrown S' after three non-test files have already been edited. The graph gives every plan the change's reach: callers, files that usually change with it, observed-sensitive tests, open tasks with overlapping footprints, and decisions with path triggers. It also gives the history: escapes and fix-of-fix chains on those exact symbols. The plan gate uses this. If the scope reaches a symbol where most past changes later escaped, the tier goes up (adding the adversary lens) unless the override is logged with a reason.
- mechanism: Seeds: the scope globs, resolved to files and then symbols, plus the backticked names in Interpretation, Approach and Steps (using the stale-fact tripwire's regex). Expand 1 or 2 hops over: calls and imports (from graphify when present, otherwise Python ast or `git grep -w` as fm impact does), co-change (fmmap._co_change), sensitivity edges, and the task-to-symbol edges of open tasks (predicted footprint = scope plus the Files touched of similar finished tasks). Score each symbol on: escape rate (tasks that changed it and later escaped, over all tasks that changed it), fix-of-fix depth (a task whose request names an earlier done task on the same symbol; fmcli._note_escapes already detects these), anchored lessons, and whether it is dark. cmd_focus writes a 'Blast radius' section of at most 8 lines next to Related. During planning, fm next names the top risk. An S plan whose risk passes the threshold gets 're-tier to M, or fm task log ID "radius override: ..."'.
- first slice: Standalone over git and the ledger (the 38 ms join): `fm graph radius ID`, plus the Blast radius section at focus. In this slice it only nudges and never refuses. Tests on a temp repo with synthetic escape events.
- bar/numbers: 2: escapes. For example, the graph knows fmguard._hint was changed by 4 tasks and 3 of them later escaped. 1: no agent plans against the measured defect history of the exact functions it is about to touch.
- verdict: escalated to: Consequence engine: executed answers to 'what breaks if...', computed before anyone asks. Inferred reach plus a tier nudge. The consequence engine runs the perturbation and returns failing tests, dark call sites and history.

### Reviews aimed by the escape graph: each lens is told exactly where it missed before [R1 · Knowledge graph; skip; kill; value 4, M]
fm audit prep gives each reviewer the six newest past findings for its lens across the whole project (fmcli._past_findings, matched on research file names). Every escape event already records which lenses passed the task that later broke. Joining diff hunks to symbols to past escapes to the lenses that missed them gives each lens a briefing for this diff. Example: 'on _top your adversary lens passed T-0354 and T-0397, and both later escaped (pipe-shell false blocks); check that class of bug again here'. The brief also names the regression fixture that now pins each past escape, and flags dark symbols. A lens that keeps missing in an area gets that area; a lens that never misses there gets a lighter brief.
- mechanism: At fm audit prep: take the frozen diff, map hunks to symbols, and query the graph for escapes on the tasks that changed those symbols (escape.data.lenses, by, title), with their fixdeltas and tests. Build a lens-miss matrix M[lens][symbol] counting escapes each lens passed. Each lens brief gets at most 4 'History on this diff' lines. For tier M, the rule 'intent plus the riskiest other lens' picks that other lens from M. New import or call edges between graphify communities (or between top-level packages when there is no graphify graph) add one 'boundary crossed' line to the maintainer lens. After a task closes, any later escape updates M automatically, so the review gets sharper on that exact code every time.
- first slice: History lines in fm audit prep, built from escapes on the diff's symbols (from git and the ledger), plus the lens-miss choice of the riskiest lens. Test: a synthetic escape on one function appears only in the brief of the lens that missed it.
- bar/numbers: 2: fewer escapes reach the user. 3: compounding, because every escape retrains where each lens looks, without editing any prompt.
- verdict: The demo is four extra lines in a reviewer brief, a nudge. Behaviour diffs aim review mechanically.

### Truth maintenance: a reversed decision or a false assumption flags everything built on it [R1 · Knowledge graph; build; escalated; value 4, M]
Decisions, assumptions, research notes, vetoes and dependency versions are premises. The tasks, commits, symbols and tests built while they held are dependents. Today a reversed decision is just a new row (--reverses), a false assumption re-checks only that task's own steps, and a stale research note is marked only when someone recalls it. The graph records rests_on edges. When a premise falls, it marks finished work and code built on it as suspect, transitively, names them in fm next, and captures one task to sweep them. This is a Doyle-style truth-maintenance system for agent work.
- mechanism: rests_on edges are DERIVED and carry their origin. They come from: a brief's Approach, Interpretation or Execution prompt citing a decision (date and words, via the decisions.md row regex), a research note name, an assumption number, a T-id or a veto; decisions logged while a task was active; and research citations (fmrecall._cites). A premise falls when: a decision event has reverses set, revisit_tag fires, an assumption is marked false (task_assume), a research note goes stale (fmrecall._stale), fm vetoes rm runs, or fm deps reports a major version bump of a library the symbols import. On a fall: walk rests_on breadth-first through task, commit and symbol, stopping at symbols rewritten since (by blame). Store suspect marks with the reason. fm next names the top dependents once, `fm graph suspects` lists them all, and fm capture --source discovered files a 're-check work built on <premise>' brief.
- first slice: rests_on edges from explicit citations only (T-ids, research names, decision dates), with propagation on --reverses and on an assumption marked false. Add `fm graph suspects`. Tests with two briefs and a reversed decision.
- bar/numbers: 1: no coding agent tracks the dependencies between its outputs and the beliefs behind them. 3: knowledge corrects itself instead of rotting.
- verdict: escalated to: Consequence engine: executed answers to 'what breaks if...', computed before anyone asks. Premise to dependent edges belong in the consequence engine's history layer, surfaced when a card is computed.

### Lessons anchored to symbols: the right lesson at the edit, plus duplicate, contradiction and supersession detection [R1 · Knowledge graph; build; escalated; value 4, M]
tripwires.json maps each file to every lesson ever left on it, and PreToolUse shows the newest. CHANGELOG.md has 190, MASTER.md 97, fmcli.py 70 and fmguard.py 53, so the lesson shown comes from whichever task finished last, not from the code being edited. Anchoring each lesson to the symbols its task's fix-deltas and commits changed lets the hook pick by the hunk being edited. It also lets the graph see lessons that were re-learned, lessons that contradict each other, and lessons that a later one supersedes on the same anchor.
- mechanism: Add lesson -about-> symbol edges, from the task's commits and fixdeltas, weighted by each symbol's share of the delta. Sync writes tripwires.json v2 for HEAD: {file: [[start, end, qualname, task, lesson]]}, with ranges from ast or hunk headers. _tripwire_note maps the Edit's old_string (or the Write's path) to a line range and picks the lesson with the most overlap, newest first among ties. Lesson hygiene runs in fm tidy or fm night, without an LLM. Same anchor and at least 3 shared content stems (fmrecall._tokens) means the lesson was re-learned: log it as a retrieval miss for backtesting, and merge the two in the shown text. Same anchor and opposite polarity on the shared key terms (the veto clause splitter: never/don't against always/must) is a contradiction: capture one 'reconcile lessons' item. A later lesson on the same anchor after an escape supersedes the older one. A lesson whose anchor was deleted is archived.
- first slice: Standalone over git and briefs: tripwires v2 with symbol ranges, _tripwire_note choosing by edit range, and `fm graph lessons --dupes`. Test: two lessons on two functions of one file; an edit to the second function shows that function's lesson.
- bar/numbers: 3: memory that keeps itself relevant and consistent. 2: fewer repeated mistakes at the exact code where they were made.
- verdict: escalated to: Lessons compile to code: memory stops being prose the model must read and becomes checks that run. Better retrieval of prose is still prose. Lessons compile to code turns lessons into checks that fire at the edit.

### Steer-protected code: Foreman notices when an edit undoes what the user explicitly asked for [R1 · Knowledge graph; build; escalated; value 4, M]
Steers are logged only as words ('steer: ...' notes, 33 in this project), and vetoes match key words in commands. Neither knows which code a steer produced. The graph attributes the delta that followed a steer (the evidence trees around it) to that steer, and keeps those line ranges mapped to HEAD. When a later edit rewrites or deletes them, PreToolUse warns and quotes the user, so a fix three tasks later can't silently revert what the user asked for.
- mechanism: For each steer note (task, ts): tree_before is the newest evidence tree in that task before ts, and tree_after is the tree of the next step_done evidence (or the task's commit). The delta's hunks become steered edges from the steer to a symbol or line range (conf DERIVED, weighted by delta size). Sync remaps the ranges to HEAD with `git blame -C --porcelain` on the few files involved and writes steered.json {file: [[start, end, steer text, task, date]]}. fmhooks._pre_tool_use adds a note, beside _veto_note and _tripwire_note, when an Edit's old_string overlaps a range by at least 50%. fm bench court, which already turns steers into judged cases, gets the steered diff to anchor its rubric.
- first slice: Standalone over the ledger, briefs and git: steered ranges from steer notes and evidence trees, `fm graph steered FILE`, and the PreToolUse note. Test: a steer followed by an edit, then a later edit that deletes those lines, gets the note.
- bar/numbers: 2: user corrections per task; a correction the user has to repeat costs the most. 1: no agent protects the code that came from user instructions.
- verdict: escalated to: Lessons compile to code: memory stops being prose the model must read and becomes checks that run. A warning note when steered lines change. Compiled, a steer becomes a test pinning the steered behaviour, which can't be ignored.

### Memory backtesting: as-of replays score and tune Foreman's own retrieval, for free [R1 · Knowledge graph; build; escalated; value 5, M]
Foreman benchmarks its instructions with paid session replays (fm bench, fm evolve) but never measures whether its memory surfaced the right thing at the right time. A bitemporal graph can answer, for each of about 300 finished tasks: what would recall, tripwires, fix-delta hints and blast radius have shown at focus time, using only what was known then? That is then scored against what mattered afterwards: files touched, failures hit, escapes suffered, lessons re-learned. Ranker weights are tuned offline, and a change ships only if the backtest improves. Memory improves itself with a number as proof, at no token cost.
- mechanism: `fm graph backtest [--channel related|tripwire|fixes|radius] [--grid]`. For each done task T with focus time t: keep only nodes and edges with ingested_at < t (and valid at t), and run the channel's query exactly as the code does (fmrecall.recall with skip=T). Compare the hits with ground truth from after t: Files touched (precision and recall@k of the 'start here' files); failure signatures met (did a fixdelta hint exist?); escapes later logged against T (did the radius flag that symbol?); lessons T wrote that duplicate an existing anchored lesson (a retrieval miss). Output a scorecard and the top misses. --grid sweeps HALF_LIFE, HITS, kind priors and the escape weight, and writes the best values to state (fmrecall reads the overrides). The gate works like fm bench gate: no channel may regress. It also measures the effect of interventions: tasks shown a tripwire or hint against comparable tasks in the same symbol risk band that weren't, by repeat-failure and escape rates.
- first slice: A backtest of the Related channel only: hit@4 against the files each task later touched and against later escapes, with as-of filtering. It prints the score and five misses. Tested on a synthetic project with known answers.
- bar/numbers: 3: compounding with proof; memory improves itself from its own history, and the improvement is measured. 1: no agent backtests its memory against its own outcomes.
- verdict: escalated to: Night school: every past mistake becomes a permanent test, and Foreman rewrites its own skills, hooks and agents overnight against them. As-of replay scoring is one of Night school's proof tiers for memory changes; alone its demo is a scorecard.

### Context packs by personalized PageRank: the ranked, budgeted read-set for a task [R1 · Knowledge graph; build; escalated; value 4, M]
The agent learns a codebase by exploring: this session's transcript alone has 1,380 Bash calls, mostly sed, grep and cat, plus 26 Read calls. Every subagent brief is put together by hand. The graph can compute the smallest ranked set of code spans a task needs to read. It is seeded at the task's scope and the names in its plan, and spreads over calls and imports, co-change, co-reading and work edges. The same pack goes to the main thread at focus and to fm-builder lanes, reviewers and fm-debugger.
- mechanism: Personalized PageRank (power iteration, stdlib, at most 50 iterations on the task's 2-hop subgraph) over weighted edges: calls and imports (graphify or ast); co_change (fmmap._co_change); co_read (files read in the same task's window, mined from transcripts by fmclaude._transcript: Read paths, plus read targets of Bash commands such as sed -n, cat, head, grep and fm outline, extracted with fmguard's shell tokenizer); touched-together (touched events); and lesson, decision and test attachments. The output is spans from fm outline (def line ranges) under a character budget, for example: 'read first: fmguard.py:1826-1990 check_bash; fmguard.py:400-440 _ends_body; test_guard_pipes.py IfsForms; lesson T-0576'. It is used by cmd_focus (after Related), fmlanes.builder_brief, audit prep and debugger briefs.
- first slice: `fm graph pack ID --budget 6000` over touched, co-change and ast-import edges (no transcript mining yet), printed at focus for M and L tasks. The backtest harness scores it by the share of files the task later edited that the pack contained.
- bar/numbers: 2: fewer tokens per finished task and less time before the first edit. 1: agents retrieve by embedding similarity or grep; none rank by the combined structure of the code and their own reading history.
- verdict: escalated to: Rebirth: sessions die at every milestone or thrash and are reborn from a capsule proven sufficient by a quiz. The ranked read-set becomes part of Rebirth's capsule, where it's tested by the sufficiency quiz rather than trusted.

### Cross-project transfer by fingerprint: machine habits and fix-deltas, not word matches [R1 · Knowledge graph; build; escalated; value 4, M]
Cross-project memory today is opt-in shared lessons matched by words (fm share), and the seen ideas grep sibling repos. A global graph links projects by keys that are safe to share: normalised failure signatures, command shapes, dependency names and imported API symbols. A failure in one project then reaches the fix found in another, and command habits that fail on this machine are caught before the command runs.
- mechanism: fm night or sync builds state/graph/global.db from each registered project's graph. Sensitive projects contribute hashed keys only, never text. Machine habits: PostToolUseFailure already computes the signature. Add the command shape (argv0, flag letters and quoting pattern, from the guard's tokenizer) and, from the same task, the next command with the same argv0 that succeeded. A shape that failed at least 3 times across at least 2 projects with the same signature goes into habits.json {shape: [sig, working example]}. On a match, _pre_tool_use adds a one-line note, e.g. 'here grep is ugrep and that pattern exceeds its regex limits; a form that worked before: ...'. Fix transfer: when a new failure's signature (or library symbol) matches a fixdelta from another project, the hint says 'seen in <project alias>: fixed in <library symbol> by ...', with a private()-filtered excerpt. This part is off unless fm share is on.
- first slice: A habit index built from every project's failures.jsonl plus command shapes, the PreToolUse note, and `fm graph habits`. Test with two temp projects that share a failing command shape.
- bar/numbers: 1: no agent learns its own environment-specific tool failures across repos and pre-empts them. 2: fewer turns wasted on known failures. 4: the user's projects teach each other without anything leaving the machine.
- verdict: escalated to: Lessons compile to code: memory stops being prose the model must read and becomes checks that run. Habit notes become compiled PreToolUse rewrites: the failing command shape is fixed automatically, not described.

### The work graph, seen: risk heat on what you're editing, and a time-slider explorer [R1 · Knowledge graph; skip; kill; value 3, M]
foreman-ui shows the task, steps, bench and budget. Nothing shows how the active work connects to its history. A pane card shows the active scope's symbols as a heat strip (escape rate, fixes, lessons, dark) with the single riskiest fact in words. A local explorer page shows the task's surrounding graph with an as-of time slider: what Foreman knew then against what it knows now, and which premises are suspect. Every node clicks through to fm why or recall text. Each glyph is a number that changes what to do next, not decoration.
- mechanism: fmwatch.view adds a graph field: {scope: [{symbol, tasks, escapes, fixes, lessons, dark, heat}], top: 'fmguard._hint: 3/4 later escaped · last fix T-0405', suspects: n}, read from graph.db and cached per HEAD and ledger offset. register.tsx renders a Raster heat strip with kit.cells / progressCells, plus a text version. `fm graph view ID` writes a self-contained HTML page (inline SVG force layout, data embedded, no network) into the state dir and prints its path. The Tauri desktop app can load the same JSON.
- first slice: The graph field in fm ui --json, plus a pane heat-strip card for the active task. Tests in mods/foreman-ui/tests and test_watch for the JSON.
- bar/numbers: 4: for the first time the user can see and question the agent's memory and risk model. 2: a suspect premise or a hot symbol is visible before the edit.
- verdict: A heat strip and a graph explorer: the demo is a picture of data. The mind view and consequence cards show what changes decisions.

### graphify as the code layer: consume its AST graph, and give Foreman's history back as an overlay [R1 · Knowledge graph; build; escalated; value 3, M]
graphify already builds a deterministic AST graph at zero token cost (the JARVIS one has 41,627 nodes and 86,358 edges, 96% EXTRACTED, 0 tokens), but it knows nothing about what happened to the code. Foreman knows the history but has only name-stem links and co-change for structure. Bridging both ways gives Foreman calls, imports and inheritance for blast radius and context packs without writing a parser. It also lets /graphify query answer 'why is it like this' with tasks, lessons and escapes on the same node ids.
- mechanism: Ingest: when <root>/graphify-out/graph.json exists, compare its built_at_commit with HEAD. Load it (0.2 s and 177 MB peak for the 56 MB JARVIS graph, so only in a detached sync or fm night). Keep the code relations (calls, imports, imports_from, inherits, implements, contains, method, references) with source_file and source_location, and join them to Foreman symbols by (source_file, normalised label) and line. Update incrementally using graphify's per-file ast_hash in manifest.json. When the graph is stale, note 'graphify update . (no LLM)' if graphify is installed; otherwise fall back to Python ast for .py files and git grep for the rest (graphify is not installed on this machine). graphify's communities serve as 'areas' for roll-ups and for boundary-crossing alarms. Export: `fm graph overlay` writes graphify-out/foreman-overlay.json in graphify's extraction schema. Lesson, decision and escape nodes use file_type 'rationale', with rationale_for edges to the code node ids, and commit-derived links are marked EXTRACTED. graphify's merge, HTML and query then include Foreman's history. The export is opt-in and never runs in sensitive projects.
- first slice: A read-only ingest of graph.json code edges keyed to Foreman symbols, plus `fm graph callers SYMBOL`, with an ast fallback for Python. Tested with a small fixture graph.json in graphify's schema.
- bar/numbers: 2: code structure for every graph feature here at no cost: no LLM and no new parser. 4: the user's existing graphify tool starts answering history questions.
- verdict: escalated to: Consequence engine: executed answers to 'what breaks if...', computed before anyone asks. Consuming graphify edges is the consequence engine's static layer; not an idea on its own.

### Runtime world model: fm sim [R1 · Reasoning and planning; build; escalated; value 5, L]
Foreman's picture of the code today comes from filename guesses (test_guard.py goes with fmguard.py) and git co-change. Instead, Foreman would build its picture by running the code: which functions each test actually runs, which caller actually called which callee, and the shapes of the values that crossed each function boundary. The agent can ask it questions before an edit ('who runs this?', 'what really calls this?', 'what values reach it?', 'which tests can this edit change?'). After the edit, the actual outcome is compared with what the model predicted.
- mechanism: New plugin/lib/fmsim.py. The tracer is a sitecustomize.py kept in Foreman state, put on PYTHONPATH when fm runs a gate or verify command with --trace (c.run_command already takes env). It uses sys.monitoring (PEP 669; the machine runs Python 3.14.7) PY_START and CALL events, restricted to code objects under the project root. Each code object returns DISABLE after its first hit per test, which keeps overhead low. To know which test is running, the tracer patches unittest.case.TestCase.run, and pytest's runtest hook when pytest is importable, to set FM_SIM_TEST. Subprocesses inherit that variable, so code that tests reach through the fm CLI is attributed to the right test. Each process writes {test: [qualname]}, caller to callee pairs and the first 3 argument and return type shapes (types only, never values) to state/projects/<slug>/sim/<tree>/<pid>.json. `fm sim build` merges these into sim.json, keyed by file::Class.func (ast symbols, as fmmap.outline finds them) so line drift doesn't break it, and stamped with the c.worktree_tree id. Rebuilds are incremental: only tests whose covered functions changed since that tree run again. Node projects (jarvis-code's npm test) use the built-in NODE_V8_COVERAGE for function hits per process. Queries: `fm sim who-runs FILE[:FUNC]`, `fm sim callers FUNC` (observed callers, including dynamic dispatch that grep can't see), `fm sim values FUNC`, and `fm sim predict FILE:FUNC…` (the tests whose outcome can change). fmmap.tests_for and `fm check --affected` use sim.json when it exists and fall back to name links otherwise.
- first slice: Python only: the sitecustomize tracer, `fm sim build` running Foreman's own `python3 plugin/tests/run.py` once with tracing, `fm sim who-runs` and `fm sim callers`, and _check_affected switched to sim.json with the name links as fallback. It is useful on its own: tests that exercise fmcore through the fm CLI, which no filename link finds, become affected tests for fmcore edits.
- bar/numbers: 1: no coding agent builds a runtime-observed model of the repo it is editing, queries it before edits and scores its predictions afterwards. Static knowledge graphs and repo maps exist, but nothing in this product family checks predictions against execution. 2: affected-test selection by real coverage instead of filenames. It is also the substrate that ideas 2, 3, 4, 7, 9 and 12 need.
- verdict: escalated to: Consequence engine: executed answers to 'what breaks if...', computed before anyone asks. The tracer is shared substrate (behaviour recorder plus consequence engine). Alone its demo is 'who-runs' queries.

### Dead-edit alarm: edits the failing check never reaches [R1 · Reasoning and planning; build; escalated; value 5, M]
When the agent edits code that the currently failing check never executes, Foreman says so right after the edit, before the next test run. Today the agent finds this out only after another red run, or never: the 'fix' goes into a sibling copy, a vendored file, the wrong branch of a dispatch, or a function the test doesn't reach.
- mechanism: When a verify run on the active task fails (seen by _auto_evidence or post_tool_use_failure), Foreman runs that one command again in the background under the fm sim tracer, at function level, and stores failing_cover[task] = {cmd, functions hit, tree} in the project dir. In fmhooks.post_tool_use's FILE_TOOLS branch, next to _thrash_note, the edited range is mapped to its enclosing function (ast on the new file, cached by mtime) and looked up. If the failing command never ran that function, the agent gets one note: 'the red check of step 2 (test_quote) never runs fmguard._quote (traced at tree e458c5); this edit can't change its outcome. Its run ends in: <the 3 functions hit nearest the assertion>'. At the next evidence run, if the check is still red after N edits and none of them were reachable, fm next escalates: 'all 4 edits since the last run are outside the failing path'. New functions, test files and code the tracer marked unknown are never flagged.
- first slice: `fm sim trace -- CMD` (function-level hit set for one Python command; it shares the tracer with fm sim and whichever lands first builds it), triggered automatically on a red verify run, plus the PostToolUse note. Test: a fixture repo where a test fails in module a and the edit lands in a copy of the same function in module b; the note must fire. An edit to a.f must stay silent.
- bar/numbers: 1: no agent checks whether an edit is reachable from the failure it is meant to fix. 2: time to root cause; each alarm saves at least one full red-run cycle, plus the confused reasoning that follows it.
- verdict: escalated to: Shadow twin: every edit is executed in a twin worktree within seconds; only behaviour flips reach the model, and edits can be tried before they land. The twin reports 'this edit changed nothing the red check sees' as a non-flip, together with every other flip.

### Spectrum fault localization on every red run [R1 · Reasoning and planning; build; escalated; value 4, M]
After a red run, Foreman ranks suspicious functions and lines on its own. The ranking comes from which code the failing tests ran and the passing tests didn't (Ochiai spectrum scoring), plus the first point where the failing test's call sequence splits from its closest passing sibling. Spectrum-based fault localization (SBFL) is decades old in research, but no coding agent runs it inside its loop.
- mechanism: On a red fm check or verify run whose output names failing test ids (unittest, pytest, cargo test and jest formats parsed), fm sim traces the failing tests F at function and line level. It takes coverage for the passing tests P in the same modules from the latest sim.json. For each function and line s it computes Ochiai(s) = fail(s) / sqrt(|F| * (fail(s) + pass(s))). It also diffs the call sequences of the failing test and the passing test with the most overlapping coverage, and names the first callee where they part. The top 5 are injected through post_tool_use_failure's additionalContext. Each one is also written as `fm task hypo ID add '<func:line> (Ochiai 0.91; diverges from test_ok at _split)' --probe '<rerun the failing test>'`, so the existing hypothesis ledger and next_action's 'test hypothesis H1 before more fixes' take over. The fm-debugger brief gets the ranked list instead of only the raw failure. Tests flagged in flakes.json are left out of the spectra.
- first slice: `fm sim suspects` for Python suites: it traces the failing tests, ranks them against per-test coverage of their passing module-mates, prints the top 5 and writes them as hypotheses. Test: a fixture repo with a planted off-by-one in one of 6 functions, where the planted function must rank first.
- bar/numbers: 1: automatic SBFL inside a coding agent's loop. 2: time to root cause; the agent starts reading at the right function instead of at the stack trace's last frame.
- verdict: escalated to: Bug nose: names the bug before a file is read, and smells bug-shaped hunks at edit time, graded on every red. Ochiai ranking is one feature for the nose; the twin's per-test coverage makes it free.

### Prediction ledger and confusion meter [R1 · Reasoning and planning; build; escalated; value 5, M]
Every check the agent runs gets a prediction first, made automatically from the last run and the world model ('expect test_quote to turn green; nothing else changes'), and the agent can add a one-line prediction of its own. Foreman scores the real result against it. The task's running prediction-error rate measures directly whether the agent understands the code. When it climbs, Foreman makes the agent switch from editing to understanding before failures pile up, rather than after three failed attempts.
- mechanism: predictions.jsonl in the project dir. `fm expect '<what will happen>' [-- CMD]` lets the agent record its own prediction. _pre_tool_use, on a Bash command that matches a criterion's verify command or a gate, adds the automatic prediction: the tests that failed last time are expected to flip, everything else is expected to keep its last result, and when fm sim exists, the tests that `fm sim predict` names for the files edited since the last run are allowed to change. post_tool_use compares exit codes and the flipped test ids parsed from the output. A test that flips outside the predicted set means hidden coupling, so a `fm surprise` record is written automatically. The confusion score per task is an exponentially weighted moving average of prediction misses, unexpected flips, repeated failure signatures (failures.jsonl) and edit bursts with no run between them. Thresholds change next_action's executing-stage line. Mid: 'say which hypothesis this edit tests (fm task hypo add)'. High: 'stop editing; investigate (fm sim suspects, trace the failing path, fm-debugger) or reframe (re-read Raw request, fm second plan)'. Each switch is logged as mode_switch, so fm friction can measure whether switches shortened time to green. FmActive gains confusion {score, misses, last_surprise}, which the band shows as a small fit glyph.
- first slice: Automatic predictions only, so it needs no agent effort and no world model. Each rerun of a red check expects exactly the last red set to change. Newly failing tests outside that set become automatic surprises. A per-step miss counter changes fm next's executing-stage action at 3 misses. Tests cover the parsing for unittest and pytest output.
- bar/numbers: 1: agents don't predict-then-score their own checks. 2: rework and time to root cause, because switching strategy is triggered by evidence that the agent's model is wrong rather than by a count of retries. 3: prediction accuracy per file area becomes a competence signal that later allocation uses (idea 8).
- verdict: escalated to: Mind view: a cockpit of the agent's live beliefs, which the user can edit, rewind and fork. Expectations versus outcomes become surprises in the live mind view; the automatic flip-expectation half lives in the twin.

### Context-inheriting speculative forks for contested decisions [R1 · Reasoning and planning; build; escalated; value 4, L]
At a genuinely contested fork in a plan, Foreman forks the live session itself into throwaway worktrees with --resume plus --fork-session. Each branch starts knowing everything the main thread has learned, follows one option under a hard dollar and time cap, and comes back with executed evidence: did the criterion go green, which other tests broke, how big the diff is. The choice is made on that evidence, not on argument. Bench replays start cold, and sampled plans never run.
- mechanism: `fm fork ID --option 'A: …' --option 'B: …' [--usd 1 --minutes 15]`. It takes a c.worktree_tree snapshot and, for each option, creates a detached worktree at a commit-tree of it (the fmbench._worktree and task_prove_hunks pattern). It copies the live transcript into the worktree's Claude project dir (fmcost.transcripts_dir naming) so --resume finds it, then calls fmsession.start('claude', wt, prompt_i, model, ['--fork-session', '--max-budget-usd', usd, '--effort', 'high'], resume=sid), the same way fmclaude already forks live sessions. Each fork has FOREMAN_STATE pointed at scratch, so its fm writes stay in the lane. When the forks end or hit the time cap, fm runs the task's verify commands and the full gates in each worktree and compares against the snapshot's results. The scoreboard has: criterion green, collateral (tests that pass on the snapshot but fail in the fork), files and lines, $ from fmbudget. Ties go to a fmsecond tool-less judge reading the diffs. Everything is saved as research/T-NNNN-fork.md. Losing branches still pay: their collateral lists become `fm surprise` coupling facts. `fm fork adopt ID B` git-applies the winner's diff, or the main thread re-implements it. A fork is refused unless an uncertainty signal fires (confusion meter high, fm second verdict 'rethink', planner disagreement) or the user asks. fm budget caps it under a 'fork' feature.
- first slice: `fm fork` with 2 options run one after the other, on Foreman's repo. Scoreboard from verify commands plus fm check per worktree, a research note, no adopt (the main thread reads the winning diff). The first test proves that a resumed fork in a worktree really carries the context (an M-sized spike in itself).
- bar/numbers: 1: no agent forks its own live reasoning context into parallel worktree branches and selects between them on executed collateral. 2: rework from taking the wrong approach, which is the most expensive kind on M and L tasks.
- verdict: escalated to: Multiverse: risky decisions fork the session, the universes attack each other with tests, the survivor merges and losers donate. The fork mechanism stays. Multiverse adds automatic risk triggers, cross-examination by tests and donation from losers.

### Trace cards: reuse the path that solved it, not just the answer [R1 · Reasoning and planning; build; escalated; value 4, M]
When a problem like one already solved comes back, Foreman shows the investigation path that worked: the dead ends to skip, the one probe that told the causes apart, where the fix landed and which strategy cracked it. The path is extracted mechanically from the earlier task's transcript and ledger, and the probe can be run again. Today failure memory points only at a task id and its one-line lesson.
- mechanism: At fmcli.task_finish, fmtrace.card(p, b) reads the task's ledger and its transcript window: session ids come from the task's focus and evidence events, and transcripts are found under fmcost.transcripts_dir. Tool calls are parsed the way fmsecond._user_messages parses a transcript. It extracts: red runs with their fmrecall.failure_signature; dead-end edits (touched files or hunks absent from the final task diff); the decisive move (the Read, Grep or probe commands in the 5 tool calls before the first red→green transition, labelled by strategy: read, bisect, sim-suspects, debugger, print-probe); hypotheses ruled out; and the fix shape (functions changed, via ast over the diff hunks). Cards go to cards.jsonl as {sig, symbols, files, dead_ends, decisive, strategy, fix, red_runs, minutes}. Retrieval happens on a signature match in note_failure (replacing seen_before's bare pointer), in fm focus's Related section, and through `fm sim suspects` overlap. Matching is BM25 over signature and symbol tokens with recall's tokenizer. Example: 'Seen in T-0412: two edits to _split were dead ends; the decisive probe was <cmd> (re-run: fm card run 0412); fix landed in fmguard._heredocs'. The 'strategy' field gives each failure class a decisive-strategy rate, which fm next uses to suggest the strategy that has worked best here. fm friction compares red runs and minutes to green for tasks that got a card hit against those that didn't, so the store shows whether it earns its keep.
- first slice: Build cards for the ~950 existing finished tasks from ledgers and briefs only, no transcripts yet (red evidence, hypotheses, touched files missing from the final diff, the evidence command before the first green). Add `fm recall --paths SIG`, and swap note_failure's hint for the card's two-line path.
- bar/numbers: 3: every solved task makes the next similar one cheaper, with the effect measured. 2: time to root cause on recurrences and near-recurrences.
- verdict: escalated to: Bug nose: names the bug before a file is read, and smells bug-shaped hunks at edit time, graded on every red. Past investigation paths feed the nose's per-class probe templates and precedents.

### Mechanical pre-mortem: the beliefs a plan silently rests on, compiled to probes [R1 · Reasoning and planning; build; escalated; value 4, M]
Before the first edit of an M or L task, Foreman derives from the code, not from the model's imagination, the facts the plan depends on. For each function being changed: every caller, the argument shapes they pass, the exceptions callers catch, the tests that cover it, the files it changes together with. It states each fact as a falsifiable belief with a probe, runs the probes, and fails the plan on any that turn out false. This turns 'what would I need to believe for this to fail' from a rubric question into an executed list.
- mechanism: `fm premortem ID`. Symbols are the identifiers in Approach, Steps and Execution prompt that resolve to definitions (fmmap.outline over the scope files plus git grep). Fact extractors per symbol: static call sites (git grep with word boundaries), runtime callers and value shapes (fm sim callers and values, when present), except-handlers around call sites (ast), covering tests (fm sim, else fmmap.tests_for), co-change partners (map.json pairs from fmmap._co_change), and public surface (CLI flag, MASTER.md or README mention). Each fact becomes `fm task assume ID add '<fact> :: <probe>'` and is verified at once with `assume verify --run`. A tool-less child then gets only the plan and the belief list and marks which beliefs the plan needs, and how it breaks if one is false ('a 4th caller passing bytes breaks step 2'). The same list feeds fmsecond.plan, whose reviewer today 'can't see the code'. plan_gaps gains 'premortem' for M and L FIX, FEATURE and SECURITY tasks.
- first slice: Grep and ast only: static callers, co-change partners and covering tests per symbol, written as assumptions with probes and verified automatically; no child. Test: a fixture where the planned function has an unlisted caller in another module, which must appear as a belief.
- bar/numbers: 2: rework, and bugs that escape through callers nobody listed. 1: a pre-mortem grounded in code facts and executed probes, where today's are LLM-imagined lists.
- verdict: escalated to: Consequence engine: executed answers to 'what breaks if...', computed before anyone asks. Plan beliefs compiled to probes are consequence cards for the symbols the plan names, executed.

### Uncertainty-allocated thinking [R1 · Reasoning and planning; build; escalated; value 4, M]
Foreman measures how uncertain each step actually is, from novelty, blast radius, the area's track record and live confusion, and spends thinking where the uncertainty is. Effort level, model, second reads, forks and audit depth scale per step instead of per declared tier. Today allocation follows the S/M/L label, fm run maps tier to model, and nothing in lib passes --effort.
- mechanism: fmcore.uncertainty(p, b, step) returns 0..1 from signals already on disk. Novelty: the top fmrecall.recall score, or a trace-card hit. Blast radius: fan-in of the step's symbols (fm sim callers, else a grep count). Area track record: first-attempt green rate and reopen or drop rate for files in scope, from Verification evidence ✓/✗ history. Live confusion: the meter from idea 4. Optionally, disagreement between two cheap planners (idea 10). An allocation table in skills/routing.json, which fm evolve can already tune, maps bands to actions. Low: Haiku scouts, effort medium, no second read. Mid: today's defaults. High: --effort max in fmserve._claude_cmd and in fmlanes.builder_brief's Agent call, fm second plan required, fm fork offered, all five lenses. _next_for appends the allocation ('uncertainty 0.8: novel area, fan-in 23 → fm second plan first; subagents at effort max'). Calibration: fm task finish logs the predicted uncertainty against the outcome (red runs, minutes, reopen). fm friction reports whether high scores really cost more and refits the weights; the score stays advisory until it predicts.
- first slice: A score from three on-disk signals (recall novelty, grep fan-in, area green rate), shown in fm next and fm focus, passed as --effort to fm run's claude command and in fm lane brief's printed Agent call, and logged with the outcome at finish. Test: the fixture history gives the expected bands.
- bar/numbers: 2: tokens per finished task go down on easy steps and failures go down on hard ones. 3: the allocation calibrates itself against outcomes, which the seen 'Route work to the cheapest model' (by type and tier) and 'Calibrate confidence' (self-reported numbers) don't do.
- verdict: escalated to: Opus on call: the cheapest model attempts every step first, checked by machines not by Opus; the frontier model is paged only on failure. Allocating effort by an uncertainty score still keeps Opus in the loop. The cascade's acceptance posteriors allocate models by measured outcome.

### Symptom-fix detector: is the fix where the bad value is made? [R1 · Reasoning and planning; build; escalated; value 4, M]
When a FIX goes green, Foreman checks whether the fix sits where the bad value is produced or where it is merely noticed. It finds which function produced the value the patch now guards and which sibling callers receive the same value unguarded. The fix then moves upstream, or the reason it doesn't is recorded. No agent checks where a fix was placed; ponytail's 'root cause, not symptom' rule is prose with nothing enforcing it.
- mechanism: At fmcli.task_finish for FIX tasks, fmcli._hunks plus ast find guard-shaped additions in the diff (`if x is None`, try/except, `or default`, an early return, a clamp). Static dataflow within the function traces x back to the call that produced it (an assignment from a callee). The producer's other call sites (git grep, plus observed callers from fm sim) are checked for an equivalent guard. With fm sim present, observed return shapes confirm the producer really returns the bad shape (None, empty, negative). A finding reads: 'the None comes from fmguard._heredoc_body, which also feeds check_python and check_node; the fix guards only check_bash'. It comes with a probe: the repro adapted to each sibling consumer. A 'root' lens joins the M and L FIX audit set in audit.md and is recorded with `--lens 'root: …'`; a legitimate defensive guard answers it with one reason line.
- first slice: Static only, Python: guard patterns in a FIX diff, the producer found in-function, the producer's other call sites without the guard, printed at finish and required as a root lens line for FIX M and L. Test: a fixture with one producer and two consumers, where only one is patched.
- bar/numbers: 2: bugs that escape to the user because sibling callers stay broken after a symptom patch. 1: automatic fix-placement analysis.
- verdict: escalated to: Behaviour diff: review and verify what the program now does, not what the code says. Sibling consumers of a bad value show up in the behaviour diff (and in consequence cards) as executed fact, not static guesswork.

### Planners scored against reality [R1 · Reasoning and planning; skip; kill; value 4, M]
Independent cheap planners each predict, in a fixed schema, which files and functions the change will touch and which tests will flip. How much they agree is an uncertainty signal before work starts. At finish, the real diff scores every planner, model and prompt, so Foreman learns which planner to trust in which area from its own history, with no human labels. That gives fm evolve a ground-truth signal for improving how it plans.
- mechanism: `fm second predict ID` runs 3 tool-less children through fmideas.run_children (for example sonnet twice with different lenses plus haiku). Each gets the brief and a grounding pack (map, impact, outline of the scope files), with Scope withheld so they can't copy it, and returns JSON {files, functions, tests_flip, risk}. Agreement is the mean pairwise Jaccard over files and functions, and items that only 1 of 3 named are listed as 'ground these before editing'. Predictions go to predictions.jsonl. At fmcli.task_finish, the actual changed files and functions (fmcore.task_diff plus ast) and the actual flips (the prove red→green set) give each planner precision and recall per model and area. fm friction prints a leaderboard. Idea 8 weights agreement by each planner's historical recall. The main thread's own plan is scored the same way (files named in Scope, Steps and Execution prompt against the diff) as 'plan fidelity'. Its misses are cross-checked against fmmap co-change pairs to find companion files plans keep forgetting. fm bench cases can then score planning prompts, and fmevolve.generation can mutate them.
- first slice: Score only the main thread's own plans: files named in Scope, Steps and Execution prompt against the actual task diff, logged at finish, with fm friction reporting plan fidelity by type and tier and the most-missed companion files. It is useful alone and can be backfilled over the ~950 existing finished briefs today.
- bar/numbers: 3: a ground-truth score for planning quality on every task, which makes planning itself evolvable. 1: self-consistency calibrated against diffs. The seen 'Compete and sample plans' was skipped for lacking exactly this signal and for its cost; this uses 3 tool-less children.
- verdict: The demo is a plan-fidelity score table. Multiverse settles contested plans by execution instead.

### Pre-edit sensing: who notices if this function changes? [R1 · Reasoning and planning; build; escalated; value 4, M]
Before touching a function, Foreman briefly breaks it in a throwaway worktree and runs the tests that execute it, to learn which tests actually depend on its behaviour, as opposed to merely running through it. This is an executed simulation of 'what will my change disturb, and is it guarded at all?', answered before the edit. prove --hunks asks a similar question, but only after the change is made.
- mechanism: `fm sim sense FILE::FUNC [...]` reuses task_prove_hunks' machinery: a commit-tree of c.worktree_tree, a detached worktree, the mtime tick that defeats .pyc caching, and restore between mutants. For each target, ast replaces the body with `raise RuntimeError('fm-sense')`, then with a 'return the default for its type' variant. It runs the tests that execute it (fm sim who-runs, else fmmap.tests_for, else the full gate). The report reads: 'guarded by test_a, test_b (fail on both mutants); 9 tests run it without noticing; no test notices the return-default mutant'. With 0 guarding tests, next_action asks for a characterization test before the edit, which fits CLEAN's existing behaviour lock. Results attach to the brief's Assumptions as verified facts ('_split is guarded by 3 tests'), so idea 7's pre-mortem and the audit can cite them.
- first slice: Python, the raise-mutant only, targets named by hand, the tests from fmmap.tests_for or the gate, and the report printed and saved as assumptions. Test: a fixture function with one asserting test and one test that only calls it.
- bar/numbers: 1: no agent senses behavioural dependence before an edit. 2: escaped bugs in refactors and CLEAN work, since unguarded code is found before it is changed, not after.
- verdict: escalated to: Shadow twin: every edit is executed in a twin worktree within seconds; only behaviour flips reach the model, and edits can be tried before they land. Breaking a function to see who notices is what try_edit (and the consequence engine's raise operator) does on demand.

### Learned decomposition: a split advisor from the project's own step history [R1 · Reasoning and planning; skip; kill; value 3, M]
Foreman learns from its own finished briefs which step shapes converge on the first try in this repo and which thrash. It lints new plans against that, and splits a step mid-flight when it starts behaving like the shapes that thrash. Today, decomposition advice is generic reference text.
- mechanism: `fm splits` mines briefs and the ledger. For each evidenced step it takes: the verb class of its text (test-first, wire, refactor, parse, doc), how many files and symbols it names, the files touched during the step (touched events between that step's evidence timestamps), modules spanned, red runs after the first (TDD makes the first red by design), minutes, and steps added later. It fits per-bucket rates with Laplace smoothing (stdlib) and writes splits.json; fm night refreshes it. At plan time (plan_gaps, fm focus) each step gets a score, for example 'step 2 spans fmguard and fmcore; here, steps spanning 2+ modules needed extra red runs 41% of the time vs 9%: split it?'. During execution, when the current step's touched files grow past the converging shape (a third module), _next_for suggests splitting it (`fm task step add`) and committing the green part first.
- first slice: The `fm splits` report over the existing foreman and volt history (buckets with rates and counts) plus one plan-time warning for the worst bucket.
- bar/numbers: 3: decomposition improves from measured outcomes. 2: rework and thrash on overgrown steps.
- verdict: A plan lint that nudges splits. The work compiler and the org's SPLIT move restructure work directly.

### Rewind and re-decide: real failure moments become one-turn counterfactual tests [R1 · Recursive self-improvement; build; escalated; value 5, M]
Today a behavioural test is a whole paid task replay, and there are 11 of them. This forks the user's real past sessions at the exact moment Foreman went wrong (a continue-nudge, a correction, a false block, a bug that later escaped) and re-runs only the next turn with a candidate plugin. Write actions are recorded and never executed, and each moment is sampled N times. The result is thousands of cheap, exact tests in real context instead of 11.
- mechanism: New fmmoments.py. (1) Mine: from the transcripts and the flight-recorder labels, take the uuid of the last record before each labelled wrong action or stop. (2) Materialise: copy the transcript prefix up to that uuid into a scratch CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR/projects folder under a fresh session id (rewriting the sessionId fields), and add a git worktree of the project at the commit that was HEAD then (git rev-list -1 --before=<ts>).
