(3) Run: claude -p --resume <id> --fork-session --plugin-dir <candidate> --setting-sources project,local with FOREMAN_DRYRUN=<file>. When that is set, fmhooks._pre_tool_use lets read-only tools (Read/Grep/Glob) run, and for Bash/Edit/Write/Agent it records tool_name and tool_input, then denies with 'dry run: recorded'. The run stops at the first recorded mutating intent or at the turn end (--max-budget-usd small). Sample N=3. The shared prefix hits the prompt cache, so samples 2..N cost about 10% of the input. (4) Grade by moment type. Wrong stop: passes when the candidate (given its own Stop hook's reason if that hook blocks) emits a work action instead of ending. Correction: a tool-less judge (fmsecond._child) checks the recorded intent against the user's corrected words. False block: the intent no longer trips the guard and stays in the same category class. Escape: re-run the read-only fm-reviewer on the diff that shipped and check that it names the defect found later. Results go to bench/results/moments-<label>.json, so fm bench gate/compare work unchanged.
- first slice: fm bench moments --kind stop builds cases from the continue-nudges in the last 60 days that did not follow API errors (about 45), runs HEAD against a candidate plugin with dry-run capture at N=3, and reports the keep-working rate per arm. This is useful alone: the next drive or rules change gets measured on the user's own idle moments.
- bar/numbers: 1: no coding agent forks its own historical sessions at failure points to test a modified version of itself. 2: about 100x more behavioural evals for cents each. 3: the evolve loop finally gets enough signal to select on.
- verdict: escalated to: Night school: every past mistake becomes a permanent test, and Foreman rewrites its own skills, hooks and agents overnight against them. Its one-turn counterfactual moments are exactly Night school's never-again suite.

### Burden meter: Foreman's own objective, measured on real sessions and attributed to each of its changes [R1 · Recursive self-improvement; build; escalated; value 5, M]
Foreman judges itself on 11 paid replays and on friction counts per window. Nothing measures what the user actually had to do (type continue, correct, re-report, wait) or which of Foreman's own commits changed that. This computes a user-burden score per day and per session from every transcript and attributes shifts in it to individual Foreman commits. It becomes the fitness function that every other self-improvement loop is optimised for and held to.
- mechanism: New fmburden.py, stdlib only. It reads ~/.claude/projects/*/*.jsonl the way fmcost.scan and fmreplay._logs do. It keeps only real user turns: entrypoint=='cli', not isMeta, isCompactSummary, a tool_result or a <task-notification>. Each user turn is classified by transparent rules into continue-nudge, correction (contrast markers, not just a prefix), re-report (names a done task, or 'still/again' plus its words, reusing the escape logic), answer, or new request. Each nudge gets a cause from the preceding records: isApiErrorMessage means api_error; 'waiting/gate is running' means idled_on_background; needs_user(msg) means asked_in_text; 'No response requested.' means interrupted; otherwise mid_work_stop. Idle minutes are the gap between the last assistant record and the nudge. Burden is reported per 100 assistant turns and per finished task, and tokens per finished task come from fmcost. Attribution: for each Foreman commit (git log with Foreman-Task trailers), the burden components its files plausibly drive (fmhooks drive code drives nudges, fmguard drives workarounds) are compared over N sessions before and after, with a bootstrap CI over sessions, stratified by project. Commands: fm burden [--days 60] [--by day|cause|commit] [--json]; a 'burden' ledger event; and a burden sparkline in fm ui --json for the pane.
- first slice: fm burden over the last 60 days: nudges and corrections per 100 turns by day, the cause table with idle minutes for every continue-nudge, and before/after for each commit touching plugin/lib/fmhooks.py, plus a unit test on a synthetic transcript fixture. This is useful alone: it names today's top idle cause, sessions dead for hours after network errors with nothing resuming them.
- bar/numbers: 1: no coding agent measures its user's burden from its own transcripts and attributes it to its own code changes. 3: every self-improvement engine, experiment and warranty is judged on it, so 'improved' becomes a measured claim.
- verdict: escalated to: Night school: every past mistake becomes a permanent test, and Foreman rewrites its own skills, hooks and agents overnight against them. The fitness function and warranty metric inside Night school; alone it's a table.

### Darwin archive: open-ended evolution of Foreman behind a multi-fidelity proof cascade [R1 · Recursive self-improvement; build; escalated; value 5, L]
fm evolve runs one generation on one instruction file and deletes every candidate it doesn't keep. This keeps an archive of every Foreman variant (instruction text, a typed parameter genome, and hook code run only in a sandbox) with lineage and per-case scores. It picks parents by performance and novelty, Darwin-Goedel-machine style, so stepping stones survive. Every child goes through a cascade that kills most candidates for free before any paid replay.
- mechanism: Archive: git refs refs/foreman/archive/<id> plus state/archive.jsonl rows {id, parent, operator, target, why, per-tier scores, novelty, status}. Operators: (a) text revise (today's mutate); (b) ablate one paragraph, which finds lines that don't earn their tokens; (c) genome: the module-level numeric constants and regexes (DRIVE_MAX, OFFERS_MAX, LONG_JOB_S, THRASH, BIG_READ, _CORRECTION, nudge words) lifted into plugin/genome.json with ranges and a safety class, where guard, evidence, consent and budget entries are frozen; (d) code: a tool-less child patches one non-guard hook function, and that code is only ever executed inside bwrap. Parent selection: p proportional to sigmoid(score - median) / (1 + children). Cascade: T0 static (check_revision, py_compile, rules <= 80 lines, the plugin tests and test_guard_diff), T1 flight-recorder replay (free), T2 rewind moments (cents), T3 bench on a sealed holdout the mutation child never sees (paid), T4 live experiment. Successive halving means only the top k of each tier advance. A child is proposed only when it beats both its parent and HEAD on the holdout, and merging stays the user's call.
- first slice: fm evolve --archive persists every generation, kept or not, as an archive row and ref with its gate lines. It adds parent selection over the archived variants of one target and a genome.json for the five fmhooks drive constants with T0 checks, plus fm evolve archive list|show. This is useful alone: no candidate is lost, and the next generation builds on the best variant rather than always on HEAD.
- bar/numbers: 3: a population that keeps stepping stones finds improvements that one-step hill-climbing from HEAD cannot, and each tier is proof. 1: no coding agent evolves its own harness as a safety-gated archive. The earlier 'breeding' skip cited volume and compute; the cascade answers that by spending model money only on survivors of the free tiers.
- verdict: escalated to: Night school: every past mistake becomes a permanent test, and Foreman rewrites its own skills, hooks and agents overnight against them. Becomes Night school's breeding stage.

### Flight recorder: every real hook decision becomes a free, labelled regression case [R1 · Recursive self-improvement; build; escalated; value 5, M]
fm replay re-judges only Bash commands through the guard and only diffs the verdicts. This records every hook decision (Stop/drive, intake and corrections, notes, guard) with its exact inputs and a compact state snapshot, and labels each one from what happened next. Any candidate fmhooks or fmguard can then be replayed over tens of thousands of real decisions in a sandbox without network, and the result is scored, not just diffed, at zero model cost.
- mechanism: (a) Recorder: after each decision, the dispatcher appends to state/flight/<day>.jsonl. Each line holds the event; the payload without large fields (prompt hashed plus a 300-char redacted excerpt, last_assistant_message capped at 500 chars); the snapshot the deciders need (trimmed c.state_dict: active/queue/inbox ids, statuses, step, autonomy, drive, paused, pending; the gate.json drive[sid] entry; background_tasks; now); the decision (block or allow, reason id, category); and the plugin HEAD. That is about 1 KB per decision, written after the output so latency stays inside the p95 budget. (b) Labeller: a Stop that ended a turn and was followed by a continue-nudge is a wrong stop. A guard block followed within 5 calls by a same-tool call that ran is a workaround (a candidate false positive). A prompt followed by 'not what I meant' is a missed correction. (c) Replayer: fm replay --hooks [--plugin DIR] imports the candidate modules in a bwrap child (--unshare-net, read-only binds, tmpfs state) and feeds the recorded (payload, snapshot) pairs through pure deciders. One small refactor is needed: decide_stop(snapshot, pl, now) under stop(), with _running, time and _turn_began injected. The report lists changed decisions, labelled wins (wrong stops now continued, workarounds now allowed) and losses (correct decisions flipped, and any newly allowed dangerous category, which is an automatic fail).
- first slice: Record Stop decisions only (snapshot plus decision) in fmhooks.stop, label them from the next user prompt, and add fm replay --stop, which re-runs _drive from the snapshots with the working tree's fmhooks against HEAD's and prints labelled wins and losses. Prove zero diffs for HEAD against itself. This is useful alone: every drive change gets a real-traffic check before merge, the way fm replay already checks the guard.
- bar/numbers: 1: no coding agent replays its own control-plane decisions from real traffic as labelled tests. 3: it makes hook code safely evolvable, which matters because that is where most of Foreman's behaviour lives and fmevolve refuses code today.
- verdict: escalated to: Night school: every past mistake becomes a permanent test, and Foreman rewrites its own skills, hooks and agents overnight against them. The free deterministic tier of the never-again suite.

### Live self-experiments: Foreman runs randomized trials of its own behaviour on real work [R1 · Recursive self-improvement; build; escalated; value 4, M]
Every Foreman change is decided today by offline replays or by judgment. This lets Foreman run real randomized experiments in the user's sessions. Two safe variants (of a drive reason, an injected note, a skill wording or a genome constant) are assigned per session and scored on the burden meter, and the winner is promoted only when the posterior says so. It is A/B testing of a coding agent on its own production behaviour, with the user's real work as ground truth.
- mechanism: state/experiments.json holds {name, arms {A, B}, target (a genome key, a note template id, or a skill-path variant), unit (session or stop chain), metric (continue-nudge after a turn end, corrections per 100 turns, tokens per finished task), status}. Assignment: arm = hash(session_id + name) mod arms, written to the flight recorder; hooks read it through one helper that falls back to the default. Analysis: Beta-Bernoulli posteriors per arm, with Thompson sampling shifting assignment toward the leader so the user pays little regret. It stops at P(best) >= 0.95 or at max N, and the winner becomes a proposed change with its posterior attached to the certificate. Safety classes: the guard, done and evidence gates, approvals, consent and budgets are never randomized, and an arm whose burden passes a floor is stopped automatically. The commands are fm experiment new|show|stop, plus an 'experiments' section in fm friction.
- first slice: One experiment type: two wordings of the background-wait message in fmhooks._drive, assigned per session. The metric is turn ends followed by a continue-nudge in the same session, shown as Beta posteriors in fm experiment show. This is useful alone: it settles with data which drive phrasing actually keeps the agent working while a gate runs.
- bar/numbers: 1: no coding agent runs randomized controlled trials of its own behaviour on its user's real sessions. 3: each trial gives causal proof that a change helped, rather than a correlation.
- verdict: escalated to: Night school: every past mistake becomes a permanent test, and Foreman rewrites its own skills, hooks and agents overnight against them. A/B on live sessions is Night school's warranty tier.

### Corrections become backtested detectors that fire before the user has to say it [R1 · Recursive self-improvement; build; escalated; value 4, M]
Today a correction is caught only if it starts with no/don't/never, and it becomes a keyword note. This turns each general correction into a precise detector over the event stream. The detector is backtested on every past session: it must fire at or before the corrected moment and stay quiet elsewhere. It then runs live, so Foreman stops itself before the user would have had to step in, and the lead time is measured.
- mechanism: (1) Capture: a two-stage classifier replaces the prefix regex. First, rules on contrast markers ('instead', 'I meant', 'you didn't', 'still', 'again', 'I don't like', 'why did you'); then a tool-less Haiku child on ambiguous turns, tagging each as correction, standing preference or one-off task detail. (2) Compile: for standing ones, a tool-less child writes a detector in a small declarative DSL, with no code: {on: PreToolUse|Stop|PostToolUse, tool, match: a regex on command/path/text, within: the last N events with predicates such as 'turn ending', 'background job running', 'queue non-empty', unless, say}. (3) Backtest: run the DSL over the flight recorder and the transcript corpus and measure recall (it fires within K events before each labelled instance, both the correction itself and similar turns the classifier finds), false alarms per 1,000 events elsewhere, and lead time. A detector is kept only above thresholds. (4) Deploy: PreToolUse/Stop evaluate active detectors precompiled (2 ms or less) and add a note quoting the user's words. fm friction counts hits against repeats, and promotion to a block needs the user's yes. Auto-memory feedback files in every project are harvested as corrections too.
- first slice: The rule classifier over transcripts, a DSL with three predicates (tool, regex, window) and fm detectors backtest, seeded from vetoes.json entries and the volt feedback memories. It prints precision, recall and lead time per detector. This is useful alone: it shows which of today's vetoes could ever fire, and which corrections were never captured.
- bar/numbers: 2: user corrections per task are the measured target, and they fall when no correction ever has to be repeated. 3: each correction permanently upgrades the live system, with backtest proof.
- verdict: escalated to: Lessons compile to code: memory stops being prose the model must read and becomes checks that run. Detectors are one compile target among diff checks, rewrites and tests, all from the same backtested compiler.

### Proof-carrying self-changes with warranties checked on real sessions and revert proposals [R1 · Recursive self-improvement; build; escalated; value 4, M]
A Foreman change today merges on a chat table and is never checked again. Here every self-change carries a machine-checkable certificate that is re-verified at merge: replay results, moments, holdout, fuzzer, guard replay, and a predicted burden effect. It also carries a warranty: after N real sessions the burden meter tests the prediction, and a change that broke its warranty gets a revert-or-repair proposal with the numbers.
- mechanism: state/certs/<branch>.json stores {base, head, files, tier results T0-T3 with input hashes, guard: fm replay newly_allowed = 0 and the newly_blocked list, test_guard_diff and fuzz pass, holdout delta with CI, predicted metric and direction (for example 'continue-nudges per 100 turn ends 9 -> <= 6'), cost}. At merge, /foreman:improve re-runs the deterministic tiers (T0, T1, guard replay) and refuses on any mismatch, then logs a 'warranty' event. fm warranty runs from fm night and once a week at SessionStart. It compares the post-merge window with the prediction using the burden attribution method. On a breach it captures a confirm-first FIX self item ('revert or repair <change>') carrying the numbers and pins it in fm next. Predicted against realized effects per engine gives the improver its own calibration score.
- first slice: Certificates for kept fm evolve branches (gate lines, fm replay result, plugin test pass, and a predicted metric the proposer must state), plus fm warranty, which checks the prediction 7 days after merge against fm friction's per-100-calls trend and captures a self item on a breach. This is useful alone: merges come with evidence the user can read, and regressions on real sessions resurface without anyone looking.
- bar/numbers: 3: it closes the loop, because every self-improvement is proven before and after merge and the ones that fail on real sessions come back on their own. 1: no agent attaches falsifiable, auto-checked effect predictions to its own changes.
- verdict: escalated to: Night school: every past mistake becomes a permanent test, and Foreman rewrites its own skills, hooks and agents overnight against them. Certificates and warranties are Night school's output format.

### Guard repair synthesizer: false blocks fixed overnight with a no-new-bypass proof [R1 · Recursive self-improvement; build; escalated; value 4, M]
The guard is patched by hand, one shell form at a time: 92 of Foreman's 397 commits touch fmguard.py. Here each false-block cluster becomes a repair job. A sandboxed child proposes the narrowest patch plus test forms. Foreman then proves the patch allows the incident and nothing dangerous it blocked before, using 5,000 real commands, the fuzzer, a mutation pass and an adversarial bypass hunt. The patch is queued with that proof for the user's core yes.
- mechanism: (1) Incidents: guard_block events followed by a same-tool call that ran, or by a later grant, or mentioned in an auto-memory, clustered by category and normalized shape. 'brief' category blocks are procedure and are excluded. (2) Repair: a tool-less child (fmideas.run_child) receives the block detail, the matched rule's code, the incident commands and FORMS, and returns a unified diff plus new FORMS twins (one allowed, one still blocked). The patch is applied in an evolve-style worktree. (3) Proof obligations, each run in bwrap without network: the unit tests; test_guard_diff with every dangerous form still blocked; a fuzz run with a fixed seed; fm replay over 14 days with the candidate, where newly_allowed must be a subset of the incident cluster and newly_blocked must be empty; a red-team child that is shown the patch and must produce commands the patched rule now admits, each judged by g.check (never run); and a mutation pass that flips the patch's conditions and expects some test to fail. (4) The output is a branch plus certificate, and the user's core yes merges it. The guard never weakens without this proof.
- first slice: fm guard repair --incident <event ts> builds the incident cluster, asks the child for a patch, runs the unit tests, test_guard_diff and the fm replay delta in a worktree, and prints the obligation table without merging. This is useful alone: a false block becomes a reviewed, proven patch in one command.
- bar/numbers: 2: false-block friction and the manual guard-patching work, the single biggest source of churn in the repo. 3: the safety layer improves itself, and the safety checks are mechanical proof obligations, not judgment.
- verdict: escalated to: Night school: every past mistake becomes a permanent test, and Foreman rewrites its own skills, hooks and agents overnight against them. The first and highest-yield Night school target (8 guard tasks this week), with the same no-new-bypass proof.

### Improving the improver: a yield-ranked portfolio of improvement engines that evolves its own prompts [R1 · Recursive self-improvement; build; escalated; value 4, M]
Foreman has many self-improvement engines (friction pass, evolve, stranger, court, soak, second session) and runs them in a fixed order, with no measure of which ones produce changes that help. This measures each engine's yield, meaning merged changes per dollar whose warranty held, and allocates the night budget by Thompson sampling. It also evolves the engines' own prompts (the evolve SYSTEM text, the friction BRIEF, the court rubric), scoring each prompt variant by how its descendants perform.
- mechanism: Every self item and evolve branch is tagged with an origin {engine, prompt variant id} (fm capture --source self --origin friction:v3). A yield ledger joins origin, status (done or dropped), certificate pass, warranty held and burden delta, giving a Beta(successes, failures) per engine and its dollars from spend.jsonl. fm night's jobs() becomes a bandit: Thompson draws of yield per dollar inside --max-usd. Meta-evolution: the engine prompts move into files (plugin/skills/improve/engines/*.md). A meta-generation mutates one of them, Promptbreeder-style mutation of mutation prompts, and the variant serves that engine's next K items. Its yield against the parent decides whether it survives. Dead engines are flagged with a redesign brief: one whose cases never discriminate, or one with no merged yield in 30 days.
- first slice: Origin tags on self items, an fm improve yield table (engine, items, done, dropped, dollars spent, friction lines that disappeared since), and fm night ordering its jobs by yield per dollar instead of the fixed list. This is useful alone: it shows where self-improvement money is wasted.
- bar/numbers: 3 at the meta level: a better improver compounds into every later improvement. 1: self-referential improvement of the improvement procedure in a production coding agent.
- verdict: escalated to: Night school: every past mistake becomes a permanent test, and Foreman rewrites its own skills, hooks and agents overnight against them. Engine yield bandits schedule Night school's operators.

### Overnight flywheel: wake up to proven Foreman improvements and merge each with one click [R1 · Recursive self-improvement; build; escalated; value 4, M]
fm night runs a fixed list of upkeep jobs and never creates a candidate. This turns the night into the whole loop: mine yesterday's sessions into labels and moments, breed archive children aimed at the top burden cause, push them through the free and cheap tiers, and race the survivors on the holdout. In the morning the Foreman pane shows a card per proposal with its certificate, predicted effect and cost, and a single click to merge or discard.
- mechanism: fmnight gains four jobs: 'mine' (fm burden, labels and moments; free), 'evolve' (k archive children picked by the portfolio bandit and aimed at the file or genome key behind the top burden cause), 'cascade' (T1/T2 on all, T3 on the top 2) and 'certify'. Each keeps the heartbeat, budget, usage stop and breaker the night already has. fm ui --json gains 'proposals' [{branch, target, why, cert summary, predicted effect, cost}], rendered as a card next to the bench card in mods/foreman-ui. Its Merge button submits the user's own prompt '/foreman:improve merge <branch>': the user's click is the yes, and the skill re-verifies the certificate before merging. Discard removes the branch and the archive keeps the row. Scheduling stays the user's: fm night --install-timer offers a systemd user timer and asks once.
- first slice: A night job 'evolve' (one fm evolve generation per night on the instruction file named most by friction and burden lines, archived) plus 'proposals' in fm ui --json and a pane card listing kept branches with their gate lines and merge/discard buttons. This is useful alone: kept candidates no longer sit unseen on branches.
- bar/numbers: 4: the user gains a tool that improves itself while they sleep and only needs a click. 3: the loop runs without anyone steering it.
- verdict: escalated to: Night school: every past mistake becomes a permanent test, and Foreman rewrites its own skills, hooks and agents overnight against them. Same loop; Night school adds the ever-growing never-again suite as the proof that nothing old regresses.

### Co-evolving benchmark: forged micro-tasks that live only while they tell variants apart [R1 · Recursive self-improvement; build; escalated; value 3, M]
The bench can only grow from finished single-commit tasks (11 cases), and nothing replaces cases that every variant passes or fails. Here a forge writes small, self-contained toy-repo tasks aimed at Foreman's current weak spots: idling while a gate runs, a command the guard trips on, a steer to honour mid-task. It validates them and keeps only those that separate archive variants. The benchmark evolves alongside the agent, POET-style, so evolution never runs out of signal.
- mechanism: fm bench forge --target <burden cause|friction line> [--n 3]. A tool-less child writes {files, hidden test, prompt, scripted mid-task user turns for soak, expected behaviour} for a toy repo built like fmbench.TOY and stranger(). Foreman validates it fail-to-pass in a temp dir and checks it costs under $0.25 on Sonnet. Accepted cases enter cases.json with origin 'forge' and a discrimination score: the variance of pass rate across the last k archive variants. Cases with zero discrimination for k rounds retire, and recurring failure modes spawn new forge targets. The forge prompt is itself an engine in the portfolio, scored by discrimination.
- first slice: fm bench forge for one class, 'idles while a background gate runs': 5 validated toy cases, each with a slow fake gate (a test that sleeps 60 s) and a second queued task, raced on HEAD against HEAD~20 with fm bench versions to check they discriminate. This is useful alone: a cheap regression test for the user's most common complaint.
- bar/numbers: 3: keeps the improvement loop supplied with discriminating tests indefinitely. 1: an agent-written curriculum about its own failure modes, kept adversarial on purpose.
- verdict: escalated to: Night school: every past mistake becomes a permanent test, and Foreman rewrites its own skills, hooks and agents overnight against them. Forged micro-tasks supplement mined moments when a weak spot has too few real ones.

### Treadmill detector: know when to stop patching and redesign [R1 · Recursive self-improvement; build; escalated; value 3, M]
Self-improvement loops hill-climb: each piece of friction gets a local patch. This watches Foreman's own change history for regions where patches keep arriving at a steady or rising rate on the same functions. The guard's shell-form whack-a-mole is the live example. Past a threshold it stops filing patch number 93 and files a redesign RESEARCH brief with the data: the improver noticing that its own strategy has stopped converging.
- mechanism: fm treadmill reads git log --numstat with Foreman-Task trailers and uses fm why's line-to-commit mapping per function. It computes the patch arrival rate per region over sliding windows, fix-on-fix chains (a FIX touching lines a FIX added less than 7 days earlier), escapes per module (ledger 'escape' events) and fuzzer and red-team yield (new bypass forms per week). A Poisson trend test flags regions whose rate is not decaying. The output lists the region, patch count, trend, recurring incident classes and a redesign prompt (for example: parse shell text, or run commands under bwrap with protected paths read-only and keep the parser as defence in depth). It feeds the portfolio as an engine that proposes architecture work instead of patches, and fm friction gets a 'treadmills' section.
- first slice: fm treadmill over Foreman's repo: patch rate per file and per function by week, fix-on-fix chains and the trend test, plus a RESEARCH self item when a region crosses the threshold. This is useful alone: an honest, data-backed answer to 'is the guard converging?'.
- bar/numbers: 3 at the meta level: it switches the improver from patching to restructuring when the evidence says patching doesn't converge. 2: it removes a whole class of recurring patches at once.
- verdict: escalated to: Night school: every past mistake becomes a permanent test, and Foreman rewrites its own skills, hooks and agents overnight against them. Alone it's a report. Inside Night school it switches the operator from patch to redesign when a region doesn't converge.

### Steerable live plan tree [R1 · Foreman UI; build; escalated; value 4, M]
The pane shows the task's steps, criteria and hypotheses as a tree you drive with the keyboard while Claude works. You can make a step current, skip it, insert one, reorder steps, or pin a steer to a single step. The change is written to the brief and reaches the model at its next tool call, not after the turn. Today the pane's steps are read-only text, and a steer is free prose typed into the composer.
- mechanism: A new surface module, hooks/plan.tsx, is drawn inside the Pane's task card as a Client (key 'fm-plan'; props = steps, criteria, hypotheses, current step, live flag). Its local state holds the cursor. surface.onKey maps the keys: j/k or arrows move; Enter makes the step current; s skips; a adds a step after (opens an Input); J/K reorder; ! steers this step; h adds a hypothesis here. Each action calls surface.post({op, n, text}). A new on('ui.message') hook in register.tsx receives it, runs fm (`fm task step ID current N` exists; new: `fm task step ID skip N`, `fm task step ID move N --to M`, `fm steer add ID --step N TEXT`), refreshes, and answers {props} so the tree updates without a full redraw. Mid-turn delivery: fm steer appends to a pending list in meta.json. fmhooks' PostToolUse handler (the same additionalContext return path _thrash_note uses) injects pending steers once, e.g. 'the user re-planned: step 3 skipped, step 5 now current; on step 4: ...', and logs steer_delivered. UserPromptSubmit picks up anything left over. Hover link: at tool.call the mod records toolUseId → current step. The mod-drawn ToolUse rows and the pane's step row share a BoxHoverProps scope ('step-T-0589-3'), so pointing at a step lights that step's tool rows in the transcript.
- first slice: M task: plan.tsx Client with cursor movement, Enter (make current) and ! (steer this step) only; new `fm steer add/list` plus PostToolUse injection of pending steers, delivered exactly once; the ui.message hook. Tests: a $.ui.mount terminal test that drives ui.key and checks the posted op and the fm call, and a fmhooks unit test that a pending steer is injected once and then marked delivered.
- bar/numbers: 4: the user can restructure an autonomous agent's plan mid-run with single keys, and the change takes effect at the next tool boundary. Claude Code's todo list, Cursor's plan and Devin's planner are read-only during execution or editable only before it. 2: steers are anchored to a step and stored in the brief, so they survive compaction and don't need repeating, which cuts user corrections per task.
- verdict: escalated to: Mind view: a cockpit of the agent's live beliefs, which the user can edit, rewind and fork. Steering steps is a subset of editing the agent's mind; plans are one belief type.

### Probe swarm on the hypothesis board [R1 · Foreman UI; build; escalated; value 4, M]
On a FIX task the debugging ledger becomes a board. The mod runs the recorded probe of every open hypothesis in parallel in the background, through the guard, while Claude keeps reasoning. Results land on the cards (exit code, output tail, time) and reach the model at its next tool call. Today hypotheses are '? H1 ...' text lines, and probes run one at a time inside the model's turn.
- mechanism: _ledger_lines() in fmwatch.py emits each probe as its own field by parsing the '— probe: `...`' suffix that add_hypothesis writes. FmHypothesis gains probe and result. A Board card in the Pane shows one row per hypothesis: status glyph, probe, a Raster activity meter while it runs, last result. Pressing p runs every open probe; in full autonomy it runs automatically when 2 or more probes are open during the execute stage. Each probe runs as $.tool.call({tool:'Bash', command:`fm task hypo ID mark N open --run '<probe>'`, run_in_background:true}). Because it is a tool call, PreToolUse (the guard) judges it exactly as it judges a model call. `mark --run` already records the exit code and output in the brief (fmcore mark_hypothesis). The brief changing moves the watched paths, so the next poll refreshes the cards. A pending steer ('probe results: H2 exit 1 ..., H3 exit 0 ...') goes through the steer queue so the model reads results without a new turn. Keys c and x confirm or rule out a hypothesis from the board (`fm task hypo ID mark N confirmed|ruled-out`).
- first slice: M task: the probe field in `fm ui --json`; the Board card with run-all (p), which starts one background $.tool.call per open probe; confirm and rule-out keys; a test that a probe the guard blocks (fixture command) is refused and the card says so.
- bar/numbers: 2: time to root cause. N probes run at once, in wall-clock time the model spends reasoning, instead of N serial tool calls inside the turn. 1: no coding agent runs the probes for its competing hypotheses in parallel outside its reasoning loop and feeds the results back in.
- verdict: escalated to: Bug nose: names the bug before a file is read, and smells bug-shaped hunks at edit time, graded on every red. Probes run themselves in the twin from the nose's proposals, with no board to fill in.

### Effort governor: reasoning effort set per plan step [R1 · Foreman UI; build; escalated; value 4, M]
The mod sets the model's reasoning effort for each request from Foreman's plan state. Effort goes to max on a step whose last verification failed or a FIX in diagnosis, and to low on bookkeeping turns (fm calls, recording evidence, close-out). A band key forces 'think hard on this step'. Today effort is a single session setting.
- mechanism: An on('turn.step') streaming hook rewrites e.effort before calling next(e). A policy table keyed by (stage, type, tier, failed runs on the current step, whether the last N tool calls were all fmCommand() bookkeeping) picks the effort. Subagent steps (e.agentId set) are left alone unless a per-agent-type row says otherwise. Hysteresis: effort changes only at a step boundary or after a failed evidence run, to keep the message cache warm. The band shows a dim word such as 'effort max · step 3 failed twice'. Hotkeys: t = turbo this step, e = economy this step; the override lives in an atom and clears at the next step. Each step's effort, outcome and tokens (TurnStepResult.usage) go to a new effort_step ledger event, and a nightly fit adjusts the table, so the policy improves with use.
- first slice: M task: the turn.step hook with a fixed three-rule policy (failed run on the current step → max; last 3 tool calls are fm bookkeeping → low; otherwise unchanged), the t/e overrides and the band word; view adds step_fails to the active step; mod tests mock turn.step and assert the rewritten effort; per-step effort and usage are logged for later fitting.
- bar/numbers: 1: no coding agent routes reasoning depth per plan step from verification state. 2: tokens per finished task fall on Foreman's bookkeeping-heavy turns, and hard steps get more thinking exactly when a check has just failed.
- verdict: escalated to: Opus on call: the cheapest model attempts every step first, checked by machines not by Opus; the frontier model is paged only on failure. Per-step effort is a knob; the cascade changes which model runs at all.

### Cache-bust radar [R1 · Foreman UI; build; escalated; value 4, M]
A live meter of prompt-cache health that names the cause of every big cache write: a Foreman injection that changed, CLAUDE.md edited, a model or effort switch, a compaction, or the 5-minute cache lifetime running out while the drive waited on a background job. Foreman counts cache writes after the fact (fm cost), but nothing says why they happened.
- mechanism: A turn.step hook reads TurnStepResult.usage for each request (input, cache_creation_input_tokens, cache_read_input_tokens, model), with turnId, index and agentId. A bust is cache creation above about 20% of the prompt after a conversation's first step. Causes are attributed from what the mod can already see: hashes of each prompt.compose section by id (register.tsx already hooks prompt.compose), a hash of the claudeMd text in prompt.context, changes in e.model or e.effort, session.compact events, the gap since the previous step (over 5 minutes means the cache expired), and tool-list changes (tool.register, mcp). A 'Cache' card in the Pane shows a Raster sparkline of hit ratio per step and the last 3 busts with cause and tokens written. Each bust is also logged as a cache_bust ledger event {cause, tokens}, which fm friction reads to file self-improvement items about Foreman's own cache-busting injections.
- first slice: M task: capture usage from turn.step; detect busts; attribute three causes (model/effort change, gap over 5 minutes, compose-section hash change); add the Cache card; log cache_bust events through a new `fm cost note`; a mod test with mocked step results.
- bar/numbers: 2: tokens per finished task. Cache writes are weighted 1.25× and reads 0.1× (fmcost WEIGHTS), so a bust from a changing injection or an idle gap is a direct, recurring cost the user cannot see. 3: each attributed cause becomes a Foreman fix that makes every later session cheaper, and the meter shows whether the fix worked.
- verdict: escalated to: Context PGO: Foreman profiles where every token goes and recompiles its own prompts, injections and output digests to the smallest form that still passes. A meter. PGO attributes busts and fixes them at the source, with proof.

### Fog-of-war code map [R1 · Foreman UI; skip; kill; value 4, M]
A mini-map of the repo in the pane. Files light up as Claude reads and edits them. Files that history says usually change together with an edited file, or that test it, stay red until Claude opens them. The 'forgot to update the sibling file' bug becomes visible before done, and one key names the file to Claude.
- mechanism: A new `fm map --layout --json` computes a squarified treemap of `git ls-files` grouped by directory (from map.json's layout) and sized by line count. Each file gets a rectangle in a W×H cell grid, cached in map.json and stable across runs. The mod keeps per-session sets: read (Read/Grep targets) and edited (tool.call edits, noteEdit). It computes 'owed' = co-change partners of edited files plus their linked tests (map.json pairs from fmmap _co_change, and tests_for), minus files already read or edited. Terminal: one Raster (about 60×10) coloured fog / read / edited / owed / hot, with the current file blinking, repainted by $.ui.blit on each tool.call. Desktop and mobile: Svg rectangles. Under the map, a list such as 'owed: plugin/tests/test_guard.py, changes with fmguard.py'. Keys: o sends Claude a note through the steer queue; @ puts '@path ' in the composer with $.prompt.fill. At `fm task finish`, owed files nobody read become a done-time warning.
- first slice: M task: compute owed files in fm (new `fm map owed --files ...`, called by the mod), a list card, a static Raster treemap without click, and a finish-time warning for owed files never read; tests on a fixture map.json.
- bar/numbers: 2: fewer escaped bugs from co-changing files that were never updated and linked tests that never ran. 4: the user sees at a glance where an autonomous agent has and has not looked in the codebase; no coding agent shows its own coverage of the repo.
- verdict: A visual nudge about owed files. Behaviour diff and twin flips catch the forgotten-sibling bug by execution.

### Verified-moment timeline: scrub, diff, fork, bisect [R1 · Foreman UI; build; escalated; value 4, L]
Every check run and evidence record already saves a git tree object of the whole working tree. A timeline in the pane lets you scrub between those green and red moments, see the real diff between any two, fork a parallel lane from a past green moment without losing current work, and bisect a regression across uncommitted snapshots. Checkpoint restore in Claude Code or Cursor rewinds one conversation; this works on snapshots tagged by verification results and keeps both lines of work.
- mechanism: A new `fm task moments ID --json` lists, by time: evidence lines with their [tree x] mark and pass/fail, check_run events with tree and results, the focus base_tree, and commits. A Client module, hooks/timeline.tsx, draws one row of coloured glyphs (● green, ✗ red, ◆ commit, ▸ focus) with a cursor moved by ←/→. Enter posts {op:'diff', a, b}; the ui.message hook runs `git diff --stat a b` and one file's `git diff a b -- f` via $.process.run, and returns them as props shown in a Code element with format 'diff'. f posts a fork: new `fm lane fork ID --tree <sha>` (git worktree add --detach, then git read-tree -u --reset <tree>, plus a lane brief 'continue from moment N with approach B'). b on a red moment runs a new `fm task bisect ID --good <tree> --bad <tree> --run '<failing criterion>'`, reusing the hunk-revert worktree machinery of prove --hunks to find the hunk that turned it red. Moments are protected from git gc by refs/foreman/moments/<task>/<n> (commit-tree wrappers).
- first slice: M task: `fm task moments --json`, the timeline Client with scrub and diff only (no fork or bisect yet), the pinning refs, a test of moment ordering and a mount test that presses ← and Enter.
- bar/numbers: 4: the user can branch an autonomous run from any verified state and compare the two lines of work, which no coding agent offers. 2: time to root cause for 'it was green an hour ago' regressions that never reached a commit.
- verdict: escalated to: Mind view: a cockpit of the agent's live beliefs, which the user can edit, rewind and fork. Scrubbing trees is weaker than scrubbing beliefs; the mind view forks from any past state with a correction.

### Motion and sound that track progress [R1 · Foreman UI; skip; kill; value 3, M]
Motion and sound report progress, not activity. The spinner, the step bar and an optional quiet audio tick speed up on real progress (a step closing, a failing check turning green). They slow down, then turn amber, while the agent is busy but getting nowhere, so a looping autonomous run is visible, and audible, from across the room. Today every animation runs at one pace whenever a turn is live.
- mechanism: The mod computes a progress signal: an exponentially weighted average where positive events (step_done, criterion checked, evidence ✓, all-green check_run, red→green on the same criterion) push it up, and negative events push it down (the same failing command repeated, a guard block, a thrash note, one file read 3 times, no progress for twice the typical step time from view.typical). advance() maps the signal to a frame stride of 1 to 4 (FRAME_MS stays fixed), pulse() shifts the hue toward C.warn as the signal goes negative, and past a threshold the band says 'busy, no progress 14m'. Sound is off by default and respects mute: $.audio.play a short tick on progress events with gain by size; a two-note rise when a criterion flips red→green; a low tone when stalled. While the user is away (the away atom is non-empty) and something has needed them for over 5 minutes, $.audio.speak (the local system voice) says one line, e.g. 'Foreman needs your yes on T-0589'. The clips are generated once with Python's wave module into sounds/; nothing is downloaded.
- first slice: M task: the progress signal from existing view and ledger changes, the frame stride, the amber 'no progress' band line and one red→green earcon; tests that a run of identical failing evidence slows the stride and shows the line.
- bar/numbers: 2: idle and loop time. A thrashing autonomous run is noticed within minutes, not when the user next reads the transcript. 4: awareness of a multi-day unattended session without looking at it.
- verdict: Ambient decoration; the demo is animation speed.

### Fleet deck with JARVIS tiles and a steer channel [R1 · Foreman UI; build; escalated; value 4, M]
One pane shows every Foreman session the user runs, local projects and the remote JARVIS machine, as live tiles: task, step bar, what waits on the user, last activity. A steer typed on a tile reaches that session as a delivered message, not as a tmux paste that Claude Code collapses and deprioritizes.
- mechanism: Local: the mod polls `fm projects --json` (exists, T-0322) every 10 s and draws each project as a tile with meter() and coloured waits. Remote, opt-in only (new userConfig 'remotes', e.g. ['<host>']): $.process.run(['ssh','-o','BatchMode=yes','-o','ConnectTimeout=5',host,'fm','projects','--json']) over Tailscale to the user's own machine, off unless configured. Steer on a local tile: `fm --project <slug> capture` or `task log`, then $.session.send({to:{sessionId}}) to that project's running session (meta 'session'). The message arrives as a peer message inside the running turn. Steer on a remote tile: ssh `fm capture` on the host plus one short tmux line, the recipe the memory note proved, or session.send to its Remote Control id when it has one. A tile flashes when any session needs the user. Enter on a local tile opens that project's view in this pane (`fm ui --json --project`).
- first slice: M task: local tiles from `fm projects --json` in a 'fleet' pane tab, a needs-you flash, and steering local sessions via session.send with an fm capture fallback; remote tiles behind the opt-in setting, read-only.
- bar/numbers: 4: the user steers several autonomous agents on two machines from one terminal, and the receiving session treats the message as real input. 2: idle time, because a session waiting on a yes anywhere is seen at once.
- verdict: escalated to: Self-reorganising org: an orchestrator of orchestrators that rewrites its own org chart while the work runs. Tiles become the org chart cockpit, where the org acts on what the tiles only show.

### Subagent tree with in-flight messages [R1 · Foreman UI; build; escalated; value 3, M]
Running subagents and builder lanes appear as a parent–child tree, with each agent's token burn and a live ticker of its last tool calls. The user can type a message into any running subagent, or ask a finished reviewer a follow-up, without spending the main thread's turn or context. Today the pane lists subagents flat, and its only control is Stop after 10 quiet minutes.
- mechanism: LiveAgent gains parentId (AgentInfo.parentId from $.agent.list) and tokens (summed turn.step usage where e.agentId matches). The card draws the tree with box-drawing indents, a miniClawd per running agent, the last 3 tool faces, tokens and elapsed time. Select an agent with keys (a Client, or a button per row); m opens an Input that sends $.session.send({to:{agentId}, text}). For a finished agent, the engine resumes it from its transcript with the message. Lanes: `fm lane list` worktrees appear as tiles, and `git merge-tree --write-tree main <branch>` (via process.run) gives a clean or conflict badge before merging.
- first slice: M task: the parentId tree, tokens per agent, and messaging a running subagent via session.send; mount tests with a mocked agent.list and session.send.
- bar/numbers: 4: the user redirects one subagent mid-run from the UI; no coding agent lets the person talk to a running subagent directly. 2: fewer wasted subagent runs, because a scout heading the wrong way is corrected instead of restarted.
- verdict: escalated to: Self-reorganising org: an orchestrator of orchestrators that rewrites its own org chart while the work runs. Part of the org cockpit: a live tree plus messages to any node through the mailbox.

### Agent whiteboard: a pane Claude draws on [R1 · Foreman UI; build; escalated; value 4, M]
The mod registers a tool that lets Claude put structured visuals on a persistent Board pane: a table comparing approaches, a component diagram, a hypothesis graph, a benchmark chart, a checklist. Claude updates the panels as it works instead of re-describing them in prose. Terminal coding agents today can only print text into the scrollback.
- mechanism: At session.start the mod calls $.tool.register({name:'board', description, inputSchema: panel {id, title, kind: 'table'|'graph'|'chart'|'checklist'|'code'|'note', data}}). A tool.call hook on 'mcp__foreman-ui__board' checks size caps, stores the panel in an atom and in Foreman state (new `fm board put` writing research/board-<task>.json, so it survives compaction and is linked from the brief), and returns {result:'drawn'}. A 'Board' pane tab renders each kind. table: Box rows. graph: layered layout by breadth-first ranks, box-drawing Text on the terminal, Svg on desktop and mobile. chart: Raster bars or sparkline (rows.tsx meter/spark). checklist: glyph rows. code: the Code element (diff format allowed). Foreman's planning reference asks for one panel per medium or large plan (the approach options) and per FIX (the hypothesis graph).
- first slice: M task: register the tool with the table, checklist, note and chart kinds; the Board pane; persistence through fm; one line in planning.md; tests that the tool.call hook stores panels and the pane renders each kind.
- bar/numbers: 1: a persistent visual surface the model can write to, inside a terminal coding agent. 4: the user follows an autonomous run's evolving picture of the problem at a glance, and the board is the plan's living diagram.
- verdict: escalated to: Mind view: a cockpit of the agent's live beliefs, which the user can edit, rewind and fork. Claude drawing tables is still output. The mind view renders the beliefs behind them and lets the user edit them.

### Hedge radar: the model's own hedges become tracked obligations [R1 · Foreman UI; build; escalated; value 4, M]
As Claude streams its reasoning and replies, the mod catches hedges and shortcuts ('probably', 'I assume', 'should be fine', 'skip the test for now', 'workaround') and lists them live under the current step. One key records a hedge as an assumption that must be verified before done, challenges it inside the running turn, or captures a shortcut as a follow-up task. Today an unstated assumption surfaces only if a reviewer happens to catch it.
- mechanism: A turn.step streaming hook passes every chunk through unchanged and copies the text of 'text' and 'thinking' chunks into a per-step sentence buffer. A small pattern set (uncertainty, assumption, shortcut, deferral), with negative patterns for quoted code and test names, scores each sentence. Hits become deduplicated items {quote, kind, step, ts, agentId} in an atom. A 'Hedges this step' card offers keys: a runs `fm task assume ID add "<quote>"` (the existing done gate already lists unverified assumptions); c sends a steer through the steer queue ('You said "...": check it before relying on it'); t runs `fm capture --source discovered` for shortcuts; x dismisses. Dismissals per pattern are counted in $.store to down-weight noisy patterns over time. In full autonomy, shortcuts are captured automatically, and assumptions are recorded automatically on medium and large tasks.
- first slice: M task: the turn.step chunk tap, the pattern scorer, the card with a/c/x, and automatic capture of 'skip / for now / TODO' shortcuts; scorer unit tests on fixture sentences and a mount test.
- bar/numbers: 1: no coding agent mines its own live reasoning for unverified claims and turns them into obligations. 2: less rework from silently wrong assumptions; foreman's ledger records 26 escapes, and this is the class of mistake behind many of them.
- verdict: escalated to: Mind view: a cockpit of the agent's live beliefs, which the user can edit, rewind and fork. Hedges are low-confidence beliefs; the mind view tracks every belief with confidence, not just the hedged ones.

### Blink comparator: the product's screens in the terminal [R1 · Foreman UI; skip; kill; value 3, M]
After a UI change, the pane shows the before and after screenshots from fm smoke as truecolour half-block thumbnails that work in any terminal, Konsole included. It flips between them like an astronomer's blink comparator, so changes jump out, and outlines the changed regions. The user sees what the product looks like now without opening a browser.
- mechanism: A new `fm smoke thumb --run A --run B --cols 60 --json` decodes the kept screenshots with a stdlib zlib PNG decoder, box-downsamples them to 60 × (2 × rows) pixels, quantizes colours to stay under Raster's 1024 colour pairs, and computes a per-cell diff mask. It returns cells for A and B plus the mask; each cell is '▀' with the top pixel as foreground and the bottom pixel as background. A 'Product' card in the Pane draws a Raster (key 'fm-shot') of B with changed cells tinted. b toggles the blink: $.clock.every(500) calls $.ui.blit to swap A and B in place without a redraw. kitty and Ghostty users get the full-resolution Image element instead; other terminals fall back to the half-block Raster. The card appears when the active task's evidence includes an fm smoke run.
- first slice: M task: the thumb command, a static after-thumbnail with diff tint, and the blink key; tests on a tiny PNG fixture (decoder and mask) and a mount test that blit is called.
- bar/numbers: 4: visual verification inside a plain terminal. 2: fewer escaped visual regressions, since fm smoke checks journeys, not looks.
- verdict: Screenshot polish; nice, but the demo is a picture flip.

### Risk heatmap and escape gauge [R1 · Foreman UI; skip; kill; value 4, M]
A gauge of how likely this task's diff is to come back as an escape, learned from Foreman's own history of escapes. A line-level heatmap of the changed files shows where the risk sits: hunks rewritten many times, hunks no test catches (prove --hunks), hot files, files with past escapes. One key proves or reviews just that hunk.
- mechanism: Labels come from 'escape' ledger events (task X's work later escaped, found by task Y; 26 in foreman). Features per done task come from the ledger and briefs: tier, files touched, overlap with hot files (map.json hot), rewrite count (touched events per file), the ratio of failed to passed evidence, guard blocks, surprises, unproven hunks, reviewer findings. A new `fm risk fit` (stdlib logistic regression, refit nightly by fm night) learns the weights, and `fm risk ID --json` returns a probability plus contributions per file. Heatmap: the mod tracks which line bands of each file were rewritten (from the Edit patches it already parses in editChanges/changedLines) and joins the unproven hunks from `fm task prove --hunks --json` (file, at). Each changed file gets a Raster strip 40 cells wide (the file's length), coloured with kit.ts heat(); desktop uses Svg. The task card shows the gauge, e.g. 'escape risk 31% · fmguard.py lines 200-260 rewritten 5×, 2 hunks unproven'. Keys: p proves that hunk, r runs a one-lens review of that file.
- first slice: M task: `fm risk` with a fixed hand-weighted score (no fit yet) over rewrite count, unproven hunks and hot files, plus the gauge and per-file heat strips; predictions are logged so a later fit can be calibrated against real escapes.
- bar/numbers: 3: the model is refit as escapes accrue, so the gauge sharpens with use and its calibration can be checked (predicted against actual escapes). 2: fewer escaped bugs, because proof and review effort go to the riskiest hunks.
- verdict: A gauge. The consequence engine and behaviour diff give the executed version of 'where will this escape'.

### The Conductor: a zero-token control plane that runs Foreman orchestrators on every venue [R2 · Orchestration of orchestrators; build; value 5, L]
Demo: The user types in any session: /conduct "Milestone: voice pipeline v2 - jarvis-api streaming endpoint, jarvis-ui live captions, HUD latency badge" --by 07:00. Foreman answers in three lines: 'Conductor up: 4 orchestrators. O1 jarvis-api (JARVIS box, claude --bg), O2 jarvis-ui (local, background), O3 HUD (local tmux pane), O4 integration (waits on O1's contract). Envelope 2.4M tokens.' The foreman-ui pane grows one tile per orchestrator, each with its task, step bar, leases and burn. The user closes the laptop. In the morning there is a merged milestone, one capsule per orchestrator and the train log. The 'wait, it can do THAT?' moment: the JARVIS orchestrator was started and steered without a single tmux keystroke. And when O2 died at 03:10 on a usage-limit StopFailure, the Conductor resumed it at 04:00 on its own. Today Foreman is one orchestrator per terminal, plus fire-and-forget fm run lanes: at most 3, one repo, one machine, and the run stops after two failures. The Conductor is a daemon whose control loop is plain Python: spawn, observe, lease, merge, restart. So coordinating N orchestrators across repos and machines costs zero model tokens. A model is called only at named decision points (decompose, judge, resolve), and it gets a compiled snapshot of the fleet, never a transcript.
- mechanism: Runs on: a systemd user unit (the fmserve.unit_text pattern, with the fmserve.beat heartbeat and start-limit breaker). Local background orchestrators use claude --bg, claude agents --json, and claude attach/logs/stop/respawn/rm, all verified in 2.1.295. Streamed children use claude -p --input-format stream-json --output-format stream-json --include-hook-events; fmsession's runner already normalises that stream. Visible local orchestrators use claude -w NAME --tmux. JARVIS is reached through an ssh ControlMaster that runs fm node on the far side. Phone-steerable orchestrators use claude --remote-control NAME. Data: state/conductor/registry.jsonl holds Orchestrator Handles {oid, venue, host, repo, session_id, branch, subqueue, leases, envelope, supervisor, parent}. One VenueDriver interface has five drivers (bg, stream, tmux, ssh, rc): spawn(prompt, cwd, model, settings_json), send(msg), observe() -> normalised events, stop(). send goes over the bus, not keystrokes. observe() reads events.jsonl, stream-json or claude agents --json. Each child starts with --settings JSON that sets FOREMAN_OID and wires the lease and envelope hooks, and with FOREMAN_DRIVE_TASK scoping drive to its sub-queue, as fm run already does. Decision points call one Opus child through claude -p --json-schema with a world-state snapshot of at most 8k tokens (registry, capsules, train state). fm pause (c.panicked) stops the Conductor and every child.
- first slice: fm conduct with two venue drivers: bg (claude --bg plus claude agents --json) and ssh (ssh host fm ...). It keeps the registry, observe and stop, and has a --once --json status. fm conduct start ID... launches one background orchestrator per task, each in its own lane with FOREMAN_DRIVE_TASK set, and shows each one in the pane. Useful alone: it replaces the hand-typed JARVIS tmux delivery and the hand-run 60 s ssh Monitor.
- bar/numbers: Coordination today: the main Opus session drives lanes and builders by hand. Every launch, poll and merge is a model turn that re-reads the context; the measured mean is ~280k tokens per reply (2,333M cache reads / 8,325 replies, fm cost, 7 days). A 4-orchestrator overnight milestone has about 60 coordination events: 60 x 280k x 0.1 (cache-read price) ~ 1.7M input-equivalent. The Conductor makes ~6 decision calls x ~10k = 60k: ~96% less coordination spend. Wall clock: serial fm run takes ~25 min per task, so a 16-task milestone takes ~6.7 h. Running 4 wide with ~20% train overhead takes ~2 h.

### Fractal Foreman: a task too big for one session becomes its own orchestrator, and only a capsule comes back up [R2 · Orchestration of orchestrators; build; value 5, L]
Demo: Mid-task, Foreman says: 'T-0612 (L, migrate state to sqlite) predicts 2.3M tokens and 140 turns. One session stays useful for about 90. Promoting it to orchestrator O-T0612: 5 sub-tasks on integration branch foreman/T-0612.' A child tile appears under the task in the pane and sprouts three builder lanes of its own. An hour later the main thread gets one 30-line capsule: what changed, five evidence trees, two decisions and one open risk. T-0612 then closes on its own criteria, run once on the integration branch. The main session's context grew by 900 tokens for a 2-million-token task. Today a task that is too big either drives one context into compaction or is split by hand into sibling briefs, which the same session then works one after another. Promotion makes any node a full orchestrator with its own sub-queue, lanes and merge train, recursively. Only a typed capsule flows back up; a parent never reads a child's transcript.
- mechanism: Runs on: nested workflow() in the Workflow tool for in-session promotion (small fan-out, up to ~12 agents); claude --bg -w foreman/<ID> for session promotion; --json-schema for the capsule; StopFailure and SessionEnd hooks to detect completion. Trigger: a predictor that runs at plan time and again mid-flight. It estimates turns from recall neighbours' step counts x this repo's turns per step, and reads the live context slope from the statusline snapshot. It promotes when the prediction exceeds 0.7 x a session's useful horizon (measured: builders reach 85-104k context after ~95 turns). Promotion: fm fractal ID writes state/projects/<slug>/sub/<ID>/, a sub-project that shares the parent's ledger through a parent field (fm batch's host/member bookkeeping, run in reverse). It then runs the partitioner plus one planning call, creates the integration branch foreman/<ID> off main, registers an Orchestrator Handle whose drive is scoped to the sub-queue, and marks the parent task delegated (c.held_elsewhere already hides it from other lanes). Children merge into the integration branch through the train. When the sub-queue drains, the node's acceptance criteria run once on the integration branch. A capsule {changed_files, trees, decisions, risks, lessons, cost}, validated by --json-schema, goes to the parent's research/, and the integration branch enters the parent's train as one car. Depth is capped at 3. Each level gets a slice of its parent's envelope. A child that wants to promote again has to ask its parent over the bus, and the parent may refuse.
- first slice: fm fractal ID for one L task. It writes a sub-queue from the brief's step groups (one child brief each, with explicit scopes) and makes the integration branch. It runs the children through the existing fm run --parallel lanes, merging into foreman/<ID> instead of main. Then it runs the host's criteria once and writes a schema-checked capsule into the parent brief. Useful alone: any L task can run unattended as a mini-queue and come back as a 30-line result.
- bar/numbers: Measured mean context per reply is ~280k tokens. A 2M-token L task in one session re-reads a context that grows past 200k. Each fractal child starts near the 25k median first-reply context and finishes under 90k, so the mean per-reply context drops from ~150k to ~55k: ~60% less cache-read spend for the same work. The parent's context grows by ~1k (the capsule), instead of the ~40-60k of launches and results a hand-driven split adds and then re-reads every turn.

### Plans compile to Workflow scripts: deterministic, resumable fan-out that never enters the model's context [R2 · Orchestration of orchestrators; build; value 5, M]
Demo: The user says 'do the 9 lint-debt items'. Instead of nine hand-driven Agent calls, Foreman writes and runs one Workflow script with three phases. 'ground' is a pipeline of 9 Haiku scouts. 'build' is a pipeline of Sonnet fm-builders in worktrees. 'prove' is one barrier into the merge train. The main thread shows a single tool row with a live progress tree, and gets back a 12-line table. The user closes the laptop mid-run. On reopen, Foreman calls Workflow with resumeFromRun, and only the two unfinished items run again. Foreman's in-session fan-out today is the model typing Agent calls one by one and reading every result into its own context. Compiling the plan to a deterministic Workflow script takes orchestration out of the model's context. Each agent's model, effort, isolation and output schema is set by Foreman's policy instead of chosen ad hoc. A crash resumes from the last completed agent.
- mechanism: Runs on: Claude Code's Workflow tool. It provides agent(prompt, {model, effort, isolation:'worktree', agentType, schema}), pipeline(), parallel(), nested workflow(), budget.remaining(), resumeFromRun and a per-workflow agent() cap; scripts must be deterministic, so no Date or Math.random. fm flow compile ID...|MILESTONE emits the .js from briefs. Each brief becomes one agent() with agentType foreman:fm-builder or foreman:fm-scout and a model from the treasury policy, and the schema is the capsule schema. Stage order comes from depends_on: pipeline() where items are independent, a barrier only at the train. args are the brief paths. A PreToolUse hook on Workflow checks the script's agent() count x per-agent estimate against the envelope; if it's over, the hook rewrites model fields through updatedInput. Results come back as capsules, and one fm flow settle RUNID call records each as evidence and queues its lane for the train.
- first slice: fm flow compile for a list of S/M task ids produces a Workflow script: builders in worktrees, schema'd capsules, policy models. fm flow settle turns the returned capsules into evidence and lane merges. Useful alone: any multi-task request becomes one resumable call.
- bar/numbers: Hand-driven fan-out costs the main thread ~3-8k tokens per Agent launch plus its result, and that text is then re-read on every later turn. Nine builders add ~9 x 6k = 54k of context, re-read over ~40 more turns at 0.1: ~216k input-equivalent plus the 54k itself. With Workflow it is one call and a ~2k summary, ~10k total: ~95% less main-thread spend on fan-out. Resume also avoids re-running finished agents after a crash, and each fm-builder is ~885k input-equivalent (measured median).

### Leases: function-level area locks across sessions and machines, enforced before the edit [R2 · Orchestration of orchestrators; build; value 5, M]
Demo: Two orchestrators run in parallel, and O2 (UI) tries to Edit fmcore.py's lock(). The tool row turns amber: 'Lease: fmcore.py:lock() is held by O1 (T-0601) until it merges, ~14 min. Asked O1 for a sublease; working step 3 meanwhile.' Forty seconds later, at O1's next tool boundary, code grants the sublease without asking O1's model, because O1's remaining steps never touch lock(). O2 gets 'lease granted' and retries. No merge conflict ever happens, and the pane's lease map shows the two regions in two colours. Lanes give physical isolation, so collisions only surface at merge time, as conflicts or semantic breaks. Leases make collisions impossible before the edit, across sessions, venues and machines, at function granularity. Contention is resolved by protocol (sublease, wait, wound-wait), with no model involved.
- mechanism: Runs on: PreToolUse deny-with-reason on Edit, Write, MultiEdit and NotebookEdit, and on the Bash writes fmguard already parses. Also a WorktreeCreate hook, so every worktree (Agent isolation:'worktree', claude -w) is born with its lease set and from the right base commit; that fixes the stale-base workaround in fmlanes.CONTRACT (T-0379) at the source. Requests and grants travel over the bus. Data: state/leases.sqlite (stdlib sqlite3, WAL) {lease_id, holder oid, repo, kind file|symbol|glob, target, mode read|write|frozen, ttl, renewed_at, waiters}. Acquisition: at dispatch the Conductor leases the task's predicted footprint (fmserve._footprint: scopes plus co-change pairs) plus symbol spans from an ast index. An edit outside the lease triggers an automatic request. It is granted if the span is free, subleased if the holder's remaining steps don't name it, and queued otherwise. Deadlock: a wait-for graph spans leases and bus RPCs, and a cycle is broken by wound-wait: the older task wins, the younger releases and retries. The holder's PostToolUse renews its TTL, so a dead session's leases expire. JARVIS: the Conductor host is the lease authority, and remote hooks call ssh conductor fm lease want over the ControlMaster. When the authority is unreachable, the remote node fails closed to its own repo partition.
- first slice: fm lease: the sqlite table with want, grant, release and list, TTLs, and file and glob granularity. fm lane new acquires leases from the task's footprint, and a PreToolUse check denies edits to files another live holder has leased, naming the holder and an ETA. Useful alone: two local sessions in one repo can't stomp on each other.
- bar/numbers: fmserve._batch only parallelises tasks whose footprints are pairwise disjoint, so any two tasks that touch fmcore.py run one after the other, and nearly every task touches it. With symbol-level leases, two tasks on different functions of the same file run together. If half of today's serialised pairs touch disjoint functions, the number of orchestrators that can work at once roughly doubles, from ~1.5 to ~3. Each avoided merge conflict also saves a resolver session (~260k, reviewer-sized) and a gate rerun (~52 s median).

### Coupling-aware partitioning: min-cut the code graph so orchestrators split along the code's real seams [R2 · Orchestration of orchestrators; build; value 4, M]
Demo: /conduct plan "move every fm command to a registry" prints: 'Footprint 41 files, 187 symbols. Best 4-way split by min-cut: cut weight 7 (5 import edges, 2 co-change). The 7 cut edges become frozen contracts with 7 contract tests, committed first. Predicted train-red rate 6%, against 31% for a split by directory.' The Conductor then sends out 4 orchestrators, each owning one region, with contract tests guarding the seams between them. The surprise: functions that always change together end up in the same region even when they live in different directories. Decomposition today is a model's guess at plan time, or sibling briefs typed by hand. This computes the split from the code's real coupling: imports, calls and fm map's co-change history. It minimises the edges between orchestrators and turns each edge it has to cut into a contract test before any parallel work starts.
- mechanism: Runs on: plain Python in fm (no model for the partition), git worktrees, then Conductor dispatch with leases in frozen mode on cut interfaces. The graph's nodes are symbols (Python ast, with a regex definition index for other languages) and files. Edge weights come from static import and call edges, fm map co-change pair strength (fmmap.load()['pairs']) and past cross-orchestrator conflicts from the train log. Algorithm: multilevel k-way partitioning (coarsen by heavy-edge matching, refine with Kernighan-Lin/Fiduccia-Mattheyses) under a balance constraint from predicted work per node (recall neighbours). k minimises predicted makespan + lambda x cut weight, with lambda learned by shape learning. Each cut edge becomes a contract item: its interface signature is frozen, and one Sonnet call writes a contract test that is recorded with fm task evidence. Each region becomes a sub-queue with its own lease set.
- first slice: fm partition --task ID|--files GLOB -k N: a Python symbol graph from ast plus co-change weights, KL refinement, and as output the regions, the cut edges and a footprint per region. When given regions, fm run --parallel uses them instead of hand-written scopes. Useful alone: a straight answer to 'can this be parallelised, and where are the seams?'.
- bar/numbers: Cross-orchestrator conflicts grow with cut weight. Example: split by directory, a 41-file change cuts 31 of 140 weighted edges (22%); min-cut cuts 7 (5%), so about 4x fewer conflicts. Each avoided conflict saves ~260k tokens of resolver work and a ~52 s gate rerun. The partition itself costs zero tokens; contract tests cost ~7 Sonnet calls x ~15k = ~105k.

### Merge train with speculative stacking and bisect-on-red: verify the batch, not every change [R2 · Orchestration of orchestrators; build; value 5, M]
Demo: Eight lanes finish within ten minutes. The pane shows a train of 8 cars stacked on main. It runs one full gate (52 s) instead of eight, and the gate goes red. The train splits: cars 1-4 are green and 5-8 red; then 5-6 green, so car 7 is red. Car 7 goes back to its orchestrator with the failing output and the diff of the car before it. Cars 1-6 and 8 land on main. That took 4 gate runs instead of 8 rebase-and-gate cycles in a row, and no session sat waiting. Today each finished lane integrates on its own: rebase, full gate, fast-forward (fmserve._integrate_lane). N lanes cost N full gates queued one behind another, and a change that conflicts only in meaning is found after it lands. The train verifies batches speculatively and finds a culprit by bisection, as bors and Zuul do for human teams. No coding agent does this for its own parallel output.
- mechanism: Runs on: one train git worktree, fm check's tree-hash reuse of passing results, and the Conductor loop, with the bus returning a culprit car to its owner. Queue: state/train.jsonl {car, branch, oid, task, base, enqueued}. Stacking: each car is rebased onto the previous one with git rebase --onto. A textual conflict ejects the car, and its owner gets the conflict hunks. Gate: each car already ran its cheap affected checks in its lane (fm check --affected). The train runs the full gate once, on the stack tip. On red it tests the first half of the cars; trees already tested reuse their result. One culprit costs ceil(log2 n) extra runs. Speculation: while train k's gate runs, train k+1 is stacked on k's tip, and restacked if k goes red. Landing fast-forwards main to the last green prefix, after the existing _main_moved checks. Flakes: a red that passes on an immediate rerun is logged to flakes.json and not bisected.
- first slice: fm train: enqueue finished lane branches, stack them in one worktree, run fm check once on the tip, bisect on red, fast-forward the green prefix, and send the culprit back to the queue with its failure. fm run --parallel uses it instead of integrating each lane separately. Useful alone: a parallel run lands in one gate run.
- bar/numbers: Measured gate median: 51.5 s. Eight lanes today means 8 x (rebase + 52 s) in a row, ~7 min of gates plus waiting in line. The train needs 1 run when all cars are green and 1 + 3 = 4 when one car is red: 50-88% fewer gate runs. The same ratio applies to minutes on slow-gate repos such as volt (189 gate runs a week). Tokens: lane sessions exit once their car is enqueued instead of waiting in context for integration, saving ~10 waiting turns x ~90k x 0.1 ~ 90k per lane.

### Milestone contracts: many items, one proof [R2 · Orchestration of orchestrators; build; value 5, M]
Demo: The user types 'CLEAN: the 23 small things in the inbox'. Foreman replies: 'Milestone M-7: 23 items, 4 orchestrators, one proof: full suite + smoke journey + one adversary review of the combined diff. Per item: automatic affected-test evidence only.' Seventy minutes later there is one close-out card. 23 items are done. One was bisected back by the train and fixed. One review found 3 issues, each fixed test-first. The ledger holds 23 per-item evidence lines that nobody typed. Then the user sees the bill: 1 reviewer pass instead of 23. Foreman's unit of proof today is the task. Every S item gets its own fm check, evidence ceremony, gates, reviewer and finish call, which is what made the user say 'don't test every little change'. Milestones move the expensive proof to the batch. Items carry cheap, automatic evidence; the full gates, audits and smoke run once on the merged result; and the train's bisection maps any failure back to the item that caused it. Verification stays complete while the ceremony stops multiplying.
- mechanism: Runs on: fm batch's host/member bookkeeping (members close done-in the host); PostToolUse auto-evidence, where the hook records each member's affected-test run with its tree hash and no model turn; the merge train for localising failures; one foreman:fm-reviewer pass over git diff base...milestone_tip; fm smoke for journeys. Rules: every member criterion must be machine-checkable. A member closes provisional when its affected checks pass in its lane. The milestone proof runs once: full gate, the audits fm gates names, and prove --hunks over the combined diff to catch hunks no evidence touched. Failures map back to members, gate failures by bisection and review findings by hunk ownership (git blame on the milestone range), and only those members reopen. A proof-debt meter in the pane shows the items closed provisionally and the proofs still owed, so nothing counts as done without the milestone proof.
- first slice: fm milestone new 'TITLE' ID..., built on fm batch: members close provisionally on automatically recorded affected checks. fm milestone prove runs the full gate, prove --hunks and one reviewer pass over the combined diff, maps findings to members by hunk ownership and reopens only those. Useful alone, even in a single session: 20 small items cost one proof.
- bar/numbers: Measured: 135 reviewer passes at a median of 264k input-equivalent across ~310 tasks, about 115k of review per task on average. Per-item proof for 23 items: 23 x (115k review + ~40k of finish and evidence turns) ~ 3.6M. As a milestone: one review of the combined diff (~1.5 x 264k ~ 400k) plus 23 x ~5k auto-evidence ~ 0.5M, about 86% less spent on proof. Gates: 23 full fm check runs (~20 min at 52 s) become 1-5 train runs (~1-4 min).

### Cross-repo atomic milestones: two-phase merge across repos and machines [R2 · Orchestration of orchestrators; build; value 4, M]
Demo: An API field rename touches jarvis-api on the JARVIS box, plus jarvis-ui and the HUD repo on the laptop. The Conductor shows three trains 'prepared', each green in its own repo. Then it runs the cross-repo checks: the UI's e2e suite against the API's prepared branch, served on JARVIS, and the HUD smoke against both. If they pass, all three repos fast-forward in the same minute. If the HUD fails, nothing lands and the HUD car goes back with the failure. The user never sees a half-migrated system. Coding agents land changes one repo at a time, so a contract change across repos and machines leaves a window where consumers are broken, or a human has to manage the order. Two-phase merge lands a multi-repo, multi-machine milestone all at once or not at all, with each repo verified against the others' prepared branches.
- mechanism: Runs on: the merge train in each repo, the Conductor as coordinator, ssh to JARVIS for remote repos, and cross checks the milestone declares, which can name peer worktrees and services (for example API_URL served from a prepared worktree). Phase 1, prepare: each repo's train builds its green prefix with the milestone's cars and holds it as refs/foreman/prepared/<milestone>. Phase 2, cross-verify: the Conductor starts the declared services from prepared worktrees (locally or over ssh) and runs the cross checks.
