Phase 3, commit or abort: commit fast-forwards every repo's main to its prepared ref, each guarded by _main_moved; abort drops the prepared refs and returns the failing cars. Every decision is written to the coordinator log before it is executed, so a crash between commits is recovered by rolling forward.
- first slice: fm milestone land M --repos A,B: prepare refs in each local repo, run the declared cross checks with both prepared worktrees available as env paths, fast-forward all of them or none, and keep a coordinator log for recovery. Local repos only; JARVIS over ssh comes in the next slice. Useful alone: jarvis-api and jarvis-ui stop shipping half-changes.
- bar/numbers: Without it, a 3-repo contract change is landed in order by hand. That leaves broken intermediate states, and usually a fix-forward task when a consumer breaks (~1M tokens each at the measured per-task average). With it, the cost is one extra cross-check run of a few minutes, with no broken window. It saves one fix-forward task for every cross-repo change that would have broken a consumer.

### Work market with context affinity: work goes to the orchestrator that already knows the code [R2 · Orchestration of orchestrators; build; value 4, M]
Demo: A bug report lands: 'guard misreads a|&b'. The pane flashes a sealed-bid round. O2 has been in fmguard.py for an hour; its cache is warm and its lease is adjacent, so it bids 38k tokens. O3 is fresh and idle and bids 140k. JARVIS is busy and declines. O2 wins and has a red test in 4 turns. The pane says why: 'won on warm context: 92% of the needed files already in its context'. Round 1's market picks a model for each task. Across orchestrators the bigger cost is a cold context: a fresh session spends 15-51 turns reading before its first edit. This market sends each piece of work to the orchestrator whose live context and leases already cover it. Bids are computed from telemetry, not by a model.
- mechanism: Runs on: the Conductor loop; bus 'cfp' (call for proposals), 'bid' and 'award' messages, handled by code inside each orchestrator's hooks with no model turn; and transcripts as context telemetry. Contract-net protocol: the Conductor announces a package {id, footprint, est_work}. Each orchestrator's bidder keeps a PostToolUse-maintained index of the files and symbols in its context since its last compaction (from Read, Grep and Edit calls) and computes coverage = |footprint intersect context| / |footprint|. Its bid = est_cold x (1 - 0.8 x coverage) + queue delay cost + lease conflict penalty + venue cost (JARVIS is cheap when idle; a local Opus session is priced from its envelope). The award goes to the lowest bid whose envelope covers it, and ties go to the shorter queue. If every bid costs more than a fresh session, the award is 'spawn new'. Settlement logs predicted against actual tokens, so the 0.8 discount is learned per repo.
- first slice: fm market: for each live session, its context file set from its transcript since the last compaction; for a given task, each session's coverage and bid. fm conduct and fm run --parallel award the work to the best live session (delivered by mail) or start a fresh one. Useful alone: 'which of my running sessions should take this?' is answered from data.
- bar/numbers: Measured: builders reach their first edit after 15-51 turns at 33-62k context. That is ~25 turns x ~45k average context ~ 1.1M cache-read tokens, ~110k input-equivalent of cold start. If 40% of packages in a multi-orchestrator run go to a warm session (coverage > 0.7), that saves ~0.4 x 110k ~ 44k per package, plus 3-10 minutes of wall clock each.

### Treasury: fleet-wide budget envelopes and model routing, enforced by rewriting calls [R2 · Orchestration of orchestrators; build; value 5, M]
Demo: At 01:30 weekly usage is at 74%, and the Conductor's treasury line reads 'pace 1.3x, tightening'. In O3, an interactive session, the model calls Agent with model 'opus' for a scout job. The tool row shows it run as 'haiku (treasury: scout jobs go to haiku above 70% pace)'. A headless orchestrator hits its --max-budget-usd 4.00 cap and stops cleanly with a capsule. The Conductor moves O1's unused 1.2M to O4, which is on its last step. The morning digest says: 'Spent 61% of the night envelope; no task starved.' Budgets today are per-feature caps checked by whatever code launches the work (fmbudget.check, subagent_pause). The treasury divides the whole fleet's weekly budget into envelopes per orchestrator and enforces them from outside the model: a PreToolUse rewrite of Agent and Workflow model fields, and a hard --max-budget-usd on headless children. It also moves budget to wherever the remaining work is.
- mechanism: Runs on: PreToolUse on Agent and Workflow with updatedInput, which rewrites model and effort by policy or denies with the cheaper alternative. Headless children get claude -p --max-budget-usd (headless only) plus --model, --effort and --fallback-model. Usage comes from the statusline rate-limit snapshot (fmbudget.rate_limits), metering from SubagentStart and SubagentStop, and settlement from transcripts (fmbudget.transcript_tokens). Data: state/treasury.json {week_budget, pace, envelopes {oid: {alloc, spent, floor}}, policy}. The policy table maps job class x pace band to model and effort, where job class comes from agentType and the description (scout, recon, review, build, judge). Allocation: each envelope is proportional to that orchestrator's remaining critical-path work x its value. Every 15 minutes it re-allocates: unspent budget above 2x need is reclaimed, and a node within one step of done gets a top-up. Below 75% weekly usage the treasury only meters, per the user's PACE_FLOOR rule. Above that it routes down a ladder (opus -> sonnet -> haiku for non-judgment work, effort high -> medium) before it ever pauses anything. fm pause still stops everything.
- first slice: A PreToolUse hook on Agent that rewrites the model field from a policy table keyed by subagent_type and pace band, logging each rewrite as a treasury_route event. Children of fm run and fm conduct start with --max-budget-usd from a per-run envelope. Useful alone: subagent routing is enforced without trusting the model to choose.
- bar/numbers: Measured launches: 135 reviewer, 18 recon and 23 general-purpose, mostly on the main model. Send recon (median 192k) and general jobs (179k) to Haiku at ~1/5 the price, and half of the reviews (264k) to Sonnet at ~1/1.7. Saved fraction ~ (18 x 192k x 0.8 + 23 x 179k x 0.8 + 67 x 264k x 0.4) / (18 x 192k + 23 x 179k + 135 x 264k) ~ 13.1M / 43.2M ~ 30% of subagent spend for the same number of launches, and more once builders take the ladder too. The hard caps bound any runaway child at its envelope instead of the session limit.

### Spawn by fork: every orchestrator is born as a prompt-cache fork of one primer session [R2 · Orchestration of orchestrators; build; value 5, M]
Demo: /conduct starts 6 orchestrators. Instead of six sessions each reading MASTER.md, the brief and the hot files, the pane shows one 'primer' reading for 90 s, then six tiles appear within the same second, each already knowing the repo. The first child's first tool call is an Edit. The token meter shows startup at 1.6x the cost of one session instead of 6x. Today every orchestrator and builder cold-reads the same context: MASTER.md, rules, the brief, the hot files. Spawning each one by forking a primer session (--resume primer --fork-session) within the prompt-cache lifetime means N children share a single read. Each pays only the cache-read price for the shared prefix and starts at its first decision.
- mechanism: Runs on: claude -p --session-id <uuid> for the primer; claude -p or --bg --resume <primer> --fork-session for each child; --system-prompt-snapshot on to keep the prefix byte-stable; the prompt cache's 5-minute lifetime. Children launch within it, and the primer re-warms with a one-token turn if the launch slips. fm builds the primer's read set: the union of the children's footprints, ranked by how many children need each file, filled up to a ~60k-token budget, plus the MASTER.md section map and the milestone brief. The primer ends on a fixed 'you will be forked; wait for your assignment' turn. Each child's first user message is its sub-brief from the partitioner and leases. Forks share no runtime state, so isolation still comes from lanes and leases. A SessionStart hook logs cache_read against cache_creation on each child's first turn to confirm the cache hit.
- first slice: fm primer build MILESTONE: compose the shared read set and run the primer headless under a fixed session id. fm conduct start --fork-from PRIMER launches the children, and a SessionStart probe records each child's first-turn cache-hit ratio. Useful alone: fm run --parallel lanes start warm.
- bar/numbers: Cold start per orchestrator: ~25 reading turns at ~45k average context, with ~60k established at cache-creation price (x1.25). Six cold children pay 6 x 60k x 1.25 = 450k input-equivalent just to establish context, before counting the reading turns. Forked: the primer pays 60k x 1.25 = 75k once, plus 6 x 60k x 0.1 = 36k for the children, 111k in total. That is ~75% less startup spend, and each child skips ~10-20 reading turns (3-8 minutes).

### Orchestrator bus: typed messages, most handled by code, wake-on-mail only for judgment [R2 · Orchestration of orchestrators; build; value 5, M]
Demo: Five orchestrators are running, and in 40 minutes 212 messages flow between them: lease wants and grants, budget top-ups, capsules, bids, heartbeats. The pane's bus ticker shows them, and the token meter reads zero for 205 of them, because hooks applied them to state without waking a model. Seven were questions or steers; each woke exactly one idle orchestrator (asyncRewake) and was answered in one turn. From the phone, the user sends one line to O3 with ssh box fm bus send O3 ..., and O3 switches tasks at its next tool boundary. Round 1's mailbox delivers text to a model. Orchestration needs a protocol. Most coordination (leases, budgets, awards, heartbeats, capsules) is machine to machine and should never cost a model turn. The rest needs request/response with deadlines, topics, and delivery across machines. The bus makes the hierarchy addressable by orchestrator id, parent, children and topic, and keeps it cheap.
- mechanism: Runs on: the round-1 mailbox delivery paths (PreToolUse additionalContext injection, a Stop decision=block, and an asyncRewake Stop entry that exits 2 to wake an idle session), plus ssh for remote nodes. Data: state/bus/<oid>/inbox/*.json, mode 0600, sequence-numbered and idempotent by message id, with topics in state/bus/topics.json. Message types: lease.want/grant/deny, budget.set, cfp/bid/award, capsule, heartbeat, interrupt, question/answer (with a deadline), steer, policy. A dispatch table in fm applies lease, budget, award, heartbeat and policy messages to state directly inside the hook. Only question, steer, interrupt and review requests reach the model, framed as data with their sender. RPC: fm bus ask OID 'Q' --deadline 300; the asker keeps working, the answer arrives as a message, and a missed deadline escalates to the asker's supervisor. Pub/sub: subscriptions by path glob or topic ('anyone touching auth/'). Across machines, the Conductor relays over the ssh ControlMaster and queues messages for offline nodes. Wakes are rate-limited per node (for example 6 an hour) and duplicates are dropped.
- first slice: The round-1 mailbox slice (fm mail send/list, PreToolUse and Stop delivery, the asyncRewake MailWait), plus typed messages with a code dispatch table for lease and heartbeat types, and fm bus ask with a deadline. Useful alone: sessions can talk to each other and to JARVIS without tmux.
- bar/numbers: If every message were model-visible mail: ~200 messages x (~2k injected + the turn's ~150k context re-read at 0.1) ~ 3.4M input-equivalent per 40 minutes. Handled by code: 7 model-visible messages x ~17k ~ 120k, about 96% less. Latency: a lease grant applied in a hook takes ~50 ms; a model turn takes 10-30 s.

### Delegated permissions down the tree: a parent answers its children's prompts and can only grant what it holds [R2 · Orchestration of orchestrators; build; value 5, M]
Demo: Six headless orchestrators work overnight without bypassPermissions. O4 needs npm install (the network category). Its permission prompt doesn't kill the session: it goes to O4's parent, which holds the user's standing grant for that project and approves it in 30 ms. O5 asks to git push --force. No ancestor holds that, so at 07:00 the user gets one phone notification: 'O5 (T-0640) asks to force-push foreman/T-0640. Approve for O5 only?' One tap, and everything else has already run. Today headless children either run with broad permission modes or get denied and stall, and fm ask grants only work in the interactive session that asked. Delegated permissions make every orchestrator the permission host for its children. A parent can grant only what it holds (attenuation), so the whole tree is as safe as its root, and the user is asked only about what no node below may decide.
- mechanism: Runs on: claude -p --permission-prompts host --permission-prompt-tool mcp__foreman__approve, so a child's prompts go to an MCP tool served by fm mcp, extended with this one decision tool. The guard (fmguard categories) is the decision function. PermissionRequest and PermissionDenied hooks log decisions, and fm ask serves the root. Each Orchestrator Handle carries a capability set {category, scope (repo/branch/path), expiry, delegable}: its parent's set intersected with the child's brief scope. approve(tool, input, oid) classifies the call with fmguard. If the category is in the child's set and the input is in scope, it allows. Otherwise it forwards to the parent's approver as a bus question with a deadline. At the root, requests batch into one user digest (fm notify), answered through fm ask at the next attach. Every decision is logged as approval_granted with its chain of grantors. A child can never receive a capability its parent lacks. Core (Foreman edits) and destructive categories never delegate below the root without a yes for that node.
- first slice: The fm mcp approve tool. fm run children launch with --permission-prompts host --permission-prompt-tool and are decided from the project's existing standing grants (fm ask --standing) through fmguard; everything else goes to one notify digest. Useful alone: fm run stops dying on permission prompts.
- bar/numbers: Today an fm run session that hits a permission prompt is denied and ends unfinished, and FAILS_MAX=2 then stops the whole run. If 1 in 8 tasks needs one non-default permission, a 16-task night stalls about twice and stops. With delegation, those calls resolve in milliseconds when an ancestor holds the grant, and the rest collapse into one morning digest instead of a stopped run.

### Supervision trees: restart, quarantine and escalate, the Erlang/OTP way, for agents [R2 · Orchestration of orchestrators; build; value 5, M]
Demo: At 03:10 O2 dies on a usage-limit StopFailure. Its supervisor waits out the limit and restarts it at 04:00 from a capsule handoff: a fresh 25k-token context, not the old 180k one. At 03:40 T-0655 crashes its orchestrator for the third time with the same signature, so it is quarantined: lease released, worktree kept, crash signatures attached. Then it is escalated: a retry on Opus with fm-debugger hypotheses, which still fails, then one line in the morning digest. The other five orchestrators never stopped. 'Let it crash' becomes a feature, because a restart is cheaper than the session it replaces. fm run has one breaker for the whole run: two failed sessions in a row stop everything. Supervision trees give every orchestrator and builder a supervisor with a restart strategy, intensity limits and an escalation ladder. One poisoned task can't stop the fleet, and a crash costs only a cheap fresh start.
- mechanism: Runs on: StopFailure hooks (payload: error, error_details, last_assistant_message) and SessionEnd hooks; heartbeat files (fmserve.beat, stale_beats); claude respawn, --resume, or a fresh claude -p to restart; the bus for escalation; fmserve._fingerprint to detect no progress. Each child has a spec: {strategy: one_for_one | rest_for_one (tasks that depend on the crashed one restart after it) | one_for_all (a tournament heat), max_restarts: 3 per 30 min, backoff, restart_from: capsule | resume}. Restart policy: if the crashed session's context was under 60% full, resume it. Otherwise start fresh, seeded with a capsule of at most 5k tokens: the brief, Resume-here, the last evidence and the last 20 events. Quarantine: after the same crash signature (normalised last error + step) three times, release the leases, keep the worktree, mark the brief quarantined with its signatures, and skip it in the queue. Escalation ladder for a quarantined task: stronger model or effort, then fm-debugger hypotheses, then a tournament if the budget allows, then the user digest. Supervisors are supervised too: the Conductor runs under systemd Restart=always with the existing start-limit breaker.
- first slice: Supervisor specs for fm run lanes: per-lane StopFailure and no-progress detection, one_for_one restart into a capsule-seeded fresh session, quarantine after 3 identical signatures, one escalation to a stronger model, and a digest line. This replaces the run-wide FAILS_MAX breaker for parallel runs. Useful alone: an unattended run survives one bad task.
- bar/numbers: fm run stops after FAILS_MAX=2 unfinished sessions in a row. In a 16-task night with a 10% per-task failure rate, two failures in a row somewhere are likely, and every task after that waits for morning. With one_for_one isolation only the failing branch stops, so close to 90% of the 16 finish instead of whatever ran before the first double failure. A capsule restart at 25k instead of resuming at ~180k saves ~155k x 0.1 = 15.5k input-equivalent per turn, ~620k over the ~40 turns that follow.

### Tournament mode: k orchestrators race different strategies, a judge merges the best and mines the losers [R2 · Orchestration of orchestrators; build; value 5, M]
Demo: T-0670, a flaky race in the voice pipeline, has already failed once. Foreman: 'Tournament: 3 strategies in worktrees, 1.5M cap in total. A: minimal lock. B: queue redesign. C: make it deterministic, then fix.' At 25% of the budget, successive halving drops B, which has turned no new tests green. C wins: its hidden deterministic repro passes 50 of 50, it has the smallest diff, and its mutation score is 0.92. Then the twist: the judge mines A. A's stress test catches a second race that C didn't fix. The test is moved into C's branch, C fixes it, and A's dead end becomes a lesson. The user gets one merged branch and a 6-line verdict. Racing attempts was rejected in the seen ideas for cost. With successive halving, a hard cap per heat and a deterministic judge, a tournament costs about 1.75x one attempt and raises the success rate on hard tasks. The losing heats aren't waste: their tests, probes and dead ends are mined into the winner and into memory.
- mechanism: Runs on: one claude --bg -w <heat> per strategy, or Workflow agent({isolation:'worktree', model, effort}); --max-budget-usd per heat; the supervisor with one_for_all; fm check and prove for judging; and one Opus --json-schema judge call only to break ties. Trigger: a task that has failed once, an L task with no close recall neighbours, or the user saying 'race it'. Strategies come from a library (minimal patch, refactor first, test first from properties, make deterministic first, rewrite the module), chosen for diversity and by past win rate for the task class. Successive halving checks at 25% and 50% of each heat's cap; progress is newly green criteria plus evidence count (fmserve._fingerprint), and the bottom half is dropped. The judge is deterministic first: all criteria and the full gate green; hidden checks the heats never saw (a held-out test the Conductor wrote from the brief); prove --hunks mutation kill rate; diff size; guard and lint clean. The LLM judges only ties. Mining: every loser's new tests run against the winner, and any that fail there are missed cases, added as failing tests on the winner's branch. Losers' hunks on lines the winner never touched are offered as cherry-picks. Each loser's dead end becomes a lesson (fm task log plus a tripwire).
- first slice: fm race ID --k 3: three worktree heats with distinct strategy prompts and --max-budget-usd each, one halving checkpoint, a deterministic judge (criteria + gate + diff size + prove --hunks), and harvesting of loser tests into the winner. Useful alone: a 'race it' button for any task that has failed once.
- bar/numbers: If one attempt on a hard task succeeds with p = 0.45, three independent heats succeed with probability 1 - 0.55^3 ~ 0.83. Cost with halving: 3 heats x 25% + 2 x 25% + 1 x 50% = 1.75 attempts. A naive race costs 3. Serial retries with fresh context until success average ~2.2 attempts, and take ~2.2x the wall clock. Mining finds about one missed-case test per tournament, which is one escape prevented; each escape costs a FIX task, ~1M tokens.

### Consumer review: orchestrators review the changes whose interfaces they consume [R2 · Orchestration of orchestrators; build; value 4, M]
Demo: O1 finishes the API change, and its capsule goes out for review. It doesn't go to a fresh reviewer. It goes to O2, the UI orchestrator that consumes that API and already has the client code in context. O2 answers in 3 turns: 'breaks captions: partial is now nullable and live_captions.tsx:88 assumes a string', with a failing test attached. A cold reviewer would have needed 18 turns and 264k tokens, and would probably have missed it, because it never reads the UI. Reviews today are cold, isolated fm-reviewer passes. They have to re-read the change's context, and they don't know who consumes it. In a fleet, the best reviewer for a change is the orchestrator whose region consumes it: its context is warm, its leases border the cut edge, and its own tests are the contract.
- mechanism: Runs on: the partition's cut edges and the lease table, to pick reviewers (the holders of regions with edges into the changed symbols); a bus 'review' message, delivered by the Stop hook at the consumer's next step boundary; SendMessage when the consumer is a live subagent; and the capsule's diff plus its list of changed symbols. Protocol: the consumer must answer either 'compatible', with the consumer-side test command it ran on the prepared train tip, or with a finding plus a failing test in its own region (prosecutor-style proof). Its verdict counts as the edge lens in fm gates. A cold fm-reviewer runs only for lenses no consumer covers (intent, security), and at milestone level.
- first slice: fm review route ID: use fm map co-change and import edges to find live sessions (fm lane list, claude agents --json) whose task scope consumes the changed symbols. Deliver the diff to them as a review request by mail, and record the answer, with its test command, as the edge lens. Fall back to fm-reviewer when no consumer is live. Useful alone with two parallel lanes.
- bar/numbers: Measured cold reviewer: median 18 turns and 264k input-equivalent. A warm consumer review takes ~3 turns at its current ~80k context: 3 x 80k x 0.1 plus output ~ 30k, about 89% less per review. It also catches cross-region breaks that a cold reviewer structurally can't see, because the cold reviewer never loads the consumer code.

### Steer cascade: one steer, typed anywhere, reaches and binds every node it concerns [R2 · Orchestration of orchestrators; build; value 4, M]
Demo: On the phone, in O3's Remote Control session, the user types 'no new dependencies; use stdlib'. Within one tool boundary, every orchestrator whose plan mentions installing a package receives it as policy. O1 is halfway into pip install pydantic; the call is denied with 'steer from the user via O3, 02:14: no new dependencies'. The pane's steer badge reads '1 steer -> 4 nodes, 1 enforced block'. Today a steer lands in one session as words, and the other sessions never hear it. In a fleet, that means typing the same correction five times. The cascade classifies each steer once, routes it to every node it concerns (down the tree and sideways), and compiles the enforceable ones into guard policy.
- mechanism: Runs on: the UserPromptSubmit hook in every node, Remote Control sessions included, to capture steers; bus 'steer' and 'policy' messages; the PreToolUse guard for enforcement; and fm taste and vetoes for storage. Classification is by code first. Scope (paths, tasks, global) comes from the files and task ids the steer mentions. Kind is either a veto pattern (deny an install, an import, a command) or a preference (words only). A Haiku --json-schema call is used only when the code classifier is unsure. Routing: global steers go to every live node; scoped steers go to nodes whose leases or sub-queues intersect the scope; a steer given to a parent cascades to its whole subtree. Enforceable steers become veto rules with provenance (who, when, from which node), checked in PreToolUse in every node. The others are injected once, as data, at the next tool boundary. Revoking a steer is one message.
- first slice: Capture steers in UserPromptSubmit and store them as global or path-scoped vetoes with provenance. Deliver them to every live session of the project at its next PreToolUse: additionalContext once, and a deny whenever a veto pattern matches. Useful alone: two sessions in one repo share corrections.
- bar/numbers: With 5 orchestrators, a steer that doesn't propagate is retyped up to 5 times or violated, and each violation costs a correction plus rework (~50-200k tokens). One cascade costs ~0 tokens (it is routed by code) plus at most one Haiku call, and the user types it once.

### Deadline scheduler: critical path and an elastic fleet, 'done by 7am' as the only input [R2 · Orchestration of orchestrators; build; value 4, M]
Demo: At 23:00 the user types '/conduct M-8 --by 07:00'. Foreman: 'Critical path 5h20 through T-0701 -> T-0705 -> T-0709; the work off that path needs 3 orchestrators. Starting 4, with the critical path on Opus at high effort.' At 02:30 the critical-path task is running long. The scheduler pulls an orchestrator off slack work, starts a tournament heat on the critical task, and pushes two cosmetic items past the deadline. At 06:41: 'M-8 landed; 2 deferred items scheduled for tomorrow; 71% of the night envelope used.' fm run works the queue in order until it is empty or something stops it, and nothing reasons about time. The scheduler turns 'done by 7am within budget' into a schedule. It computes the critical path of the task graph from predicted durations, sizes the fleet to the budget pace, and re-plans when tasks run long, the way a project manager would, every 5 minutes, at no token cost.
- mechanism: Runs on: the Conductor loop, Orchestrator Handles, the treasury, the supervisor, tournaments, and Workflow or claude --bg spawns. Data: the task graph from depends_on plus inferred dependencies (c._deps) and train order; duration distributions from recall neighbours' minutes (run_session minutes, fm cost). Algorithm: CPM/PERT with Monte Carlo over the duration distributions gives P(on time) for each plan. List scheduling puts critical-path work first across k orchestrators. k is the smallest number that reaches P(on time) >= 0.8 within the envelope and the host's load (os.getloadavg, free memory). Every 5 minutes it re-estimates from progress fingerprints and re-plans. When P drops, the levers are: add an orchestrator, upgrade the critical path's model or effort, start a tournament heat on a critical task, or defer the lowest-value off-path items (each deferral recorded with fm decide).
- first slice: fm schedule M --by HH:MM: the task graph plus duration estimates from history, CPM with a Monte Carlo P(on time), a recommended k and model per task, and fm run --parallel following critical-path-first order. Useful alone: 'will this queue be done by morning, and what do I cut if not?'.
- bar/numbers: Today serial fm run at ~25 min per task finishes a 16-task milestone in ~6.7 h, with no idea of a deadline. Scheduled 4 wide with a 5.3 h critical path, the makespan is max(critical path, total / k) = max(5.3, 6.7 / 4 ~ 1.7) ~ 5.3 h. Extra spend goes only to the critical path (for example a tournament on 1 of 16 tasks, +0.75 attempt ~ +0.8M tokens), not to every task.

### Shape learning: the Conductor learns which decompositions and team shapes win, partly from free simulation [R2 · Orchestration of orchestrators; build; value 4, M]
Demo: /conduct plan "port the guard to the policy table" shows three shapes, with predictions from this user's own history. 'Serial in one session: 5.8 h, 1.9M, low escape risk. 3 orchestrators by min-cut: 2.4 h, 2.3M, 9% train-red. Fractal, 2 levels: 2.1 h, 2.8M, 14% train-red. Recommended: 3 by min-cut, which won 7 of 9 comparable milestones.' After the run, the actual numbers are written next to the prediction and the shape table updates. A month later Foreman has stopped proposing 6-wide fleets in the guard code, because they lost every time there. Every orchestration choice above (k, partition, venue, model per level, tournament or not) is a guess today, and no coding agent learns team design from its own results. The Conductor records each run's shape and outcome and learns, per repo and work class, which decompositions and team shapes win. It also scores shapes it never ran, by replaying the fleet log under them in a simulation.
- mechanism: Runs on: the Conductor's run log (registry, bus, train and treasury events), fm bench for paid replays when needed, and a contextual bandit in plain Python. Features: work class (type and tier mix), footprint size, coupling (cut weight from the partitioner), repo, venue mix, budget pace. Arms: shapes = (k, partition strategy, depth, model ladder, tournament policy). Outcomes: makespan, tokens, train-red rate, conflicts, escapes within 14 days (joined from escape events), user corrections. Learner: Thompson sampling over a Bayesian linear model per outcome, maximising utility = -(tokens x price + minutes x value of time + escapes x cost). Counterfactuals: recorded task durations and conflict events replay through the scheduler under another k or partition, a discrete-event simulation that costs zero tokens, to score shapes that weren't run. Exploration is bounded: at most 1 milestone in 5 explores, and never above 75% weekly usage.
- first slice: Log every fm run and fm conduct run's shape and outcome (k, partition, models, makespan, tokens, train reds, and escapes joined later). fm shapes shows past shapes by work class with their outcomes and recommends the best by posterior mean; the discrete-event replay comes next. Useful alone: the first evidence-based answer to 'how parallel should this be?'.
- bar/numbers: Good and bad shapes are far apart: in the example above, 2.4 h against 5.8 h, and 2.3M against 2.8M tokens. Suppose learned choices close half the gap between the median and the best shape, ~2 h and ~0.5M tokens per milestone, on 10 milestones a month. That is ~10 h and ~5M tokens a month, compounding as the data grows. Most of the learning comes from simulation, so it costs nothing.

### Work compiler: a queue becomes milestones that are edited in one pass and proven once, with group-testing blame [R2 · Revolution pass; build; value 5, L]
Demo: The user pastes 14 lines (FIX/CLEAN/FEATURE). In 20 s Foreman replies: 'Compiled 14 items into 2 milestones. M1: 9 items, all in fmguard.py and its tests; one read plan (3 spans of fmguard.py); 31 obligations; proof plan = 6 commands instead of 41 evidence runs and 9 gates. M2: 5 items, sent to a lane.' The band shows one obligation bar. 25 minutes later one gate run goes red. The model never sees the log. Foreman bisects the stack of sealed item snapshots in a scratch worktree (3 runs, 2.5 min) and reports: 'item 6 (IFS in an assignment) broke test_heredoc_body; it interacts with item 3.' Only item 6 goes back to the model, with that diff and the assertion. The milestone closes with 9 per-item commits, each with its obligations ticked, and one reviewer pass over the whole diff. The moment: 'it verified 9 changes with 7 test runs and found which one broke, without the model reading a single failure log.' Every coding agent runs the loop once per item: read, edit, test, review, commit, repeat. The work compiler fuses items into milestones the way a compiler fuses loops. Shared reads are hoisted, edits to one file happen in one pass, and verification is deferred to one proof plan. When that plan goes red, the culprit is found by group testing (merge-queue bisection, plus ddmin for items that only break together) instead of by testing every change as it lands. The proof is no weaker: each item still ends with its own obligations proven on its own commit.
- mechanism: New fmcompile.py (stdlib) and the CLI commands `fm compile [IDs|--queue]` and `fm ms ...`. (1) Footprint per item = scope globs + predicted files (fm map co-change, fm impact). Union-find on shared files groups items, within the canonical type order and depends_on edges; at most K=10 items per group. Each group becomes a milestone brief holding a fused read plan (unique files and spans), the items in dependency order and an obligation table (item, criterion, verify command). (2) Proof plan: obligations map to commands; identical commands are merged, tests are chosen per obligation (fm check --affected), and a greedy set cover picks the smallest set of commands. (3) Execution: `fm ms item N` marks the current item, and PostToolUse attributes touched files to it. `fm ms seal N` stores c.worktree_tree under refs/foreman/ms/<id>/<n>. No test runs between items, except an item's own criterion when it takes under 5 s. (4) `fm ms prove` runs the proof plan once on the final tree. Green: every obligation gets evidence pointing at that run. Red: binary search over the seals, running only the failing commands, finds the first bad item in about log2(n) runs. If the results are not monotonic, ddmin over item subsets finds the interacting pair. The model gets only the culprit item's diff and the assertion, as a 3-line PreToolUse note. (5) Close: one foreman:fm-reviewer pass over the milestone diff with the item intents. `fm ms finish` turns each seal into a per-item commit (git commit-tree) with its Foreman-Task trailer. Primitives: CLI, PreToolUse/PostToolUse hooks, git worktrees and refs, the fm-reviewer subagent, and the Stop-hook drive.
- first slice: `fm compile --dry-run` plus `fm ms` for one milestone: clustering by shared scope files, the fused read plan, obligations, a greedy set-cover proof plan, item seals as tree refs, `fm ms prove` with binary search over the seal stack, and per-item commits at finish. Test: a toy repo with 4 items where item 3 breaks a test; prove must name item 3 in at most 3 runs and leave 4 per-item commits. Useful alone on the next guard batch.
- bar/numbers: Baseline used below (this machine, Foreman project, last 7 days, from fm cost / fm digest / ledger): 298.2M input-equivalent tokens for 349 finished tasks (~0.85M per task); cache reads 2,332M raw = 233M input-equivalent = 78% of all spend; 8,318 replies, so ~280k tokens of context re-read per reply; tool results filling context: Bash 46%, Read 44%; active time 30h11m: model 61%, Bash 38%; gate (fm check) median 51.5 s, p90 114 s over 330 runs; per finished task ~4.4 evidence runs and ~2.9 audit records. This week, 8 separate guard tasks (T-0576 to T-0668) all edited fmguard.py (2,688 lines, about 38k tokens). At ~0.85M each they cost ~6.8M. As one milestone: fmguard.py is read once instead of 8+ times, which saves at least 7 x 38k = 266k directly. The bigger saving is that each extra read is then re-read on every later turn, and cache reads are 78% of spend. ~35 evidence runs become ~6 proof commands, 8 gates (8 x 51.5 s = 6.9 min) become 1-2, 8 reviewer passes become 1, and 8 close-out cycles become 1. Estimate: 6.8M -> 2.0-2.5M (about -65%). On a red, blame costs log2(8) = 3 targeted runs. If 40% of queued work clusters like this, weekly spend falls ~25% (~75M input-equivalent).

### Shadow twin: every edit is executed in a twin worktree within seconds; only behaviour flips reach the model, and edits can be tried before they land [R2 · Revolution pass; build; value 5, L]
Demo: The agent makes 14 edits without running a single test. The band shows a strip of 14 dots, and each turns green a few seconds after its edit. At edit 9 the dot turns red, and the agent's next tool result carries 3 lines: 'twin: edit 9 (fmguard.py:412 _top) flipped test_ifs_assign red: AssertionError, a=$IFS allowed; last green edit 8.' The agent never sees the 600-line test output. Later, torn between two patches, it calls the `try_edit` tool with both. Six seconds later: 'A: no flips. B: fixes test_x, breaks test_heredoc (2).' The moment: 'it's running my tests on every edit, the model only hears when something changes, and it can try an edit before making it.' Agents verify by deciding to run tests, reading the whole output and judging it. Here verification is ambient and differential. A background twin mirrors every edit into its own worktree, runs the tests known to react to that edit, and tells the model only about flips, with the edit that caused each one already named. The same twin answers 'what happens if I make this edit?' before the edit is made, so the agent can explore candidate patches without touching the real tree. NCrunch and Wallaby run tests continuously for humans; no agent has continuous testing as a sense, with per-edit blame and a counterfactual tool.
- mechanism: (1) `fm twin start` runs at focus (from the UserPromptSubmit/SessionStart hooks). It creates a git worktree at the task's base tree and starts a detached daemon with a heartbeat in state. (2) PostToolUse on Edit/Write/MultiEdit, plus Bash writes found by fmguard._write_targets, queues {seq, path, patch}. The daemon applies each patch to the twin, bumps mtimes so caches don't lie, and runs the tests that react to that symbol. Those come from learned flip edges (test t flipped after an edit to symbol s), seeded from fm check --affected links and from a sys.monitoring coverage pass in the twin, within a 20 s budget. The full gate runs when the twin has been idle for 60 s. (3) Results are kept per seq. The difference from the previous result set is the flips. Flips go to the model once, through PreToolUse additionalContext, and the Stop hook won't end a turn while a flip is unreported. No flip means no message and no tokens. (4) Simulate-before-edit: a local stdio MCP server, fm-twin, offers `try_edit(patches[], tests?)`. Each candidate runs in a disposable detached worktree taken from a pool of 3 warm ones, and the result is its flips. Also `twin_status`. (5) When the twin's last full run is green on tree T and T equals the real tree, fm check reuses it through its existing same-tree reuse, so the agent's own gate run costs nothing. Primitives: PostToolUse, PreToolUse additionalContext and Stop hooks; git worktrees; an MCP server; a background daemon; fm check's tree reuse.
- first slice: A Python-only twin for Foreman's own repo: `fm twin start|stop|status`, Edit/Write mirroring in PostToolUse, test choice from fm check --affected links plus learned flip edges, flips delivered once through PreToolUse, and fm check reusing a green twin run on an identical tree. Test: in a fixture repo, an edit that breaks one test makes the next PreToolUse payload carry exactly that flip, naming the edit's seq. try_edit comes in slice 2.
- bar/numbers: Baseline used below (this machine, Foreman project, last 7 days, from fm cost / fm digest / ledger): 298.2M input-equivalent tokens for 349 finished tasks (~0.85M per task); cache reads 2,332M raw = 233M input-equivalent = 78% of all spend; 8,318 replies, so ~280k tokens of context re-read per reply; tool results filling context: Bash 46%, Read 44%; active time 30h11m: model 61%, Bash 38%; gate (fm check) median 51.5 s, p90 114 s over 330 runs; per finished task ~4.4 evidence runs and ~2.9 audit records. fm repeats: `python3 plugin/tests/run.py -k ...` was typed 721 times in 155 tasks (4.7 per task), plus 288 runs with -p and 140 of `python3 -m unittest`, about 1,150 manual test runs. Each manual run costs a turn (~280k context x 0.1 for cache reads = ~28k) plus its log (~3k, written to cache and re-read for ~20 more turns, ~6k), so ~35k. If the twin removes 3 of the 4.7 runs per task, that's ~105k per task (~12% of 0.85M), or ~37M a week over 349 tasks. Wall time: Bash is 38% of active time, and the twin's runs happen while the model is thinking, off the critical path.

### Behaviour diff: review and verify what the program now does, not what the code says [R2 · Revolution pass; build; value 5, L]
Demo: A 14-edit milestone finishes. The user and the reviewer don't get a 600-line code diff. They get: 'Behaviour changes over the full suite (2,140 function calls compared). 9 intended: _split("a${IFS}b") now returns [a, b] (was [a${IFS}b]), matching spec items 3 and 4, ... 1 unintended: _heredoc_body(" x") now strips the leading space; it reaches check_python and check_node, and no test asserts it.' One key turns the unintended change into a FIX or pins it with a characterization test generated from the recorded input and output. On a CLEAN refactor the result is: 'Behaviour diff empty over 2,140 calls: the refactor preserves behaviour.' The moment: 'it diffed what the program does across every call in the suite and found a change no test checks.' Code review reads text and guesses at behaviour, and tests check only what someone thought to assert. Recording the input and output at every function boundary while the whole suite runs, at the base tree and at the final tree, and then lining the two up, gives an exhaustive, executed answer to 'what did this change change'. That includes changes no assertion notices. Batched work gets a cheap, strong oracle for its negative space (nothing else changed), and 'is this refactor safe?' becomes a proof.
- mechanism: (1) Recorder: a sitecustomize shim on PYTHONPATH. It uses sys.monitoring (PEP 669) PY_START/PY_RETURN/RAISE on code under the repo root and gets the test id from a patched TestCase.run or the pytest hook. Each call is stored as (test, qualname, arg fingerprint = hash of a reprlib repr capped at 120 chars and passed through c.redact) -> return or raise fingerprint, with caps per function. Storage: sqlite at state/projects/<slug>/behav/<tree>.db. (2) `fm behav record --tree T` runs the gate's test commands in a worktree at T with the shim. `fm behav diff A B` joins on (test, qualname, arg_hash) and reports changed results, call keys that appeared or vanished, and new raises, grouped by function and by caller. A nondeterminism mask records the base tree twice and drops keys that differ between those two runs (timestamps, ids, ordering). (3) Classification: changes whose names or examples match the brief or the compiled spec are intended; the rest are unintended. An unintended change no assertion reaches is marked 'unasserted'. (4) Wiring: the work compiler's prove step adds the diff from base to final; CLEAN tasks need an empty diff or each change acknowledged; fm audit prep gives the reviewer the behaviour diff; a mod card offers f (capture a FIX) and p (pin a characterization test). On a red, `fm behav diff last-green now --test t` names the first divergent call on the failing path. Primitives: CLI, PYTHONPATH exported through SessionStart's CLAUDE_ENV_FILE, git worktrees, the fm-reviewer subagent, a foreman-ui card.
- first slice: Python only: the recorder shim, `fm behav record` at two trees, and `fm behav diff` with the two-run nondeterminism mask, grouped by function and printed at `fm task finish` for CLEAN tasks. Test: in a toy repo, a refactor changes one helper's output for one input that only an unasserted caller reaches; the diff must name exactly that call.
- bar/numbers: Baseline used below (this machine, Foreman project, last 7 days, from fm cost / fm digest / ledger): 298.2M input-equivalent tokens for 349 finished tasks (~0.85M per task); cache reads 2,332M raw = 233M input-equivalent = 78% of all spend; 8,318 replies, so ~280k tokens of context re-read per reply; tool results filling context: Bash 46%, Read 44%; active time 30h11m: model 61%, Bash 38%; gate (fm check) median 51.5 s, p90 114 s over 330 runs; per finished task ~4.4 evidence runs and ~2.9 audit records. Recording costs about 1.3-2x suite time with sys.monitoring and disabling after the cap. On Foreman's ~51 s gate that is ~100 s, once per milestone rather than per edit. Review: a reviewer reading a 600-line diff plus files uses ~60-100k tokens; a 2-4k behaviour diff plus the hunks it points to should cut that ~60%. Escapes: round 1 counted 26 in Foreman. If a third were unasserted behaviour changes reachable from the suite, ~8 would have been shown before close.

### Rebirth: sessions die at every milestone or thrash and are reborn from a capsule proven sufficient by a quiz [R2 · Revolution pass; build; value 5, M]
Demo: A 6-hour unattended run. The band reads 'gen 9 . context 31k'. Each time a milestone closes or the agent thrashes, the session ends and a new one starts from an 8k-token capsule, and the user sees no break. On a thrash the band says: 'Step 3 failed 3 times with the same signature. Rebirth from the last green tree, carrying 2 dead ends, the constraint that killed them, and 1 untested hypothesis.' The reborn session fixes it in 4 calls. The moment: 'it ran for 6 hours, the context never passed 40k, and the retry after the thrash started from a clean tree but still knew what had failed.' Today a session grows until auto-compaction squeezes it with a summary nobody checks (about 686k tokens, per the efficiency research), and every reply re-reads everything. Here that is 78% of spend. Rebirth makes session death a planned, checked operation. The capsule is built from structured state (brief, evidence, beliefs, dead ends, the spans actually used) and tested before the old session dies: a cheap child given only the capsule must answer questions generated from the old transcript, and any it misses are patched in. A thrash rebirth also resets the tree to the last green moment. That is the human move of starting over with what you've learned, done mechanically.
- mechanism: (1) Triggers: a milestone or task closes; context passes T* = b + sqrt(2*K*g), the efficiency research's midpoint-law reset point, fitted per project from transcripts; or a thrash (the same failure signature 3 times). (2) `fm capsule build`: brief, Resume-here, open hypotheses with their status, dead ends (reverted edits and edits after which the signature didn't change, from touched events and evidence), steers verbatim, a ranked read-set of fm outline spans by reads and edits in this task, and the twin's flip state. Budget 6-10k tokens. (3) `fm capsule quiz`: a Haiku claude -p child writes 10-15 question/answer pairs from the outgoing transcript tail (facts learned, approaches rejected and why, what the user said). A second tool-less child answers from the capsule alone, a grader marks the misses, and the misses are appended (at most 2 rounds). The score is logged. (4) Rebirth. Unattended (fm run, fm serve, tmux): the runner starts a fresh claude with the capsule as its first prompt and closes the old one. Interactive: a foreman-ui command does /clear and then $.prompt.submit(capsule). Thrash: the attempt tree is saved as refs/foreman/attempt/<n>, the last green evidence tree is restored with git read-tree -u --reset, and the capsule cites the attempt ref. SessionStart injects the live parts (fm next, unreported flips). Primitives: Stop and SessionStart hooks, the fm run/serve runners (claude -p, tmux), the mod's $.prompt.submit, Haiku children, git refs.
- first slice: `fm capsule build ID` plus `fm capsule quiz ID` (Haiku question/answer pairs from the transcript tail, answered from the capsule alone, misses patched in), and rebirth wired only into fm run's headless loop at task boundaries: a fresh claude -p per task with the capsule, instead of --resume. Measure tokens per task on the next 20 run tasks against the last 20, and the quiz score per capsule. Test: a fixture transcript with 3 planted facts; a capsule missing one fails the quiz and is patched.
- bar/numbers: Baseline used below (this machine, Foreman project, last 7 days, from fm cost / fm digest / ledger): 298.2M input-equivalent tokens for 349 finished tasks (~0.85M per task); cache reads 2,332M raw = 233M input-equivalent = 78% of all spend; 8,318 replies, so ~280k tokens of context re-read per reply; tool results filling context: Bash 46%, Read 44%; active time 30h11m: model 61%, Bash 38%; gate (fm check) median 51.5 s, p90 114 s over 330 runs; per finished task ~4.4 evidence runs and ~2.9 audit records. If rebirth holds average context near 50k instead of ~280k, cache reads fall from 8,318 x 280k = 2,332M raw (233M equivalent) to 8,318 x 50k = 416M raw (42M equivalent), saving ~191M. Costs: ~400 rebirths x (8k capsule + 24k system prompt rewritten) x 1.25 = ~16M, plus quizzes of ~400 x 40k Haiku tokens (~16M Haiku, ~3M Opus-equivalent at a 1:5 price ratio). Net ~ -170M input-equivalent a week, about -57% of all spend. Thrash retries start at 8k instead of the full context (~35x cheaper per turn) and without the contaminated context that drives repeated identical fixes.

### Opus on call: the cheapest model attempts every step first, checked by machines not by Opus; the frontier model is paged only on failure [R2 · Revolution pass; build; value 5, M]
Demo: The user leaves a 30-item queue running overnight. Morning card: '30 done. Steps attempted: Haiku 71 (58 accepted by the twin and the spec), Sonnet 19 (15 accepted), Opus 6 (paged: 4 hard failures, 2 one-way-door decisions). Opus tokens 0.4M; a similar queue yesterday used 4.1M.' Opening a page shows: 'Haiku and Sonnet both failed step 2 of T-0712 with the same flip (test_x). Opus read their two diffs and the flip and wrote the fix in 3 calls.' The moment: 'the frontier model woke up six times all night, and nothing got worse.' Model routing today is a fixed map, a bid or an effort knob, and the expensive model still drives the loop and reads everything. This inverts the loop, like speculative decoding at the level of plan steps. A cheap model drafts each step, and machines decide whether to accept it: the shadow twin's flips, the compiled spec's tests, the behaviour diff and the guard. Most steps never reach Opus. Opus becomes an exception handler that sees only a compact failure packet (the step, the failed diffs, the flips) and never the exploration.
- mechanism: (1) `fm run --cascade haiku,sonnet,opus`, also usable by the General (H). For each step: `claude -p --model haiku` in the task's lane worktree with the step and the capsule. Acceptance: the step's verify command is green, the twin shows no new red flips, the behaviour diff has no unintended changes (when it exists), and the guard is clean. On failure, escalate to Sonnet with a ~3k failure packet (the previous attempt's diff ref and flips), then to Opus. (2) Learned start level: an acceptance posterior Beta(a,b) for each (model, step verb class, file cluster, task type). Start at the level that minimises expected cost, sum over k of P(reach k) x cost_k; skip Haiku when P(Haiku accepts) < 0.2. (3) Opus is always paged for one-way-door decisions (decide --kind costly|outward) and writes L plans, which are cheap next to execution. (4) Interactive mode: the Opus main thread hands off a step with `fm cascade step N`. The attempts run in background lanes, and an asyncRewake Stop entry wakes the main thread with either the accepted result or the failure packet. Primitives: claude -p --model, git worktree lanes, fm budget, asyncRewake hooks, the twin/spec/behaviour checks.
- first slice: `fm run --cascade haiku,sonnet` for S tasks only, per task rather than per step. Haiku attempts the task in a lane; acceptance is the criteria's verify commands plus fm check green on the lane tree; a failure goes to Sonnet with a failure packet. Acceptance is recorded per (type, area), and fm cost reports a cascade line. Useful alone: overnight S queues stop using Opus.
- bar/numbers: Baseline used below (this machine, Foreman project, last 7 days, from fm cost / fm digest / ledger): 298.2M input-equivalent tokens for 349 finished tasks (~0.85M per task); cache reads 2,332M raw = 233M input-equivalent = 78% of all spend; 8,318 replies, so ~280k tokens of context re-read per reply; tool results filling context: Bash 46%, Read 44%; active time 30h11m: model 61%, Bash 38%; gate (fm check) median 51.5 s, p90 114 s over 330 runs; per finished task ~4.4 evidence runs and ~2.9 audit records. Assume a per-token price ratio of Haiku 1 : Sonnet 3 : Opus 5 (check current prices with the claude-api skill) and equal-size attempts. Every step tries Haiku (1 unit); 30% go on to Sonnet (0.3 x 3 = 0.9); 10% reach Opus with a packet about half the size (0.1 x 2.5 = 0.25). That's 2.15 units against 5 for all-Opus: -57% cost on cascaded work, and Opus use down ~95%, which frees the Opus quota for planning and hard debugging.

### Trajectory JIT: the agent's hot habits are compiled into deterministic tools, and the hook swaps them in when the habit starts [R2 · Revolution pass; build; value 4, M]
Demo: The agent starts the JARVIS update it has done 16 times (ssh, git pull, plugin update, reload, check). At the first ssh call, PreToolUse answers: 'This is the start of trace jarvis-update (16 runs, median 7 calls, 41k tokens). The compiled tool `fm do jarvis-update` reproduces all 16 recorded command sequences. Running it.' One call, 3 seconds, a 2-line result. Overnight Foreman compiled 5 tools like this from its own transcripts, and wrote a skill for each. The moment: 'it watched itself work, spotted the same 7-step routine, turned it into a tool, and now uses the tool instead.' Agents work out the same multi-call procedures from scratch every time and pay for every intermediate output. This is a tracing JIT for agent behaviour. It mines hot call sequences across transcripts, turns their varying parts into parameters, and compiles each into a deterministic script with the agent's own intermediate checks as assertions. The script is proven against the recorded runs, installed as a tool plus a skill, and the hook routes the habit to it. fm repeats spots repeats and suggests an alias; nothing compiles agent trajectories into proven tools and rewires the agent to use them.
- mechanism: (1) Miner (an fm night job): transcripts (fmclaude reader) become tool-call sequences per task window. Commands are normalised with the guard's shell tokenizer, so paths, T-ids, hosts and shas become typed parameters. A stdlib frequent-subsequence pass finds runs of at least 3 calls seen in at least 4 tasks with a consistent successful outcome. (2) Compiler: a Sonnet claude -p child, given the instances, writes plugin/tools/<name>.sh or .py with parameters, the agent's intermediate checks (grep -q, exit codes) as assertions, an output contract, a `--dry-run` mode, a SKILL.md saying when to use it, and a trigger signature (the first 1-2 command shapes). (3) Proof: read-only or idempotent tools run beside the recorded sequence in a scratch worktree at the recorded commit, and outputs and exit codes are compared. Tools with side effects (ssh) must print, in --dry-run, exactly the recorded command sequence for every past instance once parameters are filled in. A certificate is stored. The guard still judges each command the script runs. (4) Routing: PreToolUse matches the trigger on Bash. It adds a note saying the tool exists, or, for a proven tool under a standing grant, rewrites the call through updatedInput to `fm do <name> <params>`. Uses, bypasses and failures are logged; a tool that fails twice drops back to a suggestion. Primitives: transcripts, fm night, claude -p child, PreToolUse updatedInput, the skills directory, the guard.
- first slice: `fm jit mine --days 30` (frequent normalised Bash subsequences with parameters, support and tokens spent) and `fm jit compile NAME` for one sequence: a script with --dry-run, proven by dry-run equivalence against every recorded instance, plus a PreToolUse note on its trigger. Targets: the JARVIS update routine and the MASTER/CHANGELOG close-out check.
- bar/numbers: Baseline used below (this machine, Foreman project, last 7 days, from fm cost / fm digest / ledger): 298.2M input-equivalent tokens for 349 finished tasks (~0.85M per task); cache reads 2,332M raw = 233M input-equivalent = 78% of all spend; 8,318 replies, so ~280k tokens of context re-read per reply; tool results filling context: Bash 46%, Read 44%; active time 30h11m: model 61%, Bash 38%; gate (fm check) median 51.5 s, p90 114 s over 330 runs; per finished task ~4.4 evidence runs and ~2.9 audit records. fm repeats: run.py -k 721 times over 155 tasks, `claude plugin test` 120 over 33, `ssh -o BatchMode=yes` 16 over 8 (the JARVIS routine), `grep -q T-ID MASTER.md && ... CHANGELOG.md` 11 over 10, `git -C ...` 17 over 9. A 7-call routine at ~28k cache-equivalent per turn costs ~196k plus outputs; compiled, it's 1 call (~28k), saving ~170k per use. At 50 uses a week that's ~8.5M, and 7 model round trips (1.5-3 min) become ~5 s.

### Context PGO: Foreman profiles where every token goes and recompiles its own prompts, injections and output digests to the smallest form that still passes [R2 · Revolution pass; build; value 5, M]
Demo: Monday morning card: 'Context PGO, last night: always-loaded rules 6.1k -> 2.3k chars; intake skill 9.4k -> 4.1k; MASTER.md section reads (31 last week) replaced by a 1.8k capsule; a learned digest of run.py output keeps the 3 kinds of line the agent actually used in 94% of 212 past runs. Replayed on 380 past moments: 0 behaviour changes. Projected: -22M tokens a week. Merge?' The user merges, and the next week's fm cost shows -19M. The moment: 'it measured which of its own words change its behaviour and deleted the rest, with proof.' Context cost is treated as fixed overhead, or trimmed by fixed rules (the round-1 firewall). PGO treats context the way a compiler treats a hot loop. It profiles which bytes are paid for and which are used: an output line counts as used when later reasoning, edits or commands reference it. Then it rewrites: instruction text shrinks by ablation, per-command output digests are learned from that usage, files read again and again are hoisted into capsules, and cache busts are fixed at their source. Every rewrite is accepted only if behaviour on replayed past moments is unchanged.
- mechanism: (1) Profiler (fm night): from transcripts, attribute the input tokens of each reply to rules, skills, injected note kinds (fmhooks additionalContext), tool results by tool and command shape, files read (path and how many times per session), and cache writes (from turn.step usage in the mod). Usage labels: a tool-result line is 'used' if its distinctive tokens (identifiers, paths, numbers) appear in an assistant text, thinking or tool input within the next k turns. The midpoint-law model (N, b, T, K) prices each lever. (2) Rewriters: (a) ablate or rewrite rules, skills and note templates with the fm evolve --drop machinery; (b) learned digests: per command shape, a stdlib line classifier (FAIL/Error/path:line/summary features) trained on used and unused lines, installed as a sync PostToolUse updatedToolOutput digest with the raw output stashed and readable through `fm peek`; criterion verify commands are exempt; (c) read hoisting: files read N+ times across sessions get a capsule (outline plus the spans actually used) injected at focus; (d) cache-bust fixes: injections that change every turn are de-timestamped and moved to the end. (3) Gate: every candidate runs Night school's never-again moments in dry-run, where behaviour means the same next-action class. It's accepted only with 0 regressions and a negative token delta, then gets a certificate and a one-click merge card. Primitives: transcripts, PostToolUse updatedToolOutput, fm evolve and bench moments, claude -p dry-run replays, mod turn.step usage, fm night.
- first slice: `fm pgo profile --days 7`: token attribution per source, and used/unused labels on Bash output by command shape. Plus one learned digest (run.py/unittest output) installed as a sync PostToolUse updatedToolOutput with the raw output stashed, `fm peek`, and the exemption for criterion verify commands. Measured on next week's fm cost.
- bar/numbers: Baseline used below (this machine, Foreman project, last 7 days, from fm cost / fm digest / ledger): 298.2M input-equivalent tokens for 349 finished tasks (~0.85M per task); cache reads 2,332M raw = 233M input-equivalent = 78% of all spend; 8,318 replies, so ~280k tokens of context re-read per reply; tool results filling context: Bash 46%, Read 44%; active time 30h11m: model 61%, Bash 38%; gate (fm check) median 51.5 s, p90 114 s over 330 runs; per finished task ~4.4 evidence runs and ~2.9 audit records. Learned digests on half of Bash's share at an 80% cut, and capsules on a quarter of Read's share at a 70% cut, shrink tool-result context by 0.46 x 0.5 x 0.8 + 0.44 x 0.25 x 0.7 = 18% + 8% = 26%. If tool results are ~60% of context, context shrinks ~15%: 233M x 0.15 = ~35M a week of cache reads. Halving ~8k of static instructions saves 4k x 8,318 replies x 0.1 = ~3.3M a week. Total ~38M a week (~13%). This multiplies with Rebirth rather than adding to it.

### Self-reorganising org: an orchestrator of orchestrators that rewrites its own org chart while the work runs [R2 · Revolution pass; build; value 5, L]
Demo: The user says: 'Do the whole queue overnight across foreman, volt and JARVIS.' The General, one Agent SDK process, starts 3 Foreman leads, one per project, each with builder lanes. At 01:12 four volt lanes hit the same schema error. The org chart in the pane animates: the General spawns a migration specialist and pauses the four lanes. The specialist fixes the shared cause once and posts the fact on the hive bus, and the lanes resume. At 02:40 the foreman lead is stuck on an L task, so the General splits it into a contract swarm under a sub-lead. At 03:05 two leads' footprints collide, so it merges them. In the morning there's an org timeline with 9 reorganisations, each with its measured effect ('throughput +40% after the split'). The moment: 'the team noticed a common blocker and reorganised itself around it, without me.' Multi-agent systems today fix their shape up front: a lead and N workers, or a static team. Here the org chart is state that a meta-orchestrator edits from live signals, the way a good engineering manager does. It spawns a specialist when several workers hit the same wall, merges sessions whose footprints collide, splits an overloaded lead into a sub-org, retires idle roles and moves budget to whoever is making progress. Every reorganisation is logged with its measured before-and-after effect, so the General learns which moves pay.
- mechanism: (1) `fm general`: an Agent SDK (Python claude_agent_sdk) process run as a systemd user unit, the way fm serve is. org.json holds nodes {id, role, model, project, task or milestone, worktree, budget, parent}. Each node is a Claude session (ClaudeSDKClient for leads; claude -p or native agent teams for workers) with the Foreman plugin loaded, so its hooks keep each node disciplined. (2) Signals every 30-60 s, rule-based with no LLM: per-node progress (ledger events, twin flips, evidence passes), token burn, idle time, thrash, shared failures (the same normalised signature in at least 3 nodes), footprint collisions (overlapping touched files) and queue pressure. (3) Moves, each a typed operation: SPAWN_SPECIALIST(sig), a new node with an on-the-fly agent definition (the SDK agents parameter) briefed with the shared failure and the nodes' capsules; PAUSE/RESUME through the mailbox and a Stop-hook wait; SPLIT(node), which runs the work compiler or contract swarm and adds a sub-lead; MERGE(a, b), which moves b's capsule into a and retires b; REBUDGET; RETIRE. A Sonnet 'chief of staff' call is made only when two moves compete, under a budget cap. (4) Hive bus: facts.jsonl per org. Confirmed hypotheses, environment quirks and resolved shared failures are appended, and PreToolUse in every node injects new facts once, deduplicated by hash, so no two agents rediscover the same thing. (5) Learning: each move logs its predicted effect and the measured one (progress rate over the 20 minutes after against the 20 before), and a posterior per move type gates future moves. (6) The cockpit shows org.json as a live tree with move markers (through fm ui --json); the user can message any node. Primitives: Agent SDK, claude -p, native teams (TeammateIdle/TaskCreated hooks), git worktrees, the round-1 mailbox (kept), Stop and PreToolUse hooks, a systemd user unit, ssh for JARVIS.
- first slice: `fm general --once` over local projects: org.json built from running fm run lanes and sessions, a shared-failure detector across lanes (the same normalised signature in 2+ lanes), and one move, SPAWN_SPECIALIST through claude -p with the cluster's failure packet. Affected lanes pause through a flag file the drive honours, and a hive facts.jsonl is injected through PreToolUse. Test: two toy lanes failing on the same missing env var lead to one specialist run, one broadcast fact, and both lanes resuming green.
- bar/numbers: Baseline used below (this machine, Foreman project, last 7 days, from fm cost / fm digest / ledger): 298.2M input-equivalent tokens for 349 finished tasks (~0.85M per task); cache reads 2,332M raw = 233M input-equivalent = 78% of all spend; 8,318 replies, so ~280k tokens of context re-read per reply; tool results filling context: Bash 46%, Read 44%; active time 30h11m: model 61%, Bash 38%; gate (fm check) median 51.5 s, p90 114 s over 330 runs; per finished task ~4.4 evidence runs and ~2.9 audit records. Round 1 found 47 general-purpose subagent launches at ~2.7M tokens each doing recurring jobs, and parallel lanes multiply rediscovery. If 4 lanes each spend ~0.5M thrashing on one shared cause, one specialist at ~0.6M saves ~1.4M per incident. fm run today caps lanes at 3 and never reallocates; the General moves idle or thrashing lane-minutes to unblocked work. It also replaces the user's hand-run 60-second JARVIS monitor (memory note) completely.

### Bug nose: names the bug before a file is read, and smells bug-shaped hunks at edit time, graded on every red [R2 · Revolution pass; build; value 5, L]
Demo: A gate goes red. Before the agent opens a file, a 3-line note arrives: 'Smells like a stale cache (mtime) in fmcore._load_cached, 0.62: 5 of the 7 nearest past bugs (T-0412, volt T-0233, ...) had this symptom shape. Confirm with one command: touch plugin/lib/fmcore.py && python3 plugin/tests/run.py -k test_state (passes if it is the stale cache).' The probe has already run in the background: confirmed. Another day, mid-edit, the band flashes: 'this hunk smells like an off-by-one seen 6 times in this file (slice end), 0.71.' The agent fixes it before running anything. `fm nose stats`: top-1 44%, top-3 71% over the last 90 reds. The moment: 'it named the bug and the probe that confirms it before reading the code, and it shows me its own track record.' A senior engineer's intuition is pattern recall over every bug they've seen, plus one cheap confirming experiment. Foreman's history is full of labelled examples (every red-to-green with real git trees, across every project), and it can make more by planting bugs overnight. The nose is a small local model (stdlib kNN and naive Bayes over symptom features) trained on real and planted bugs. In under 200 ms it returns a location, a cause class and a confirming probe; it runs that probe in the background, and it is graded continuously. The same model, applied to diff hunks, flags bug-shaped edits before any test runs.
- mechanism: (1) Corpus: (a) real episodes, the red-to-green pairs from evidence and check_run trees in every registered project, with causal hunks from prove --hunks where present. Symptom features: exception class, first error line tokens, frame function names, failing test ids, file stems. Cause class from a fixed taxonomy (off-by-one, None/empty, stale cache, order dependence, quoting, encoding, wrong branch, missing case, API misuse, environment), labelled once per episode by a Haiku child reading the fix diff. (b) Planted bugs: fm night applies stdlib ast mutations, each mapped to a cause class, to hot functions and records the failing tests and signatures. (c) Debugging episodes from transcripts outside Foreman tasks. (2) Model: kNN over TF-IDF of symptom tokens plus Jaccard over failing-test sets; naive Bayes for the cause class; the location is the neighbours' functions mapped onto current code; a probe template per class (stale cache: touch and rerun; order: reverse the order; None: assert at the producer). Calibrated with bucketed frequencies. (3) Online: on PostToolUseFailure or a red fm check, `fm nose` (<200 ms on a cached index) injects the note and runs the probe detached in the twin; the result arrives at the next PreToolUse. At edit time, PostToolUse computes hunk features (changed operators, boundary arithmetic, removed None handling, quoting) and matches them against hunks that later needed a fix, speaking only above a calibrated 0.6. (4) Grading: every red that gets fixed scores the nose's top 3 against the real fix hunks (the localisation gym, run on live data). Weights are refit nightly, `fm nose stats` shows precision, and a cause class whose top-3 rate falls below 30% goes quiet. Primitives: PostToolUseFailure/PostToolUse hooks, fm night, git trees, a Haiku labeller, the twin for background probes.
- first slice: `fm nose build` over every registered project's evidence trees (red-to-green pairs, symptom features, Haiku cause labels for up to 300 episodes under fm budget), `fm nose '<failure text>'` returning the top 3 locations, classes and probes, injection on PostToolUseFailure for gate and verify commands, and `fm nose stats` grading every later fix. Planted bugs and the edit-time smell come later.
- bar/numbers: Baseline used below (this machine, Foreman project, last 7 days, from fm cost / fm digest / ledger): 298.2M input-equivalent tokens for 349 finished tasks (~0.85M per task); cache reads 2,332M raw = 233M input-equivalent = 78% of all spend; 8,318 replies, so ~280k tokens of context re-read per reply; tool results filling context: Bash 46%, Read 44%; active time 30h11m: model 61%, Bash 38%; gate (fm check) median 51.5 s, p90 114 s over 330 runs; per finished task ~4.4 evidence runs and ~2.9 audit records. Round 1 found zero hypothesis events across 9 projects: debugging here is unaided. A red typically costs several read and grep turns before the first hypothesis, ~30k each in context re-reads plus outputs. If the top 3 hits 60% of the time and a hit saves 3 turns (~100k with outputs), that's ~60k per red. At ~1.5 debugging reds per task (TDD's planned reds excluded) over 349 tasks, ~31M a week. Each bug caught by the edit-time smell saves a full red, investigate, fix cycle (~0.3M).

### Consequence engine: executed answers to 'what breaks if...', computed before anyone asks [R2 · Revolution pass; build; value 5, L]
Demo: The user types: 'what breaks if _split returns a tuple instead of a list?' Nine seconds later: 'Run in a twin: 3 tests fail (test_ifs_split, test_heredoc_args, test_pipes_7). 2 call sites break silently with no test (check_node:211 calls .append; fmhooks:88 JSON-dumps it): dark code. Past escape T-0354 went through check_node:211. Your steer of 2026-09-30 (never rewrite heredoc args) anchors fmguard:1880. Safer: keep the list and add a tuple view.' When a plan names _split, the same card appears at focus without anyone asking. The moment: 'it didn't guess the blast radius, it ran the hypothetical, and it remembered the escape and my steer on that exact line.' Code knowledge graphs answer structural questions (who calls X) by inference. The consequence engine answers counterfactual questions by running them. It compiles a 'what if' into a concrete perturbation (type change, raise, return default, removed branch, dependency bump), applies it in a twin, runs the tests that reach it, and joins the result with the history graph (escapes, steers, lessons, premises). For hot symbols it precomputes cards nightly, so 'what will break if...' is answered at plan time, before anyone asks.
- mechanism: (1) Static layer: graphify's AST graph when present, else Python ast plus git grep. Runtime layer: real callers and argument and return shapes from the behaviour recorder (C). (2) History layer: a stdlib sqlite graph.db joining symbols to tasks, commits, escapes, steers, lessons and decisions, ingested from the ledger and briefs (node, edge, valid_from, valid_to). Facts are pinned to the hash of the symbol's source and expire when the code changes. (3) Perturbation compiler: a Sonnet child maps the question onto a small set of operators: ast transforms (wrap the return in a tuple/str/None, raise, return default, delete a branch, rename a parameter) and dependency pins in a venv copy. Each is applied mechanically in a twin worktree from B's pool, and tests are chosen by runtime reach. Output: failing tests, plus call sites that receive the perturbed value with no test touching them (from C's call graph). (4) Precompute: fm night runs the raise and return-default operators on the N most-edited symbols and caches a card per (symbol, tree). (5) Delivery: cards at focus and at the plan gate for symbols the plan names; an MCP tool `what_if(question)` for the model; `fm whatif '<question>'` for the user; a mod card. Primitives: CLI, MCP tool, git worktrees, a Sonnet child, fm night, graphify output, sqlite.
- first slice: `fm whatif SYMBOL --op raise|default|tuple` (Python, ast transform in a worktree, tests from fm check --affected plus grep reach), printing failing tests, static call sites with no covering test, and the escapes, steers and lessons the ledger has on that file range. The card appears at focus for symbols named in M/L plans (raise only). The natural-language front end and nightly precompute come next.
- bar/numbers: Baseline used below (this machine, Foreman project, last 7 days, from fm cost / fm digest / ledger): 298.2M input-equivalent tokens for 349 finished tasks (~0.85M per task); cache reads 2,332M raw = 233M input-equivalent = 78% of all spend; 8,318 replies, so ~280k tokens of context re-read per reply; tool results filling context: Bash 46%, Read 44%; active time 30h11m: model 61%, Bash 38%; gate (fm check) median 51.5 s, p90 114 s over 330 runs; per finished task ~4.4 evidence runs and ~2.9 audit records. Round 1 counted 26 escapes in Foreman, and 92 of 397 commits touch fmguard.py. If plan-time cards prevent a third of escapes on symbols with a known consequence, that's ~8 fewer escape-to-FIX cycles at ~0.85M each. Exploration: Bash and Read are 90% of what fills context. A 9-second card replaces ~5-10 exploratory calls (150-300k tokens) on M/L tasks, roughly a third of tasks: ~120 tasks x 0.2M = ~24M a week.

### Multiverse: risky decisions fork the session, the universes attack each other with tests, the survivor merges and losers donate [R2 · Revolution pass; build; value 4, L]
Demo: Mid-task, Foreman detects a contested decision ('cache the parse or rewrite the tokenizer'): the plan names two approaches and the consequence card shows a wide blast radius. The band splits into three universes (A cache, B rewrite, C hybrid). Each is a forked session with full context in its own worktree, Sonnet-priced and capped at 15 minutes. Each writes its implementation and its tests. Then cross-examination: every universe's new tests run against every other universe's code. 14 minutes later: 'Universe B wins: 31/31 of the union suite, including 4 tests written by A and C. A died to C's test_ifs_nested; C is 3x slower on the bench. Donated: 4 tests from A and C merged into B.' Any universe's diff can be opened. The moment: 'it built three implementations, had them try to break each other, and kept the survivor plus the tests the losers wrote.' Parallel attempts today are best-of-N, judged by an LLM or by the existing tests, and the losers are thrown away. Multiverse forks the live session, context intact, only at decisions the system itself detects as risky. The universes are adversaries: each one's tests are weapons against the others, so the union suite is stronger than any single agent's. Scoring is mechanical (union suite, behaviour diff, perf, diff size), and the losers' tests and facts are harvested into the winner.
- mechanism: (1) Fork triggers: the plan or an assistant message names 2+ approaches (a Haiku classifier on turn text from the Stop/PostToolUse hook); or the consequence card's blast radius passes a threshold; or a one-way-door decision; or an explicit `fm fork`. Standard autonomy asks once; full autonomy forks within budget. (2) Fork: commit-tree of the current tree, then N detached worktrees, each running `claude -p --resume <sid> --fork-session --model sonnet --max-budget-usd X` with the transcript copied into the worktree's project dir. The prompt: pursue option i, write tests that would expose a wrong approach, stop at green or at the cap. FOREMAN_STATE points at scratch per universe. (3) Cross-examination: collect each universe's new and changed test files and run the union suite on every tree, at no model cost. A test from universe j that fails on universe i must pass on j and must assert something in the brief or spec; a tool-less judge sees only the spec and the test (the prosecutor rule), and invalid tests are dropped. (4) Score: valid union-suite pass rate, unintended behaviour-diff count, perf when a criterion has one, and diff size. Ties go to a tool-less Opus judge. (5) Merge: after a checkpoint, the winner's tree goes into the real worktree (git read-tree -u); valid loser tests are added; loser dead ends go to the capsule and the hive. Universe refs are kept for 7 days. Primitives: claude -p --resume --fork-session, git worktrees, Stop/PostToolUse hooks, tool-less judge children, fm budget, the mod band.
- first slice: `fm fork ID --option A --option B` run one after the other with no automatic trigger: cross-examination of new test files across the two trees, the validity judge, a scoreboard, and valid loser tests donated as a patch. Test: a toy repo where option A passes its own tests but fails an edge-case test B wrote.
- bar/numbers: Baseline used below (this machine, Foreman project, last 7 days, from fm cost / fm digest / ledger): 298.2M input-equivalent tokens for 349 finished tasks (~0.85M per task); cache reads 2,332M raw = 233M input-equivalent = 78% of all spend; 8,318 replies, so ~280k tokens of context re-read per reply; tool results filling context: Bash 46%, Read 44%; active time 30h11m: model 61%, Bash 38%; gate (fm check) median 51.5 s, p90 114 s over 330 runs; per finished task ~4.4 evidence runs and ~2.9 audit records.
