Three Sonnet universes for 15 minutes cost about 3 x 1.5M Sonnet tokens. A wrong approach found late costs the whole task again; this week's most expensive tasks were 3-6M each. Forking only on ~5% of decisions (contested and high-blast) breaks even if about one redo is avoided per 3-4 forks. Each fork also adds ~4 valid tests a single agent didn't write.

### Intent compiler: a one-line wish becomes a red, executable spec, and ambiguity is resolved by picking between concrete outputs [R2 · Revolution pass; build; value 5, M]
Demo: The user types: 'make the guard stop blocking harmless IFS uses'. 40 seconds later: 'Compiled spec: 23 executable cases. 12 must be allowed, 9 must stay blocked (from today's fixtures), 2 are ambiguous. Pick: [1] a=$IFS; echo "$a": allow or block? [2] read -r x <<< "$IFS": allow or block?' The user taps two answers. The spec commits as a red test file plus a negative-space snapshot: the 5,000 replayed real commands must keep their verdicts, except the ones listed. The agent works unattended against it; done means the spec is green and the negative space is unchanged. The moment: 'it turned my sentence into a test suite and asked only the two questions whose answers change the code.' Agents turn intent into prose plans and ask clarifying questions in prose, which users answer vaguely. The intent compiler produces the spec as executable cases, plus a negative-space snapshot of what must not change, captured from current behaviour. It finds where plausible readings of the request disagree and asks only distinguishing questions: concrete inputs on which two readings give different outputs (active learning of intent). The answers become cases, and the spec is the contract every agent, universe and milestone proof is checked against.
- mechanism: (1) Trigger: UserPromptSubmit, after fm intake classifies a FEATURE/FIX/CLEAN request, runs `fm spec compile ID`. (2) Readings: 3 tool-less Sonnet children (fmideas.run_children) with different lenses (literal, generous, conservative). Each writes an executable oracle for the request over an input domain the grounding pack suggests: command strings for the guard, argv/stdout for a CLI, smoke journeys for a web UI, args/returns for a function. (3) Cases: inputs come from existing fixtures, real inputs from the replay and flight-recorder corpus, and generated neighbours. Every oracle labels every input. Where all agree, the input becomes a case. Where they disagree, inputs are ranked by how many readings they split and how natural they look, and the top 2-4 become AskUserQuestion multiple choice with the concrete outputs, or go to the user twin (M). (4) Negative space: current behaviour outside the request (fm replay verdicts, the behaviour recorder, smoke screenshots) is captured as 'must not change except as listed'. (5) Output: tests/spec_<task> (red) and a negative-space check command, both added as criteria with verify commands. The brief's Interpretation section is generated from the chosen cases. Primitives: UserPromptSubmit hook, tool-less claude -p children, AskUserQuestion, the fm replay corpus, fm check criteria. Builds on fm oracle.
- first slice: `fm spec compile ID` for the guard domain only: inputs are shell commands, oracles are allow/block predicates written by 3 children, and inputs come from the FORMS fixtures, the replay corpus and mutations. It writes a red spec test and the negative-space check (fm replay verdict diff) as criteria and asks distinguishing cases through AskUserQuestion. Measured on the next 5 guard tasks: steers per task before and after.
- bar/numbers: Baseline used below (this machine, Foreman project, last 7 days, from fm cost / fm digest / ledger): 298.2M input-equivalent tokens for 349 finished tasks (~0.85M per task); cache reads 2,332M raw = 233M input-equivalent = 78% of all spend; 8,318 replies, so ~280k tokens of context re-read per reply; tool results filling context: Bash 46%, Read 44%; active time 30h11m: model 61%, Bash 38%; gate (fm check) median 51.5 s, p90 114 s over 330 runs; per finished task ~4.4 evidence runs and ~2.9 audit records. This project has logged 33 steers. If half are misreadings a distinguishing question would have caught at intake, that's ~16 fewer mid-task reroutes, each wasting the work done in the wrong direction (often 0.3-1M). Compile cost: 3 Sonnet children at ~15k plus case labelling, ~60k per M/L task. The user answers 2 concrete multiple-choice questions instead of none (and a correction later) or a prose exchange.

### User twin: answers on your behalf where your own history proves it would, and pre-builds your next ask [R2 · Revolution pass; build; value 5, M]
Demo: 2 a.m., an unattended run needs a decision: one PR, or split out the migration? Instead of waiting until morning: 'The twin answered: one PR, skip the migration script. Agreement on scope questions 94% (n=63, leave-one-out on your real answers); precedents T-0312, T-0388.' The morning digest says: 'The twin answered 7 questions for you. Overrule any.' The user overrules one, and that retrains it. Then the user types 'now add the changelog line', and Foreman replies: 'Already drafted 4 minutes ago (predicted at 0.81 from your pattern after a FEATURE closes), verified, applying.' The moment: 'it learned to answer as me, proved it on my own past decisions, and only speaks for me where it has earned the right.' Agents either ask and block, or decide by their own taste and surprise the user. The twin is a model of this user built from every question Foreman asked them and what they answered, every approval, steer, correction and overrule. It is validated by leave-one-out backtests per decision category and may answer only in categories where its measured agreement clears a bar and the decision can be undone. Its answers are logged for review, and overrules lower its rate. The same model predicts the user's next request and builds it ahead of time in a lane. Earlier brainstorms skipped user modelling as unverifiable; the backtest is the verification.
- mechanism: (1) `fm twin build`: from transcripts, AskUserQuestion calls and results (question, options, choice), plan approvals and rejections, fm ask grants and denials, steers with the state they answered, corrections, and overrules of twin answers; also vetoes, taste and memory files. A Haiku classifier puts each item in a category (scope, naming, UX/copy, risk, process, priority). (2) Answering: a tool-less Sonnet child with BM25 retrieval of the 12 most similar past decisions and their real answers, plus taste. It returns an option, a confidence and its precedents. (3) Validation: a nightly leave-one-out backtest per category (each past decision answered with it and everything after it removed) gives an agreement rate and a Wilson lower bound. The twin has authority only in categories with a lower bound of at least 0.85, and only for two-way doors. Guard grants, core and destructive categories always stay with the human. (4) Wiring: PreToolUse on AskUserQuestion. If every question is in an authorised category under full or standing autonomy, it denies the call with the reason 'The user's twin answered: ... (agreement ..., precedents ...); logged for review', and the model proceeds. These are twin_answer events. `fm twin review` and a mod card take overrules, which become new corpus items and capture a reversal if work depended on the answer. (5) Pre-build: a Markov model of request types after task close, plus twin retrieval, predicts the next request. Above 0.7, and only for S-sized asks, a Haiku lane drafts it, and UserPromptSubmit matches the real request to the draft. Primitives: transcripts, PreToolUse deny-with-reason on AskUserQuestion, Sonnet/Haiku children, git worktree lanes, UserPromptSubmit hook, a foreman-ui digest card.
- first slice: `fm twin build` (AskUserQuestion pairs, approvals and steers from the last 90 days, categorised) and `fm twin backtest` (leave-one-out agreement per category with Wilson bounds). Then PreToolUse answering only in the best-scoring category under full autonomy, every answer logged, and `fm twin review` for overrules. Pre-build comes later.
- bar/numbers: Baseline used below (this machine, Foreman project, last 7 days, from fm cost / fm digest / ledger): 298.2M input-equivalent tokens for 349 finished tasks (~0.85M per task); cache reads 2,332M raw = 233M input-equivalent = 78% of all spend; 8,318 replies, so ~280k tokens of context re-read per reply; tool results filling context: Bash 46%, Read 44%; active time 30h11m: model 61%, Bash 38%; gate (fm check) median 51.5 s, p90 114 s over 330 runs; per finished task ~4.4 evidence runs and ~2.9 audit records. This week's digest has 6 costly/outward decisions waiting for the user, and AskUserQuestion is ~0% of active time because full autonomy decides silently. If the twin answers 60% of those at 90%+ measured agreement with citations, silent guesses become measured, reviewable ones and overnight waits go to zero. Pre-build: if 25% of follow-up requests are predicted, each saves a full intake, plan, edit cycle (minutes); misses cost a wasted Haiku draft (~50k).

### Mind view: a cockpit of the agent's live beliefs, which the user can edit, rewind and fork [R2 · Revolution pass; build; value 4, M]
Demo: The pane shows the agent's current mind instead of a log: 5 beliefs with confidence bars ('_split handles ${IFS}: 0.8, from reading fmguard:410'), 2 open hypotheses, the next 3 intended actions, and, before each tool call, what it expects to see. A result that contradicts an expectation turns that belief red (a surprise). The user spots a wrong belief ('the cache is per-process') and presses x. The agent's next tool result carries 'The user marked belief B3 false', and the plan visibly reroutes. Later the user scrubs back 40 minutes to where a wrong belief formed, flips it and presses f. A parallel universe starts from that moment with the correction, while the current run carries on. The moment: 'I'm editing what it believes, not what it types, and I can fork it from the moment it went wrong.' Every agent UI is a transcript: tool calls and prose in order. The mind view pulls a typed belief state out of the model's own stream (beliefs with sources and confidence, hypotheses, intentions, expectations), scores expectations against what actually happened, and makes that state interactive. Edit a belief and the edit reaches the model as a correction at the next tool boundary. Rewind to any past belief state and fork a session from it with one belief changed.
- mechanism: (1) Stenographer: the mod's turn.step streaming hook copies text and thinking chunks (or assistant text and tool inputs where thinking isn't exposed) into a per-step buffer. Every ~3 steps a budget-capped Haiku call (~2k in) extracts typed changes: beliefs {id, text, conf, source}, hypotheses, intent (next actions), and expect (what the next tool call should show). They're stored in an atom and in `fm mind put` (state/projects/<slug>/mind/<session>.jsonl, keyed by transcript uuid). (2) Surprise: PostToolUse compares the latest expectation with the result (a rule such as 'expected green, got exit 1', or a Haiku yes/no) and logs surprise events, which also feed fm surprise. (3) Edit: a Client surface (j/k to move, x to mark false, e to edit, ! to pin) posts ops; the ui.message hook runs `fm mind correct B3 '<text>'`, and the correction is delivered once through PreToolUse additionalContext. (4) Rewind and fork: belief states are versioned by transcript uuid. Pressing f on a past state calls the multiverse fork (K) with --resume <sid> --fork-session from that uuid (a copy of the transcript prefix) and the correction as its first message, in a worktree at that moment's tree (evidence trees or the twin's per-edit trees). (5) Rendering: a 'Mind' pane tab in register.tsx with Box/Text rows and Raster confidence bars; an SVG graph on desktop and mobile. Primitives: foreman-ui mod (turn.step tap, Client surface, ui.message, pane), a Haiku stenographer, PreToolUse injection, claude --resume --fork-session, git worktrees.
- first slice: The Haiku stenographer extracting beliefs and next intent every 3 steps into `fm mind` (file-backed), a Mind pane listing them with confidence and source, and x (mark false) delivered as a correction at the next PreToolUse. A mount test with mocked turn.step chunks, and a unit test that a correction is delivered exactly once. Rewind and fork in slice 2.
- bar/numbers: Baseline used below (this machine, Foreman project, last 7 days, from fm cost / fm digest / ledger): 298.2M input-equivalent tokens for 349 finished tasks (~0.85M per task); cache reads 2,332M raw = 233M input-equivalent = 78% of all spend; 8,318 replies, so ~280k tokens of context re-read per reply; tool results filling context: Bash 46%, Read 44%; active time 30h11m: model 61%, Bash 38%; gate (fm check) median 51.5 s, p90 114 s over 330 runs; per finished task ~4.4 evidence runs and ~2.9 audit records. Stenographer cost: ~2k in and 300 out per 3 steps; 8,318 replies a week is ~2,800 calls, ~6.4M Haiku tokens a week, or far less if it runs only while the pane is open. Value: a wrong belief caught at minute 5 instead of at review saves all the work built on it. Rewind-and-fork answers 'what if it had known X' without rerunning the session.

### Night school: every past mistake becomes a permanent test, and Foreman rewrites its own skills, hooks and agents overnight against them [R2 · Revolution pass; build; value 5, L]
Demo: Morning card: 'Night school: 14 new mistakes mined from yesterday (3 continue-nudges, 2 corrections, 1 false guard block, 1 escape, ...); the never-again suite is now 826 moments. 23 rewrites of 6 skills and hooks tried; 3 survive: the intake skill (-31% tokens, fixes 4 nudge moments), the drive wait message (fixes 3 idles), the guard IFS reader (fixes 1 false block, 0 new bypasses over 5,000 replays plus fuzz). Each has 0 regressions on all 826. [Merge 3] [Show proofs].' A week later, the warranty check: 'drive wait message: continue-nudges 9 -> 4 per 100 turn ends; warranty held.' The moment: 'it turned every mistake it ever made into a test, rewrote itself overnight to pass more of them, and proves no old mistake came back.' Self-improving agents today tune a prompt against a small, static benchmark, or get patched by hand from complaints. Night school makes the training set the agent's own complete error history. Every moment the user had to step in, every false block, escape, hook crash and idle becomes a permanent, cheap, one-turn counterfactual test, in a never-again suite that only grows. Rewrites of skills, hook constants, agent prompts and guard rules are bred nightly. To survive, one must fix new moments and regress none of the old. Each merges on the user's one yes and carries a warranty checked later on real sessions.
- mechanism: (1) Mistake miner: transcripts and the ledger yield labelled moments: a continue-nudge after a turn end, a correction or steer, a false block (blocked, then allowed by a grant or a rewrite), an escape (a later FIX naming a done task), a hook crash, a question asked twice, idling with work queued. Each moment is a transcript prefix uuid, a repo commit and an expected behaviour class (keep working, don't do X, allow, block). (2) Never-again suite: model moments become round-1 rewind tests (claude -p --resume --fork-session at the uuid with --plugin-dir <candidate>, and the hooks in dry-run mode, recording the next action without executing writes). Deterministic decisions (guard verdicts, Stop/drive decisions) are replayed free from the hook flight recorder, with no model. (3) Breeding: targets are ranked by moment counts. Operators: rewrite or ablate a skill or rule (fm evolve), tweak genome constants, patch a hook function (run sandboxed), and guard repair with a no-new-bypass proof (fuzz, replay, red team). Parents come from a Darwin-style archive. Children go through a cascade: free deterministic replays, then Haiku/Sonnet dry-runs on a sample of moments, then the full suite for survivors. (4) Certificate: 0 regressions on the full suite, at least 1 new moment fixed, a token delta and a predicted burden effect. The morning card's merge button submits the user's own prompt '/foreman:improve merge <branch>', so core consent stays human. (5) Warranty: the burden meter (nudges and corrections per 100 turns) before and after the merge; a breach produces a revert proposal. (6) Non-convergence: a region where patches keep arriving at a steady rate (the treadmill) switches from patching to a redesign proposal with the data. Primitives: transcripts, claude -p --resume --fork-session --plugin-dir, a FOREMAN_DRYRUN hook mode, git worktrees and branches, fm night (systemd), a foreman-ui card, fm budget caps.
- first slice: The miner for one kind of moment (continue-nudges after turn ends, last 60 days), materialised moment tests with dry-run capture at N=3, `fm school run --candidate <plugin-dir>` comparing the keep-working rate against HEAD, and a morning card with the result for one evolve candidate of the drive wait message.
- bar/numbers: Baseline used below (this machine, Foreman project, last 7 days, from fm cost / fm digest / ledger): 298.2M input-equivalent tokens for 349 finished tasks (~0.85M per task); cache reads 2,332M raw = 233M input-equivalent = 78% of all spend; 8,318 replies, so ~280k tokens of context re-read per reply; tool results filling context: Bash 46%, Read 44%; active time 30h11m: model 61%, Bash 38%; gate (fm check) median 51.5 s, p90 114 s over 330 runs; per finished task ~4.4 evidence runs and ~2.9 audit records. Round 1: 11 bench cases today, ~45 continue-nudges in 60 days, 92 of 397 commits touching the guard. A moment test is one turn (~30-60k Sonnet tokens with the cached prefix), about 40x cheaper than a full replay (1-3M). A full suite of 800 moments x 3 samples is ~100M Sonnet tokens, so it runs only on finalists; a sample of 100 moments per child is ~12M. Guard and Stop replays cost no tokens. If each merged change removes 3-5 nudges a week at ~2 minutes of the user's time each (plus idle hours), and 3 merge a week, the gains compound.

### Lessons compile to code: memory stops being prose the model must read and becomes checks that run [R2 · Revolution pass; build; value 4, M]
Demo: `fm lessons compile` runs overnight: '412 lessons -> 163 compiled. 71 regex/ast checks on diffs (T-0668's "getopt_long takes unique prefixes" becomes a check that any new long-option match in fmguard accepts prefixes); 38 PreToolUse rewrites ("grep here is ugrep, use -F for that pattern" now rewrites the command); 29 tests generated from the incident; 25 hook detectors from steers. Injected lesson text -70%.' The next day the agent writes a long-option match without prefixes, and the check fires at edit time with the lesson and the past incident. The moment: 'its lessons stopped being notes it might read and became checks it can't forget.' Every agent's memory is text, retrieved and hopefully obeyed. It costs tokens each time and fails silently when it isn't retrieved or is ignored. Here each lesson, steer and correction is compiled, where possible, into the cheapest form that can be enforced: a static diff check, a command rewrite, a regression test or an event detector. Each is backtested on history: it must fire on the original incident and stay quiet elsewhere. Only judgement that can't be compiled stays prose. Memory becomes executable, costs nothing until it fires, and can't be forgotten.
- mechanism: (1) Sources: task lessons (finish --lesson), steers, corrections, vetoes, memory files, and cross-project habits (failure signature plus the alternative that worked). (2) Compiler: per lesson, a Sonnet child given the originating incident (task diff, failure, command) picks a target: a diff check (regex or ast pattern over added lines in a file glob, run by fm check and by PostToolUse on Edit), a PreToolUse rewrite or refusal (command-shape match, then updatedInput or deny with the fix), a regression test, a detector in the round-1 event DSL, or prose as the fallback. (3) Backtest: checks and rewrites must fire on the incident and on at most 1% of the last 90 days of edits and commands; tests must be red at the incident's pre-fix tree and green at HEAD; detectors must fire at or before the corrected moment. Only passing compilations are installed, each with its provenance (lesson id, task). (4) Lifecycle: firings are counted; a check overridden 3 times drops back to prose; a lesson that compiled leaves tripwire injection. Primitives: PreToolUse (updatedInput/deny), PostToolUse on Edit, fm check gates, a Sonnet child, fm night.
- first slice: `fm lessons compile --kind diffcheck` for one project: a Sonnet child proposes regex/ast checks for lessons that have an originating diff; each is backtested against its incident and 90 days of commits; passing ones go into an fm check gate and the PostToolUse edit note; a report counts compiled versus prose. Test: a fixture lesson and incident commit; the check fires on that commit and on none of 50 others.
- bar/numbers: Baseline used below (this machine, Foreman project, last 7 days, from fm cost / fm digest / ledger): 298.2M input-equivalent tokens for 349 finished tasks (~0.85M per task); cache reads 2,332M raw = 233M input-equivalent = 78% of all spend; 8,318 replies, so ~280k tokens of context re-read per reply; tool results filling context: Bash 46%, Read 44%; active time 30h11m: model 61%, Bash 38%; gate (fm check) median 51.5 s, p90 114 s over 330 runs; per finished task ~4.4 evidence runs and ~2.9 audit records. Round 1 counted 53 tripwire lessons on fmguard.py, 97 on MASTER.md and 190 on CHANGELOG.md, injected on edits as text. The bigger value is re-learned mistakes: this week's 6 guard lessons (T-0585 to T-0668) each describe a class of bug. Compiled into checks, the next instance is caught at edit time instead of after an escape, and each escape-to-FIX cycle costs ~0.85M. If 40% of lessons compile, injected lesson text falls by about that share.

### Metric hunt: state a number, and Foreman runs an overnight evolutionary search on your code against it, with statistical proof [R2 · Revolution pass; build; value 4, M]
Demo: The user types: 'PERF: hook p95 under 80 ms.' Foreman reuses bench_hooks.py as the harness, measures the baseline (p95 210 ms, CI +/-6) and starts the hunt. Overnight: 140 experiments across 4 parallel lanes (profile-guided ideas: lazy imports, precompiled regexes, caching the state read, ...). Each is a small patch checked for correctness (gate green, behaviour diff empty) and measured with repeated runs, and the winners stack. Morning: 'p95 210 -> 74 ms. 9 changes kept, each with its own measured gain and CI; 131 rejected (31 changed behaviour, 100 showed no significant gain). One commit per kept change.' The moment: 'it ran 140 controlled experiments while I slept, and every change it kept comes with a confidence interval.' Agents handle 'make it faster, smaller, more accurate' by guessing a change or two and eyeballing a number. A metric hunt is a disciplined search loop, like AlphaEvolve or autoresearch but on the user's own repo: a harness that estimates noise, a profiler-guided idea generator, cheap parallel experiments in worktrees, a correctness gate (tests plus an empty behaviour diff), statistical acceptance (a bootstrap CI against the baseline), and greedy stacking with re-measurement. It works for any number: latency, memory, bundle size, test time, tokens, model accuracy, the guard's false-block rate.
- mechanism: (1) `fm hunt '<metric cmd>' --goal '<80' [--budget-usd --hours]`: the metric command prints a number; k baseline runs give the mean, CI and noise floor. (2) Ideas: profiler output (cProfile, py-spy if present, perf for native code, or the metric's own breakdown) plus a code pack go to a Sonnet child that proposes 10 ideas with patches. Later rounds are conditioned on what worked (an archive). (3) Experiments: each patch in a lane worktree. fm check --affected and the behaviour diff (C) must be clean. Measurement is k runs interleaved with the baseline (ABAB, to cancel drift); a patch is accepted if the bootstrap CI of its improvement excludes 0 and clears the noise floor. (4) Stacking: accepted patches are applied cumulatively on a hunt branch and the stack is re-measured; any patch whose gain disappears in combination is dropped. (5) 3-4 lanes, CPU-pinned with taskset to cut noise; the General (H) can allot more. Output: one commit per change with its numbers in the message, a research note, and a mod card with a live metric curve. Primitives: CLI, git worktrees, claude -p children (Sonnet/Haiku), fm check, the behaviour recorder, fm night/systemd, a foreman-ui card.
- first slice: `fm hunt` with a user-supplied metric command: one Sonnet child proposes 5 patches per round for 3 rounds; experiments run one at a time in one worktree; fm check is the gate; ABAB measurement with a bootstrap CI; greedy stacking; one commit per change on a hunt branch. Target: bench_hooks.py p95 on Foreman.
- bar/numbers: Baseline used below (this machine, Foreman project, last 7 days, from fm cost / fm digest / ledger): 298.2M input-equivalent tokens for 349 finished tasks (~0.85M per task); cache reads 2,332M raw = 233M input-equivalent = 78% of all spend; 8,318 replies, so ~280k tokens of context re-read per reply; tool results filling context: Bash 46%, Read 44%; active time 30h11m: model 61%, Bash 38%; gate (fm check) median 51.5 s, p90 114 s over 330 runs; per finished task ~4.4 evidence runs and ~2.9 audit records. 140 experiments at ~20k Sonnet tokens per patch is ~3M Sonnet tokens a night, against a day or two of an engineer's work. Foreman's own case: every check_run includes fm doctor (7.4-14.6 s) and bench_hooks (11.8 s). 330 check_runs at a median 51.5 s is 4.7 hours of gate time in the current ledger; a 30% faster gate saves ~1.4 hours over the same span, all on the agent's critical path.

### Branch prediction: Foreman predicts the agent's next slow read-only commands and runs them while the model is still thinking [R2 · Revolution pass; build; value 4, M]
Demo: The agent finishes an edit and spends 20 s composing its next message. Meanwhile Foreman has already run the test it is about to ask for, in the twin, on exactly this tree. The agent issues `python3 plugin/tests/run.py -k ifs`, and the result arrives in 40 ms marked '(run 12 s ago on this exact tree)'. The same happens for `git diff`, `fm doctor`, a cargo build and an ssh status check. By evening the band shows 'branch prediction: 41 hits, 37 min saved, 0 wrong results'. The moment: 'it runs my next command before I ask for it, and it's right most of the time.' CPUs hide latency by predicting branches and executing speculatively; agents just wait. Model thinking is 61% of active time and Bash 38%, run strictly one after the other. Branch prediction overlaps them. A predictor learned from the agent's own trajectories guesses its next slow read-only commands while the model thinks, runs them on the current tree, and serves the result instantly if the model asks for exactly that command on exactly that tree. Wrong guesses cost CPU, not tokens.
- mechanism: (1) Predictor: an n-gram model over normalised command shapes from transcripts (the guard's tokenizer), conditioned on the last tool call and Foreman's stage, plus fixed rules: after an Edit of f in a task with a verify command, that command; after a red test, `git diff` and a read of the top project frame; after `git status`, `git diff`. (2) Speculation: at PostToolUse (when the model starts thinking), up to k=2 predicted commands that the guard classifies as read-only (no write targets, no network unless allowlisted) run detached. Test runners run in the twin worktree, so caches in the real tree aren't touched. Output, exit code and timing are stored under (normalised command, tree hash, cwd, env id), and any edit invalidates them. (3) Hit: PreToolUse on Bash. On an exact match with the same tree hash, updatedInput rewrites the call to `fm spec take <key>`, which prints the stored output and exits with the stored code. Anything else runs normally. (4) Safety: the guard judges each speculative command when it launches, exactly as if the model had issued it; nothing that writes is ever speculated; hits are logged with their age. Primitives: PostToolUse and PreToolUse (updatedInput), background processes, the guard's write-target classifier, git tree hashes, the shadow twin's worktree.
- first slice: A rules-only predictor (the verify command after an Edit, git diff after git status, fm check after step evidence), speculative runs in a scratch worktree, the PreToolUse rewrite on an exact hit with the same tree hash, and hit rate and seconds saved logged into fm cost. Test: a fixture where an Edit is followed by its verify command gets an instant served result; a second edit between them invalidates it.
- bar/numbers: Baseline used below (this machine, Foreman project, last 7 days, from fm cost / fm digest / ledger): 298.2M input-equivalent tokens for 349 finished tasks (~0.85M per task); cache reads 2,332M raw = 233M input-equivalent = 78% of all spend; 8,318 replies, so ~280k tokens of context re-read per reply; tool results filling context: Bash 46%, Read 44%; active time 30h11m: model 61%, Bash 38%; gate (fm check) median 51.5 s, p90 114 s over 330 runs; per finished task ~4.4 evidence runs and ~2.9 audit records. Bash is 38% of 30h11m = ~11.5 h a week. If 40% of that time is predictable read-only or test commands and 60% of predictions hit, 0.4 x 0.6 x 11.5 h = ~2.8 h a week of wall time is removed, ~9% of active time, at zero token cost. Misses cost only background CPU.

### Ambient verification: a background test daemon whose results arrive only when something flips [R2 · New skills and tools; build; value 5, M]
Demo: User types 'FIX: quote parser drops escaped commas'. Claude edits parser.py. The Edit's tool result already carries one line: 'autotest: test_quote_escaped red->green; test_split_empty green->RED (parser.py:88)'. Claude never typed a test command all task. At finish, fm task finish reuses the daemon's last green run on the same tree as evidence. Senior engineer: 'it ran the right 4 tests on every save and only spoke when one flipped?' Coding agents spend a turn launching tests and another reading them. Here verification is ambient: every saved tree is tested in the background and only state flips reach the model, at the next tool boundary. 'Don't test every little change' stops being a trade-off: nothing is skipped, it just costs no turns.
- mechanism: PostToolUse additionalContext; a detached background process (same pattern as fm serve); fmcore.worktree_tree tree hashes; fm check same-tree pass reuse.
- first slice: `fm autotest start|stop|status`: fm impact-selected tests per poke on the live tree (rerun if an edit lands mid-run), flips-only injection.
- bar/numbers: Turn cost T = 36k input-equivalent tokens (fm cost, last 7 days: 297.1M / 8,285 replies). This repo has 1,008 explicit test runs (721 `run.py -k`, 287 `run.py -p`); each costs a launch turn plus usually a read turn = 2T = 72k. Removing half: 504 x 72k = 36M input-eq, about 12% of a week's 297M. Wall time: Bash is 39% of active time; tests now overlap model thinking instead of running serially.

### Symbol reads: a definition and its one-hop neighbourhood, never a whole file [R2 · New skills and tools; build; value 5, M]
Demo: Claude needs tests_for in fmmap.py. `fm sym tests_for` prints the 6-line body, the 2 names it calls resolved to file:line with signatures, and its 3 callers as one-liners: 180 tokens. Then `fm sym cmd_check --callers 2` draws a two-level caller tree through the 2,829-line fmcli.py without opening it. Reading becomes a walk over a symbol graph. Claude Code's Read is line ranges only and LSP needs a running server per language; this is stdlib and works on any repo instantly.
- mechanism: stdlib ast/re; rg (/usr/bin/rg) optional; PreToolUse additionalContext (a nudge, never a deny).
- first slice: Python-only `fm sym NAME [--callers N] [--refs]` with cache, plus the big-file Read nudge.
- bar/numbers: Read is 45% of tool-result context (fm cost). fmcli.py is 167,939 bytes = ~42k tokens whole; a typical function is ~40 lines = ~600 tokens. If 60% of whole-file Reads become symbol reads at 1/10 the size, Read context falls ~54%, i.e. ~24% of all tool-result tokens, and the saving compounds because every result is re-read each later turn (mean cache read 281k per reply).

### Failure records: any test runner's 400-line failure becomes 15 lines with the right source [R2 · New skills and tools; build; value 5, M]
Demo: pytest fails with 380 lines. Claude's tool result ends with: '2 failed: test_quote_escaped: 'a,b' != 'a\\,b' at parser.py:88 split_field (changed 4 min ago by this task); test_x: TypeError, first project frame utils.py:12' plus 5 source lines each. The raw log sits in a file if wanted. Runner-specific parsers turn failures into a fixed record (test id, minimal expected/actual diff, first project frame with source, touched-by-this-task flag) with zero model tokens.
- mechanism: stdlib re/difflib; fm quiet failure path; PostToolUse updatedToolOutput where Claude Code supports it (2.1.295+), else additionalContext.
- first slice: unittest, pytest and cargo parsers wired into fm quiet.
- bar/numbers: Assume 40% of the 1,008 runs are red = 400 failures at ~3k tokens each, compacted to ~400: 400 x 2.6k = 1.04M fresh tokens avoided, each re-read on ~30 later turns at 0.1 = ~3.1M input-eq. Plus ~1 fewer 'find the frame' turn per failure: 400 x T(36k) = 14.4M.

### Structural search and replace (comby/ast-grep style) with no binary dependency [R2 · New skills and tools; build; value 5, M]
Demo: 'Replace every open(p).read() with Path(p).read_text()'. `fm rewrite 'open(:[p]).read()' 'Path(:[p]).read_text()' --lang py` -> '31 matches in 12 files; diff saved; 2 skipped (in strings)'. --apply writes, autotest stays green. One turn instead of 31 Edits. Templates with holes that match balanced delimiters and respect strings and comments, in pure stdlib; Python results are re-parsed so a rewrite that breaks syntax is refused.
- mechanism: stdlib tokenize/ast/difflib; ledger event; fm check --affected after apply.
- first slice: Python + generic C-family, :[hole] only, dry-run/apply, ast check.
- bar/numbers: 31 Edit turns x T(36k) = 1.1M input-eq becomes 1-2 turns (~72k): ~94% saved on the step; ~10 minutes of edit turns becomes under 1.

### Do likewise: Foreman generalises the agent's own last two edits into a rule and finds the rest [R2 · New skills and tools; build; value 5, M]
Demo: Claude edits log.warn(f'x {a}') -> log.warning('x %s', a) in two files. After the second Edit the hook adds: 'Pattern seen twice: log.warn(f..{:[1]}..) -> log.warning(..%s.., :[1]). 23 more sites match: fm likewise --apply'. One command, all 25 done, and the 'fixed 4 of 7 copies' bug can't happen. Programming by example (Refazer/PROSE-style anti-unification) over the agent's own edits. The agent doesn't have to notice it is repeating itself.
- mechanism: Transcript JSONL, PostToolUse additionalContext, fmrewrite engine.
- first slice: Single-line Edit pairs only; suggest, never auto-apply.
- bar/numbers: Each avoided Edit is ~T (36k). A 25-site sweep found after 2 examples saves 23 x 36k = ~830k input-eq and ~8 minutes, and removes the sibling-site escape class.

### A debugger that survives between turns [R2 · New skills and tools; build; value 5, M]
Demo: `fm live test tests/test_parser.py::test_quote` runs the test and stops at the failing assertion, process kept alive. Next turn: `fm live eval 'field, sep, self.state'`. Next: `fm live up; fm live eval 'locals()'`. Then `fm live break parser.py:80 --rerun`. Claude debugs interactively across turns, like a human in pdb. Agents can't use interactive debuggers because every Bash call is a new process. A per-task debugger server keeps the frame alive across turns.
- mechanism: stdlib bdb/socket; same-user socket only; MCP tool wrapper; guard still sees every fm live call.
- first slice: Python post-mortem only: test, eval, where, up/down, stop.
- bar/numbers: A print-debugging cycle is edit + run + read = ~3T = 108k; a live eval is 1T = 36k with no re-import or fixture setup. Expect 3-5x fewer turns on 'what is the state here' questions.

### Corrections become enforced code rules, backtested on history [R2 · New skills and tools; build; value 5, M]
Demo: User says 'never call subprocess.run without a timeout'. Foreman proposes `fm rule add no-timeout --pattern 'subprocess.run(:[a])' --unless ':[a~timeout=]'` and backtests: 'would have flagged 7 past commits; 0 hits on the current tree after 2 exemptions'. From then on a diff adding such a call fails fm check with the user's own words, and PreToolUse warns before the Edit lands. Zero tokens, forever. Lessons stop being prose the model may skip (fm usage: 46 of 47 playbooks never read) and become diff-scoped lint rules the user wrote by speaking.
- mechanism: fmrewrite matcher; fm check gate; PreToolUse additionalContext; UserPromptSubmit correction detection already in fmhooks.
- first slice: add/list/check with backtest, Python and generic patterns, fm check integration.
- bar/numbers: A repeated mistake costs a correction plus rework, ~3T = 108k each time it recurs; the rule check costs 0 tokens and fires before the mistake lands.

### Work as one milestone, land as a stack of commits that each pass on their own [R2 · New skills and tools; build; value 5, M]
Demo: A batched milestone touched 14 files across 6 steps with one gate run at the end. `fm split T-0601` proposes 6 commits, one per step, from the hunks that appeared between that step's evidence trees; it builds each prefix in a scratch worktree and gates them in parallel in the background: '6/6 green alone; commit 4 needs commit 2 (import)'. One milestone of work, a bisectable history. Attribution comes from the evidence trees Foreman already records, not from the model re-reading the diff. Testing once and landing atomic verified commits stop being opposites.
- mechanism: git trees from fmcore.worktree_tree, git worktree, fm check, fm budget.
- first slice: Hunk-to-step attribution and a commit branch, gates run sequentially in one worktree.
- bar/numbers: Per-step commits today cost ~2T each plus a serial gate: 6 x 72k = ~430k input-eq; split costs ~1T and the gates run while other work proceeds: ~92% fewer tokens, gate time hidden.

### 48,000 log lines become 37 templates, and what's new since the good run [R2 · New skills and tools; build; value 5, M]
Demo: `fm logs /var/log/jarvis/api.log --since 1h --vs yesterday` -> 37 templates with counts; 'NEW: voice: decoder timeout after <*> ms x212 from 09:14:02; retrying <*> x1,904 (30x baseline)'. Claude reads 40 lines and knows when it started. Drain-style online template mining plus a template-level diff against a baseline window, stdlib only.

- first slice: Drain + new-template diff over a file or stdin.
- bar/numbers: 48k lines x ~15 tokens = 720k tokens (won't fit); grep-sampling takes ~5T = 180k and can miss the new template; fm logs output is ~1.5k tokens.

### Schema, stats and samples for any data file instead of reading its bytes [R2 · New skills and tools; build; value 5, S]
Demo: Claude is about to Read a 9 MB transcript. `fm peek s.jsonl` -> '8,285 records; type: assistant 59% / user 41%; message.content[]: text|tool_use|tool_result; usage.{input_tokens, cache_read_input_tokens}; 3 truncated samples'. Then `fm peek s.jsonl --where type=assistant --path message.usage.output_tokens --stats` -> p50/p95/sum. Inferred schema plus a tiny where/path/stats query, so data files are queried, not read.

- bar/numbers: A Read of a big JSON dumps up to ~2,000 lines = 25-60k tokens, re-read every later turn; peek is ~600 tokens.

### Mutation testing of the task's changed lines only, in seconds [R2 · New skills and tools; build; value 5, M]
Demo: `fm mutate T-0601` -> '12 mutants on your changed lines, 9 killed, 3 survived: parser.py:88 < -> <= (no test noticed); parser.py:91 dropped return None; ...'. The survivors go straight to fm-tester, which returns 2 tests that kill them. Mutation testing scoped to the diff and to the tests that execute it, so it costs ~40 s instead of hours. prove --hunks reverts whole hunks; this flips single operators inside them.
- mechanism: stdlib ast; fm cov or fm impact for test selection; git worktree.
- first slice: Python mutators, sequential runs, tests from fm impact.
- bar/numbers: Diff-scoped: 10-20 mutants x ~2 s covering tests = ~40 s, 0 tokens. Each survivor caught before finish avoids an escape, which costs a FIX task of ~20T = ~720k input-eq.

No coverage.py is installed; it uses Python 3.12+ sys.monitoring (this machine runs 3.14.7). ### Flake hunter: rate, polluting test and the dimension that makes it deterministic [R2 · New skills and tools; build; value 4, M]
Demo: `fm flake test_lane_merge` -> 'fails 3/50 in the suite, 0/50 alone; fails 50/50 when run after test_sync_mirror (found in 8 bisect runs over 212 earlier tests); leaked: env FOREMAN_STATE and /tmp/fm-lock'. Four minutes, root cause named. Three searches in one command: repetition rate, test-order pollution bisection, and environment perturbation (hash seed, TZ, locale, CPU count, random seed).

- bar/numbers: Polluter search over 212 tests: ceil(log2 212) = 8 runs, 0 tokens; manual flake debugging is ~10T = ~360k.

### One bisector over commits, dependency changes, config and evidence trees [R2 · New skills and tools; build; value 4, M]
Demo: After a `pip install -U`, `fm bisect --deps 'pytest -q tests/test_api.py'` -> 'lockfile changed 17 packages; culprit urllib3 2.2 -> 2.3; 5 runs'. `fm bisect --commits` over 140 commits takes 8 runs in a scratch worktree and names the Foreman task and lesson behind the bad commit. - bar/numbers: 140 commits: 8 runs; 17 deps with one culprit: ~5 runs; 0 tokens, versus ~2T per manual bisect step.

### Every thread's stack at the moment of a hang, in any language [R2 · New skills and tools; build; value 4, S]

The agent can't silently make things slower, and doesn't chase noise.

- bar/numbers: Reading every save on Opus costs T (36k) per save; event-driven Haiku costs a few thousand tokens per flip.

### Handoffs that must reproduce the failure before they're accepted [R2 · New skills and tools; build; value 4, M]
Demo: `fm handoff T-0601 --to jarvis` builds a bundle: git bundle of the branch, the uncommitted diff, brief, evidence and the failing check. On JARVIS `fm takeover bundle` restores the worktree and re-runs the failing check first: 'reproduced red, same failure signature'; only then does it focus the task. A bundle that doesn't reproduce is refused. A handoff carries state and proves it, instead of a prose paste that collapses in tmux.
- mechanism: git bundle, fm lane, fm fail signatures; ssh opt-in.
- first slice: Local bundle and takeover with the reproduce check.
- bar/numbers: Today: long send-keys paste (deprioritized per memory notes) + ~10T of re-exploration (~360k); takeover ~1T.

Structure-aware pre-resolution so the model only spends on semantic conflicts.
