### One conductor, several orchestrator sessions, one milestone [R2 · New skills and tools; build; value 4, M]
Demo: 'Ship the release milestone.' The main session splits it into 4 sub-milestones, starts each as a headless claude -p orchestrator in its own worktree (each may run its own subagents, which a subagent can't), reads only their 10-line ledger reports, pre-resolves merges with fm resolve, lands with fm split, and runs the full gate once. The user watches 4 lanes in the pane and one final verdict. Hierarchical orchestration: children are full orchestrators, not single-task lanes, and the conductor's context holds only reports.
- mechanism: claude -p sessions, git worktrees, fm budget caps, ledger.
- first slice: Skill driving fm run --parallel with sub-milestone contracts and report reading.
- bar/numbers: Conductor context holds ~4 x 10-line reports instead of 4 tasks' tool output; wall time divides by the number of independent parts (up to the host limit).

### Clean-room tasks: every task starts in a fresh ~30k context, handed off by a cache-priced fork instead of a /compact [R2 · Token economics; build; value 5, M]
Demo: The user queues 6 items and walks away. As T-0412 closes, the context gauge in the pane reads 381k. There is no 98-second 'Compacting…' spinner. The band flashes 'handoff 640 tok · fresh room' and the gauge drops to 31k in under 2 s. T-0413 starts from its brief, a context pack and a 640-token handoff, written by a model.fork of the old session. The economy chip says T-0413 cost $0.31, against $1.40 for the last task of the same tier. A senior engineer: 'it threw away 350k of context, got faster AND cheaper, and it still remembers the decision from two tasks ago?' Today each task inherits everything that came before it. Tasks start at a median of 380k tokens of context, yet the next task re-reads only 15% of the files the previous one read. T-0098 compacts only past 40% of the window, using a model summary pass. Clean rooms make the task (or milestone) the unit of context. The handoff needs no summarization pass: model.fork reads the old context at cache price and writes at most 700 tokens. Then /clear runs, and SessionStart(clear) seeds the brief, the pack and the handoff.
- mechanism: foreman-ui mod (mods/foreman-ui/hooks/register.tsx, the T-0098 task-close hook near line 282). (1) Call $.model.fork({prompt: HANDOFF}). The binary shows the fork reuses the main thread's cacheSafeParams, so the system prompt and messages are a cache hit; it cannot use tools, skips the cache write and skips the transcript. It asks for at most 700 tokens: standing requests, live decisions, facts the next queued task needs (given its title) and open threads. (2) Pipe the result into `fm task handoff ID --stdin`, which writes state/projects/<slug>/handoff.md. (3) $.prompt.submit({text:'/clear', asUser:true}); the mod already submits slash commands this way. (4) The existing fmhooks.session_start → session_context path for source=clear injects STATE, the next brief's Execution prompt, handoff.md (consumed once) and pack.md (from M12). Milestones use a rolling variant: the same handoff runs at a step boundary once context passes 80k. Guards: never mid-step (only after step_done or finish); never with unrecorded uncommitted work (checkpoint first); if the fork answers isAnswered:false, fall back to T-0098 compaction.
- first slice: Replace the freshAt compaction in register.tsx's task-close handler with the sequence model.fork handoff → `fm task handoff` → /clear → SessionStart(clear) injection. Add a setting cleanRoom: task|off. Tests: foreman-ui.test.tsx asserts the order (fork, write, clear) and that nothing clears mid-step; a Python test asserts session_context injects handoff.md once and then deletes it. Prove it with fm cost (start-of-task context, cost per finished task) for 2 days before and after.
- bar/numbers: Measured (30 days, 47.2k main calls): median start-of-task context 380k. Mean Opus context per call 335k. 83% of main calls run above 100k and 54% above 300k. Context grows by a mean of 2.27k tokens per call. There were 95 auto-compactions at a median 687k. Consecutive tasks share 15% of read files on average (median 0). Alone (calls per task unchanged at 48): mean context = 27k seed + 2.27k × 48/2 = 81.5k. Cache reads go from 48 × 0.05 × 335k = 804k to 196k input-equivalent, and writes from 108k to 119k (a 10k seed write per room). E per finished task: 1,262k → 676k (−46%). Handoff fork: 380k × $0.2/M + 700 × $20/M = $0.09. A compaction costs ≈ 687k read + ~15k summary out ≈ $0.44, plus 98 s. Latency: median 3.0 s per call below 100k vs 4.0 s at 300-500k (−25%). In the stack: C 335k → 46k.

### Distill at the source: PreToolUse rewrites Read, Grep and Bash into their distilled forms before they run [R2 · Token economics; build; value 5, M]
Demo: Claude calls Read on plugin/lib/fmcli.py (2,829 lines). What lands in context is 1.9k tokens: the outline with line ranges of every def, 60 lines around the symbols the active step names, and 'full: fm peek fmcli.py 400-520'. Next, `python3 -m unittest discover` comes back as one line: '412 passed, 1 failed: test_batch_merge (AssertionError 3 != 4, fmcore.py:1391)'. The 14k-token log never existed. `git diff` returns --stat plus only the hunks inside the task's scope. 'Wait — the agent asked for the file and got exactly the part it needed, with zero model calls?' Round 1's firewall rewrote output after the fact (PostToolUse updatedToolOutput). Rewriting the input (PreToolUse updatedInput) is cheaper and more exact. The command itself changes to produce the distilled form (outline + spans, per-file counts + top hits, a summarizing test runner, scoped diffs). Raw bytes are never produced, stashed or read, and the tools also run faster. An output-side digest stays only for shapes that have no input rewrite.
- mechanism: hooks.json: the sync PreToolUse matcher (today Bash|Write|Edit|MultiEdit|NotebookEdit|Agent|Task) gains Read|Grep|Glob, with a 2 s budget. fmhooks._pre_tool_use gets a distiller table keyed by command shape; fmguard's shell parser already tokenizes Bash. Rules: (a) Read with no offset on a file over 400 lines → updatedInput {offset, limit} for the spans the active step, the facts cache (M7) and the outline ranking point to, plus additionalContext with fmmap.outline(path). (b) Test runners → wrapped through `fm quiet --test` (fm quiet exists), printing the summary, failure names and first/last frames. (c) grep -r/rg → `fm peek --grep`: per-file counts plus the 30 best hits ranked by task scope. (d) git diff → --stat plus scoped hunks. (e) cat/sed -n over a whole big file → same as Read. Escape hatches: a trailing `# raw` comment, or a Read with explicit offset/limit, is never rewritten. Exit status is preserved, so auto-evidence for criterion verify commands still sees real exit codes. Haiku 5.5 digests only unknown shapes over 20k chars, through a separate sync PostToolUse(Bash) using updatedToolOutput (in the 2.1.295 binary).
- first slice: Ship the Read rule (no offset, over 400 lines → outline-ranked spans + outline context) and test-runner wrapping via fm quiet. Add one fixture per shape in plugin/tests/test_hooks.py asserting the rewritten input and preserved exit codes. Compare tool-result tokens with fm cost before and after.
- bar/numbers: Measured residency (token-turns: what a tool result costs while it sits in context): Bash file reads 15% of all context residency, Read tool 10%, Bash searches 7%, other Bash 4.5%, tests 1.8%. Tool results total ≈43%. Bash file reads average 741 tokens and stay resident for a mean 185 later calls. Assumption: distilled forms cut tool-result tokens by 60% (an outline plus spans is typically 10-20% of a 1k+ line file; test summaries ≈5%). Alone at today's context sizes that is ≈ −0.6 × 43% ≈ −26% of cache-read cost, ≈ −17% of E. In the stack it is part of g 2.27k → 1.6k per call, together with M3 and M13.

### Delta re-reads: a file the model already saw comes back as 'unchanged' or as only its diff [R2 · Token economics; build; value 4, S]
Demo: Forty calls after reading fmguard.py:300-420, Claude reads it again. The result is one line: 'unchanged since call #12 (blob 3f2a…)'. After its own edit, a re-read returns only the 9 changed lines with ±3 lines of context. The pane counter: 're-reads served from memory: 31 · 48k tokens not re-sent'. 'It knows what is already in my context?' Claude Code notes when a file changed since it was last read, but a re-read still resends the bytes. Foreman knows, per session, which spans of which blob are already in context, and serves the delta.
- mechanism: PostToolUse records each read (Read, or Bash cat/sed -n/head/tail of one file) into state/sessions/<sid>/seen.json as {path: [(blob_sha, start, end, call_no)]}. A sync PreToolUse on the same shapes then rewrites: if the requested range is covered by an entry with the same `git hash-object` blob → updatedInput to `fm peek --unchanged PATH --since N` (one line). If the blob changed → `fm peek --diff PATH --from <old blob> --range a-b`, a git diff --no-index of the stored blob against the working file, limited to the range. Stored blobs live as git objects (hash-object -w) and are pruned by tidy. seen.json is cleared at SessionStart(clear|compact), since the context no longer holds those bytes. Edit-tool safety: a path targeted by the Edit tool since the last read always gets a real read, so Edit's freshness check is never starved.
- first slice: Ship seen.json bookkeeping plus the 'unchanged' rewrite for an identical blob and a contained range (fm peek --unchanged). Tests cover containment, invalidation after a write, and clearing at SessionStart(clear|compact).
- bar/numbers: Measured: 12,409 file-read calls in main sessions. 50% re-read a path already read in that session. 31% of read characters (16.3M of 53.1M) were re-reads with no Edit-tool edit in between. Assumption: 70% of those are servable as unchanged or as a delta, so file-read tokens fall ≈22%. Alone: file reads are 25% of residency, so cache reads fall ≈5.5%. In the stack it is folded into g (2.27k → 1.6k) with M2 and M13. It is smaller inside clean rooms (fewer stale re-reads per session) and counted only once there.

### Lean, self-silencing Foreman: rules by stage capsule, notes only when they change, plugins pruned per stage [R2 · Token economics; build; value 3, M]
Demo: At session start the band shows 'prefix 19.8k (was 28.7k)'. While Claude edits, no 'Next:' line is injected for 14 turns, because nothing changed. When a test turns red, the one-line note appears. /foreman:status shows 'Foreman's own tokens this task: 1.1k'. 'The discipline layer costs less than the date line?' Foreman's injected context is a fixed tax that every call re-reads. Measured today it is small (hook notes are 0.5% of residency) because contexts are huge. After clean rooms, the fixed prefix becomes about half of a 46k context, so every 1k of prefix is worth ≈2% of cost. Capsules, silence-unless-changed and per-stage plugin pruning make the prefix adaptive.
- mechanism: (1) Split plugin/rules/foreman.md (6.4k chars) into a ~1.2k-char core plus stage capsules (intake, execute, verify, close). Only the current stage's capsule is injected (fm next already knows the stage), and only at a stage change. (2) Keep a hash of the last injected Next/state note per session in gate.json; UserPromptSubmit injects only on change. (3) Per-stage plugin set: at a clean-room boundary (M1), write a session settings overlay that drops skills and agents unused in that stage. Measured: the skill listing is 3.2k tokens re-sent 1,051 times, and ponytail's 5.7k-char SessionStart text rides every subagent; keep ponytail for CLEAN and execute only. (4) fm doctor adds a prefix-budget check: fail if Foreman's share of the first context exceeds 2.5k tokens.
- first slice: Ship the capsule split of rules/foreman.md, silence-unless-changed for the per-turn Next note, and the fm doctor prefix-budget check. Measure first-context tokens before and after with fm cost.
- bar/numbers: Measured: first-context median 28.7k tokens; skill listing 3.2k tokens per listing; rules + output style 7.8k chars; Foreman hook notes added 0.13M tokens in 30 days. Target prefix 21k (−7.7k: rules core −1.6k, skill/agent pruning −3k, ponytail outside CLEAN/execute −1.5k, misc −1.6k). At today's 335k mean context that is only −2.3% of cache reads. In the stack (46k mean context) it is −7.7k/46k ≈ −17% of cache reads, and it shrinks every room's seed write.

### Cache-shaped sessions: no cache busts — model and effort changes land at boundaries, and idle sessions rotate instead of rewriting [R2 · Token economics; build; value 3, S]
Demo: The user steps away for 75 minutes. Today the first call after they return rewrites 380k tokens at the 1-hour cache price ($3.04). With cache-shaped sessions the band says 'idle 56 min → fresh room queued', and the next call writes 30k ($0.24). A /model switch typed mid-step is deferred: 'applies at the next step boundary (saves a 340k rewrite)'. 'It's managing the prompt cache for me?' Prompt caching is invisible to Claude Code users and agents, and nobody schedules work around it. Foreman knows the cache's shape (one prefix per model, a 1-hour TTL in this setup) and knows its own boundaries. So it moves expensive transitions to cheap moments.
- mechanism: foreman-ui mod: (1) turn.step: a model or effort change requested mid-step while context is over 60k is deferred to the next step or clean-room boundary (the step comes from fm ui --json). The router (M15) only switches below that size. (2) A clock.after timer: an open task idle for 55 min with context over 100k → run the clean-room handoff (M1) now, so the next call writes a 30k room, not a 380k rewrite. (3) Foreman injections are append-only (SessionStart material is never rewritten mid-session); plugin reloads are deferred to boundaries (the mod's UI-reload stop exists). (4) Drive waits on background jobs (T-0145) expected to run past 55 min trigger the room first. fm cost gains a 'busts' line: mid-session writes over 30k, with their cause.
- first slice: Ship turn.step deferral of mid-step model/effort changes, the 55-minute idle clean-room trigger, and the fm cost busts line.
- bar/numbers: Measured: cache writes are 16.6% of Opus 5.5 main spend (all 1-hour TTL, $8/M). Of 139.8M written tokens: 18% are session-start writes, 61% normal suffix growth, and 21% mid-session rewrites over 30k. Of those rewrites, 10% come from idle past the TTL, 7% from mid-session model switches, and ~3% from plugin, agent-listing or compaction changes. Alone, removing them saves 0.21 × 16.6% ≈ 3.5% of spend. In the stack, W 108k → 42.8k per task comes mostly from M1. This mechanism keeps busts from returning once M15 switches models inside a conversation.

### Ask, don't read: a cheap reader answers code questions with citations, and the expensive model never opens the file [R2 · Token economics; build; value 5, M]
Demo: Claude needs to know how the guard decides that a shell write needs an active task. Today it would grep → sed → grep → sed (the measured top Bash 4-gram). Instead it calls Ask('where does fmguard decide a shell write needs an active task, and which forms are exempt?'). Four seconds later, 280 tokens come back: fmguard.py:1412-1460 `_brief_write`, the three exempt forms with line refs, and 'confidence high · read 3 spans / 2.1k lines'. Haiku 5.5 read 24k tokens; Opus read 280. 'The expensive model is now a manager who asks questions, and the answers come with line numbers?' Coding agents explore by pouring raw code into the expensive model's context. Here exploration is a tool backed by a cheap model plus zero-token tools. Haiku 5.5 costs $0.10/M input, $0.50/M output and has a 1M window; it was verified available on this machine ($0.0016 for an 8.7k-token headless call). The expensive model reads only cited answers. Answers are checkable: every claim cites path:line, and Foreman checks the cited lines exist in the current blob before returning them.
- mechanism: An `Ask` tool, registered by the foreman-ui mod ($.tool.register) or by Foreman's MCP server (fmmcp.py) when the mod is absent: ask(question, scope?). It runs a headless `claude -p --model claude-haiku-5-5 --tools Read,Grep,Glob` child, launched like fmideas.run_child, with a byte-identical system prompt so it hits the cache (M18). Scope is the active task's scope plus fmmap-related files. Budget: ≤60k tokens in (staying under Haiku's 100k long-prompt price step), ≤400 out, JSON schema {answer, citations:[path:start-end], unsure}. Post-check: every citation must resolve in the current file; uncited sentences are dropped; unsure=true returns the cited spans raw (≤1.5k tokens) instead of a summary. Every answer is written to the facts cache (M7). Nudge: PreToolUse adds 'Ask(…) answers this for ~300 tokens' to the third consecutive read/search call of a burst. In strict mode (chosen by M17), exploratory main-thread reads are rewritten into Ask.
- first slice: Ship `fm ask-code "<question>" [--scope PATHS]` (headless Haiku child, JSON schema, citation verifier) plus the PreToolUse nudge on the third read of a burst. Benchmark by replaying 20 real exploration bursts from transcripts: the answer's citations must hit the function the session later edited in at least 80% of cases.
- bar/numbers: Measured: 38% of Opus calls are read/search only (17.3k calls). They come in bursts with a mean length of 2.31, and 60% sit in bursts of 3 or more. That is 18.2 read/search Opus calls per finished task. With one Ask per burst, plus orientation covered by packs and facts, 18.2 → 5.5 calls (−12.7 per task). Each Ask is Haiku reading ~25k ($0.0025) plus 400 out ($0.0002) ≈ $0.003, so 5.5 per task ≈ $0.016. Alone at today's context: −12.7 × 26.3k = −334k E per task (−26%). Wall-clock: one 4-6 s Ask replaces 2.3 round-trips that average 9.5 s each.

### A verified-facts cache keyed by blob hash, shared by every session, lane and subagent [R2 · Token economics; build; value 4, M]
Demo: An hour ago a JARVIS lane asked 'what does fmcore.batched do with criteria prefixes?'. Now a reviewer asks the same thing in different words, and the answer returns in 40 ms, marked 'cached · verified against blob 9c1e'. The user then edits fmcore.py:1377. The next ask about it says 'stale: a cited span changed — re-deriving' and takes 3 s. The pane: 'facts served today: 212 · 0 tokens'. 'Every agent on the machine shares a memory that knows when it's wrong?' Every session and subagent re-derives the same facts about the same files. In 30 days fmguard.py was read in 129 different transcripts, fmcore.py in 121, fmcli.py in 117 and fmhooks.py in 111. Facts with citations become a content-addressed cache: a fact is valid exactly while the bytes of its cited spans are unchanged. There is no TTL and no guessing.
- mechanism: state/facts/<repo-id>.sqlite (stdlib sqlite3), with rows {question_norm, answer, citations[(path, start, end, span_sha1)], model, created, hits}. Lookup: BM25 (fmrecall has it) over normalized questions, restricted to facts whose cited spans still hash the same at those lines (rehashing a few spans is milliseconds; a moved span is found by searching the file for the stored sha). Writers: Ask (M6), fm-recon reports, reviewers' verified findings, and handoffs (M1) when they state code facts with citations. Readers: Ask (first), the pack builder (M12) and review packs (M19). Lanes and worktrees share facts by repo but verify against their own bytes. The data stays local; `fm facts stats` shows hits.
- first slice: Ship the sqlite table, span-hash validation, `fm facts get/put/stats`, and Ask reading and writing it. Test: a fact goes stale when a cited line changes and stays valid when an unrelated line changes or the span merely moves.
- bar/numbers: Measured: 3,805 distinct paths read in 30 days; 808 read in 2+ transcripts and 221 in 5+; the four Foreman core files in 111-129 transcripts each. Assumed hit rate after a week: 35% of Asks, dominated by hot files. That saves 35% of Ask cost and latency (≈$0.006 and ~4 s per task). More importantly, it lets packs seed clean rooms with verified facts so orientation bursts disappear. That effect is counted inside M6's 18.2 → 5.5, not again here.

### Compiled skills: repeated tool-call sequences mined from transcripts become zero-token macros, triggered by their first step [R2 · Token economics; build; value 4, L]
Demo: fm night reports 'compiled 6 macros from 1,518 transcripts'. Next day Claude starts the familiar volt dance: `cargo build -q`, then `./target/debug/voltc-bootstrap check tests/diag/x.volt`, then grep the output. After the first call, PreToolUse says 'macro volt-diag-check matched (seen 136× in 9 sessions): ran all 3 steps' and returns one distilled result. 'It watched itself work for a month and turned its habits into tools?' fm repeats today finds repeated commands per brief and suggests a prose playbook. Compiled skills are executable. A miner turns frequent call n-grams with a stable argument dataflow into parameterized scripts. Each script is verified by replaying past instances and bound to a PreToolUse trigger on its first step, so the model never has to remember it exists.
- mechanism: fmrepeats.py extended. (1) Mine: normalized call shapes (tool + command head + argument roles) from the project's transcripts; n-grams of length 2-5 with ≥10 occurrences across ≥3 sessions; a dataflow check that later arguments derive from earlier arguments or outputs (regex captures). (2) Compile: state/projects/<slug>/macros/<name>.sh with parameters, plus a distiller for the combined output (the M2 table). (3) Verify: read-only macros replay their last 5 instances in a scratch worktree, and outcomes are diffed. Only read-only and Foreman-bookkeeping macros auto-enable; a writing macro needs one grant (fm ask … core). (4) Bind: PreToolUse matches the first call's exact shape and rewrites it (updatedInput) to run the macro, with additionalContext naming the macro and the `# raw` opt-out. (5) Retire: a macro whose result the model then redoes by hand (seen in the next calls) is disabled.
- first slice: Ship the miner report (`fm repeats --ngrams`) plus compile, verify and bind for read-only macros, behind a setting. Test: mining a synthetic transcript yields the macro with the right parameters, and the trigger rewrites only exact-shape first calls.
- bar/numbers: Measured: 29.7k tool calls (52% of all) sit inside 3-grams that recur 10+ times across 3+ sessions. The top Bash 4-gram, grep>sed>grep>sed, occurred 315× in 47 sessions. Foreman pairs: task set→set 67×, evidence→evidence 62×, evidence→finish 30× (Foreman chains are M9's job). Misc Bash (builds, runs, scripted checks) is 25.5% of Opus calls, 12.2 per task. Assumption: macros absorb 30% of those, −3.7 calls per task. Alone: −3.7 × 26.3k = −97k E per task (−8%).

### Zero-call bookkeeping: Foreman infers its own state, and close-out is one call that returns one verdict [R2 · Token economics; build; value 4, M]
Demo: Claude finishes the last edit of T-0731 and types one command: `fm close T-0731`. In the pane five jobs run at once: every criterion's verify command, fm check, prove --hunks, audit prep, and a Sonnet reviewer on a review pack. 70 seconds later a 9-line verdict lands: 'AC1 ✓ AC2 ✓ gates ✓ 14/14 hunks caught · review: 1 edge finding verified at fmcore.py:212'. Claude fixes it, and the task closes. During the task it never typed `fm task step` or `fm task evidence`; steps ticked themselves when their checks went green. 'The paperwork does itself?' Foreman's discipline currently costs model calls. 7.6% of Opus calls are fm bookkeeping (3.6 per finished task), and close-out is 9.6 calls after the last edit. Hooks already see every tool result, so inference plus one fan-out close command takes the model out of the bookkeeping without weakening any gate.
- mechanism: (1) PostToolUse already sees exit codes, and auto-evidence exists for criteria. Extend it to steps: when a command equal to a step's declared check exits 0 on a tree where the step's scope files changed, record evidence and step_done, and inject a one-line note. (2) `fm close ID` in fmcli runs the criteria verify commands, fm check (reused on the same tree), prove --hunks, audit prep and a headless reviewer (fmideas.run_child with an M19 review pack) concurrently in a thread pool, and prints a verdict of at most 12 lines. In autonomy full, when every gate is green and review is clean, it calls `fm task finish` itself with the audit text; otherwise the model judges once. (3) Steer notes are logged from UserPromptSubmit, which already classifies them.
- first slice: Ship `fm close ID` (parallel verify/check/prove/audit-prep, one-verdict output) and step auto-completion from a passing declared check, tested on a toy project in plugin/tests.
- bar/numbers: Measured: fm bookkeeping is 7.6% of Opus calls (3.6 per task). Close-out after the last edit averages 9.6 calls per task. fm commands are also 23.6 h (12%) of foreground Bash time, because finish runs gates serially. Effect: fm calls 3.6 → 1.0 (−2.6 per task); the test and review calls in the tail are counted under M10 and M19. Alone: −2.6 × 26.3k = −68k E per task (−5.4%). Wall-clock: the 1.4 min per task of serial gates becomes the longest single job.

### The model never runs tests during a change: a zero-token test daemon runs the affected ones after every edit and whispers the verdict [R2 · Token economics; build; value 5, M]
Demo: Claude edits fmcore.py. Before its next tool call returns, a one-line note arrives: 'daemon: 7 tests reach the changed lines · 6 ✓ 1 ✗ test_batch_merge — AssertionError 3 != 4 (fmcore.py:1391) · tree d23e'. Claude fixes it; the next note says '7 ✓'. It never typed a test command until close-out, and a strip of green and red ticks per edit fills the pane. 'Tests run themselves, in the background, only the ones that matter, and the model never waits or pays for them?' Today the expensive model types every test command and reads every result: 4.1 Opus test calls per finished task, and 6,923 foreground test runs that took 47.6 h of wall-clock in 30 days. A daemon that maps changed lines to tests from observed coverage runs them while the model thinks. Only a verdict line enters the context.
- mechanism: A per-project background process, `fm daemon tests`, started at SessionStart and managed like fm watch. PostToolUse(Edit/Write, and Bash-touched files from _bash_touches) feeds it through a FIFO. It maps changed hunks to tests using an observed coverage map (round-1 'Observed test-sensitivity map'; coverage or settrace refreshed on every full gate run), falling back to fmmap.tests_for name stems. It debounces 1.5 s and runs the affected tests in a detached scratch worktree with the dirty files copied in, so concurrent edits never race it. It writes the verdict plus the tree id (fmcore.worktree_tree) as check_run events, so fm close and why-red can reuse them. The next hook injects the verdict as additionalContext: at most 200 chars, failures carrying the first assertion and frame. Opus may still run exploratory tests, but is nudged not to rerun what the daemon covers.
- first slice: Ship `fm daemon tests` for Python projects (unittest/pytest), with name-stem mapping first and the coverage map next, and verdict injection via the existing PostToolUse. Test: an edit to a covered function yields a verdict line naming the failing test within one hook cycle.
- bar/numbers: Measured: 4.1 Opus test calls per finished task (8.8% of Opus calls). 6,923 foreground test runs, 47.6 h wall-clock (24% of foreground Bash), median 1.1 s, p90 87 s, plus 23.6 h of gates inside fm commands. Effect: Opus test calls per task 4.2 → 0.5 (−3.7; the rest is the close-out verdict). Assumption: 70% of test wall-clock is hidden behind model time. Alone: −3.7 × 26.3k = −97k E per task (−7.7%), and −2.0 min of 19.7 min wall-clock per task.

### Milestones: related items fused into one task — one read, one plan, red tests in bulk, a fast oracle per change, then full gates, proof and one review at the end [R2 · Token economics; build; value 5, L]
Demo: The inbox has 7 items, and 3 touch fmguard.py's heredoc parsing. The user types 'milestone it'. Foreman answers: 'M-0012 Guard heredoc forms = T-0811, T-0815, T-0819 (shared: fmguard.py, test_guard_pipes.py; ordered by dependency)'. Claude writes all 3 red tests in one step, each proven red on the start tree. It makes 3 changes, with a 2-second daemon verdict after each. Then there is one close: full gates, 23/23 hunks caught by the full suite, and one review of the union diff ('interaction: change 2 shadows change 1's quote handling — verified'). Three commits land together. Elapsed: 26 min, against 3 × 21 min. Tokens: 0.9M, against 3 × 1.3M. 'It batched my tickets and the verification got stronger, not weaker?' Today every item pays the full fixed cost: orientation (8.8 calls), close-out (9.6 calls), full gates and a reviewer. That holds even when three items edit the same function on the same afternoon: 39% of finished tasks share a code file with one finished in the previous 24 h, and 43% fall into same-file clusters of 3 or more. fm batch (T-0257) only merges briefs. A milestone changes the order of operations: plan and read once, write tests first in bulk, use cheap oracles per change, and run the expensive gates once, on the tree that actually ships.
- mechanism: `fm milestone [--auto] [IDs]`. Clusters come from fmrelate, touched files and fmmap co-change; order is topological over #T- dependencies; L items are never auto-merged. It builds a host brief with these steps. S1: all members' red tests, each proven red on the start tree. S2..Sk: one change per member; the daemon's affected-test oracle (M10) must be green and the tree is snapshotted. Sfinal: fm close (M9) on the final tree — every member's criteria, full gates, prove --hunks against the full suite, and one review of the union diff with an added 'interaction' lens. Commits: one per member, built from the recorded per-change patches and landed together. A background, zero-token `git rebase --exec 'fm check'` certifies each intermediate commit for bisect. A red at the end → why-red ddmin over the per-change trees names the change, and only that member's step reopens. Why verification stays as strong: (a) every criterion still has red→green proof. (b) The shipped tree gets the same full gates, proof and review a single task gets, and all members' criteria are checked on that final tree. The per-task flow checked member i only on an intermediate tree that later members could break, so this is stricter for what ships. (c) Each hunk's mutation proof uses the full suite, not the affected subset. (d) The review sees cross-member interactions that separate reviews miss. (e) Intermediate commits are still certified, just off the critical path. What is lost: immediate full-suite detection of a break the per-change oracle cannot see (a test that never executes the changed lines). That is caught at the end and localized by ddmin at zero model cost.
- first slice: Ship `fm milestone ID ID …` on top of cmd_batch: a host brief with the red-tests-first step, per-member change steps with an affected-test oracle (fm check --affected until M10 lands), a final fm close, and per-member commits built from recorded per-change diffs. Test: a 3-member toy milestone where member 2 breaks member 1 is caught at the end, and ddmin names member 2.
- bar/numbers: Measured: tasks with edits average 52 Opus calls = 8.8 orientation + 33.7 edit span + 9.6 verify/close (35% fixed). 39% of 729 finished tasks shared a code file with one finished in the previous 24 h; 315 of 729 (43%) fall into same-file clusters of 3+. Reviewers ran 341 times. Alone (nothing else in the stack): 18.4 fixed calls amortized over 3 members = −12.3 calls per member × 43% of tasks ≈ −5.3 calls per task (−11% E). In the stack, the fixed residue is ≈6 Opus calls (plan 2, orientation Asks 2, close verdict 2); a 3-member milestone pays it once: −4 × 43% = −1.7 calls per task. Full gates once per 3 members: gate time × (0.57 + 0.43/3) = ×0.71. Reviews ÷3 for those tasks.

### Affinity scheduler with pipelined prefetch: the queue reorders itself so neighbours share files, and the next task's room is built while this one closes [R2 · Token economics; build; value 4, M]
Demo: fm queue reports: 'reordered 9 items into 3 affinity runs (fmguard ×3, statusline ×2, docs ×2) · est. −38% reads'. As T-0820 enters close-out, a ghost row appears in the pane: 'preparing T-0821: pack 5.8k · baseline gate ✓ · 4 facts hits'. When T-0820 closes, T-0821's clean room opens already seeded, and its first Opus call is an edit, not a grep. 'The next task was ready before this one finished?' Queue order today follows the canonical stage order and dependencies, and the measured result is that consecutive tasks in a session share only 15% of their read files (median 0). Ordering by file affinity inside the stage constraints, and building the next room in the background (pack, facts, Asks, baseline gate), turns orientation into precomputed data.
- mechanism: fm queue: within each canonical stage bucket, respecting dependencies, a greedy nearest-neighbour tour over the Jaccard similarity of predicted footprints (T-0445 footprints + fmmap co-change + scope). Prefetch: when the active task reaches its last step (fm next knows), launch a background job that builds the next task's context pack (outline + spans ranked by round-1 personalized PageRank, facts-cache hits, Haiku Asks for the brief's open questions), runs its baseline gate (fm check on the current tree, reused if unchanged), and writes pack.md for SessionStart(clear) (M1). The prefetch is discarded if the queue or the tree under it changes. Milestone candidates (M11) are surfaced from the same similarity.
- first slice: Ship affinity ordering in fm queue (stage- and dependency-preserving) plus the prefetch job writing pack.md. Tests: the ordering never violates dependencies or stage order, and a stale prefetch is discarded.
- bar/numbers: Measured: an 8.8-call orientation phase per task with edits; mean read overlap between consecutive tasks of 15%; baseline gate runs are inside the 47.6 h of test time. Alone: assume neighbour overlap rises from 15% to 40%, cutting orientation reads ≈25% (≈ −2 calls per task, −4% E). In the stack: one orientation Ask per task instead of two (inside M6's 5.5), baseline gate time off the critical path (≈ −0.5 min per task), and more tasks eligible for milestones.

### Intent edits with speculative drafting: Opus says what changes, Haiku writes the bytes, Opus reads a 30-line diff [R2 · Token economics; build; value 5, L]
Demo: Claude writes Intent(file=fmguard.py, symbol=_heredoc_body, change='treat a quoted python heredoc after a cd DIR && chain like a bare one; keep ; and pipes on the text match', test='test_guard_pipes::test_cd_chain_heredoc'). That is 70 tokens. Six seconds later a 24-line diff arrives with 'daemon: test_cd_chain_heredoc ✓, 31 affected ✓', and Claude accepts. Meanwhile, while Claude was thinking about step 3, Haiku had already drafted step 3's intent from the plan; it waits as 'draft ready (unverified)'. 'The expensive model stopped typing code, and the code is checked before it even sees it?' Output tokens are the most expensive tokens: Opus 5.5 charges $20/M, 40× Haiku 5.5's $0.50/M. 46% of Opus's visible output is edit payload (Edit tool 26%, python edit scripts 20%), and Bash command text — mostly those heredocs — is the largest single source of context residency (21%). An intent protocol makes writing code the cheap model's job. The expensive model verifies by reading a diff, and a cached token costs Opus 1/100 as much to read as a token costs it to write ($0.2 vs $20 per M).
- mechanism: An `Intent` tool (mod $.tool.register, or MCP) with schema {file, symbol|range, change, constraints, test}. It runs a Haiku 5.5 headless child with Read/Edit tools in a detached scratch worktree of the current tree (dirty files copied in), with at most 2 attempts. It must make the named test pass under the daemon (M10) or report failure. It returns a unified diff (≤60 lines; bigger diffs return a hunk list with stats) plus the oracle verdict. Opus accepts — fm then applies the patch with `git apply`, through fmguard's file-write checks exactly like an Edit — or answers with a revised intent. Speculation: at each step start, fm launches Haiku drafts for the next 1-2 planned steps whose intent follows from the plan text; drafts are dropped if the tree under them changed.
- first slice: Ship the `fm intent` CLI: a Haiku child in a detached worktree returning diff + test verdict, applied with git apply; no speculation yet. Benchmark on 15 past single-file edits taken from transcripts; ship only if first-try acceptance is at least 70%.
- bar/numbers: Measured: edit payload is 46% of Opus visible output; thinking is 43% of output tokens; output is 18.6% of Opus 5.5 main spend. Bash command text is 21% and Write input 6% of context residency. Per finished task: 4.1 python edit-script calls + 2.5 Edit/Write calls. Effect in the stack: edit payload tokens −75% (12.7k → 3.2k per task), edit calls 6.6 → 3.5 (multi-hunk intents), and less tool-input growth. Haiku cost ≈ 3.5 × (15k in + 1.5k out) ≈ $0.008 per task, with drafts that get discarded adding ≈$0.008. Opus still reads every diff (≈600 tokens each).

### Surprise-priced thinking: reasoning effort follows how surprised the model should be, set per request [R2 · Token economics; build; value 3, S]
Demo: The pane's effort chip changes per call: 'low' when recording a verdict or applying an accepted intent, 'high' the moment the daemon reports an unexpected red, 'xhigh' when that red contradicts the plan's prediction. Session summary: 'thinking tokens −31% · first-try-green unchanged'. 'It thinks hard only when reality disagrees with it?' Round 1's governor set effort per plan step. Setting effort per request from measured surprise (did the last observation match the prediction?) puts thinking exactly where it buys something, and routine calls stop paying for deliberation.
- mechanism: foreman-ui turn.step hook (it accepts an effort per request; Opus 5.5 lists per_turn_effort). Inputs: the class of the last tool result (verdict green/red, exit code, an unexpected file change), whether the previous turn declared a prediction (fm next asks for one-line predictions before checks), and the stage. Policy: low for bookkeeping, apply and accept turns; medium by default; high on the first red; xhigh on a red that contradicts the prediction or on a second consecutive failure. M22 tunes the thresholds against rework and red-after-green rates.
- first slice: Ship the turn.step policy table in register.tsx, keyed on the last tool result and the stage, plus the pane chip. A/B it by session parity for a week with fm cost.
- bar/numbers: Measured: thinking is 43% of Opus 5.5 output tokens, and output is 18.6% of spend. The binary's effort cost index for Opus 5 is low 0.67, medium 0.76, high 1.0. Assumption: about 60% of the remaining calls run at low, with ≈40% less thinking each, so thinking falls 20% in the stack (10.0k → 8.0k per task). Alone at baseline: ≈ −0.2 × 43% × 18.6% ≈ −1.6% of spend.

### Per-request model router: Opus decides, Haiku performs — switched inside one conversation [R2 · Token economics; build; value 4, M]
Demo: The pane shows a two-colour strip of the last 20 requests: violet (Opus) for planning, judging diffs and debugging; grey (Haiku 5.5) for applying an accepted intent, running a macro, fm close and commits. When a grey turn hits an unexpected exit code, the next request is violet automatically. Session tally: 31 requests, 19 on Opus. 'Two models take turns in the same chat, routed by what the next step needs?' Model choice is per session or per subagent today. The mod's turn.step hook can rewrite the model of every request, so one conversation can be piloted by the expensive model and driven by the cheap one. The switching cost (each model has its own prompt cache) is only affordable once contexts are clean-room sized, which is why this stacks on M1.
- mechanism: turn.step(model) in register.tsx routes to claude-haiku-5-5 at low effort when the previous Opus turn ended in a tool call that is mechanical and whose outcome it predicted (applying an accepted Intent, a macro, fm close, a commit, a listed command) and context is under 80-100k (Haiku's long-prompt pricing is 5× above 100k). It routes back to Opus on any non-zero exit, unexpected output, user message or declared decision point. Cache economics: Haiku's first request writes the context to Haiku's cache (46k × $0.2/M ≈ $0.009); returning to Opus writes only the suffix Haiku added (counted in M1's growth). Claude Code itself refuses models the org policy disallows (the binary logs 'model … is not allowed by policy').
- first slice: Route two classes only: applying an Intent the model accepted, and running fm close. Return to Opus automatically on any non-zero exit. Add the pane strip, and compare first-try-green and rework rates by session parity.
- bar/numbers: Measured: today's Opus calls include 7.6% bookkeeping and 25.5% misc Bash. After M6-M13 about 20.5 Opus-decided calls per task remain. Assumption: ~25% are mechanical continuations, so 5.1 move to Haiku and 15.4 stay on Opus. Haiku cost ≈ 5.1 × (46k × $0.01/M + 1.5k × $0.5/M) + one cache write ≈ $0.015 per task. In the stack: −25% of Opus calls.

### Advisor inversion for small work: Haiku 5.5 runs the loop, and Opus 5.5 is the server-side advisor it consults [R2 · Token economics; build; value 4, M]
Demo: An S task, 'fix typo in fm doctor message + test', is picked up. The band shows 'executor Haiku 5.5 · advisor Opus 5.5'. Haiku plans, edits and runs the check. Once, it asks the advisor 'is changing this string safe for the statusline parser?', gets one paragraph back, and finishes. Cost: $0.05, against a measured ~$5 of Opus per finished task today (all tiers). 'The expensive model was only on call?' Claude Code 2.1.295 ships a server-side advisor tool (the advisorModel setting; feature-gated in the binary): the executor model consults a stronger advisor. Inverting who is in charge — a cheap loop with an expensive advisor — fits small, well-specified work, and Foreman decides per task class from measured outcomes.
- mechanism: fm run and lanes start S-tier headless sessions with --model claude-haiku-5-5 and settings {advisorModel: 'claude-opus-5-5'}. fm doctor first probes availability and compatibility (the binary has an 'advisor_incompatible' error path). The brief's Execution prompt says when to consult (after planning, before finishing). Gates are unchanged (fm close), so the executor cannot skip them. In interactive sessions the budget planner (M17) proposes it for S tasks.
- first slice: Ship the advisor probe in fm doctor plus `fm run --advisor` for S tasks in lanes, recording per-task cost and gate outcomes to state/spend.jsonl. Compare 20 S tasks against Opus-loop lanes.
- bar/numbers: Measured: 36% of finished tasks with a recorded tier are S (172 of 478). This is upside only and is not counted in the stack arithmetic: whether advisor consults are cached on a ~40k context is unknown. Three uncached consults would be ≈120k input-equivalent, against ≈150-200k E for a task under the stack, so it wins only if consults are cached or fewer. M17 decides per task class from fm cost data.

### Budget-aware planner: every plan is priced, and Foreman picks the cheapest path that meets the bar [R2 · Token economics; build; value 4, M]
Demo: At intake for a FIX M task, the plan shows three priced paths: 'A Opus solo ≈ $1.30, 22 min, P(first-try green) 0.81 · B Opus plan + Haiku intents + daemon ≈ $0.41, 16 min, P 0.79 · C Haiku loop + Opus advisor ≈ $0.12, 19 min, P 0.58 (below bar 0.75)'. It picks B and says why in one line. After a week, the P estimates come from this repo's own history. 'It has a price list for its own ways of working, and it learns which ones actually work here?' Routing today is a fixed tier→model map. The planner treats the execution configuration as a decision with two measured models. Cost per call is a function of context, model and effort, fit by fm cost. Success is measured per configuration × type × tier: first-try green, reopen and escape rates. It picks the cheapest configuration whose predicted quality clears the bar, and also chooses between milestone, lane and serial execution.
- mechanism: fmcost.py exposes the per-call cost model, fit on transcripts (C, N, O, W per type × tier × configuration). fmbudget.py adds success statistics from the ledger: reopened tasks, escapes and review findings, each task tagged with its configuration. Intake writes 'execution: B' into the brief; fm next, the router (M15), clean rooms (M1) and Intent (M13) obey it. 10% of eligible tasks run the second-best path (logged) so estimates stay honest. Budget pace (fm budget) raises the price weight when the week is ahead of pace.
- first slice: Ship the cost-model fit plus priced paths shown at intake and stored in the brief (no automatic selection yet), and tag finished tasks with their configuration so success statistics accumulate.
- bar/numbers: Measured cost spread: mean $1.38 vs median $0.49 input-equivalent per task segment (a heavy tail); 23% of known-tier tasks are L. In the stack arithmetic, the planner sends ~90% of tasks down path B and keeps an Opus-heavy path for ~10% (L or novel work), budgeted at 2× the stack's per-task cost.

### Warm workers: every cheap worker shares one byte-identical prefix, and reviewers start as cache hits [R2 · Token economics; build; value 2, S]
Demo: fm budget shows 'worker cache hits 94% · cold starts today: 3'. A reviewer spawned at 14:02 and an Ask at 14:03 both open with cache_read 9.8k and cache_write 0.4k. 'Even the helpers ride the prompt cache?' Each subagent starts cold today. fm-reviewer's first call hit the cache only 27% of the time (341 runs, median first write 10k), and fm-recon only 21%. Byte-identical worker prefixes, plus parent forks where independence doesn't matter, make helpers cache-native. This matters once Ask and Intent spawn about ten cheap workers per task.
- mechanism: All Foreman children (fmideas.run_child, Ask, Intent, reviewers via claude -p) take their system prompt from one generated file per repo per day: a stable worker preamble + tool list + repo map (fmmap.compact), ordered stable-first, with task-specific text only in the user turn. The model and flags are identical, so the cache key matches. Agent definitions avoid per-run text in their prompts. Tool-less side questions use $.model.fork, which reads the parent's cache and writes no transcript. state/spend.jsonl gains cache_read and cache_write fields read from each child's usage.
- first slice: Ship one shared worker-prefix file for fmideas.run_child and fmsecond children, plus the spend.jsonl cache fields, with the hit rate shown in fm budget.
- bar/numbers: Measured: subagents are 6.5% of spend ($447 of $6,861). Cold first-call writes total 5.3M tokens. First-call cache hit rates: reviewer 27%, recon 21%, builder 68%, general-purpose 75%. Effect: worker first-call writes −80%. In dollars this is small (≈$0.002 per Haiku worker call, ≈$0.03 per Sonnet review); the main win is first-token latency for the ~10 cheap workers per task the stack adds.

### Review packs: reviewers read the diff, the facts and the call graph — once per milestone [R2 · Token economics; build; value 3, S]
Demo: The reviewer's transcript has 2 calls. Call 1 reads a 7k-token pack: the union diff with ±15 lines, the callers of each changed function, facts-cache entries for the touched symbols, and the criteria. Call 2 returns 3 findings, 2 verified by line. Today's reviewer took 12 calls and read 60k. 'The reviewer got a briefing instead of a building tour?' Reviews are the biggest subagent job (341 runs in 30 days), and each one re-explores the repo. Prebuilt packs turn review into read-and-judge, and milestones (M11) make it one review per cluster with an interaction lens.
- mechanism: fm audit prep (exists) also emits a pack: the union diff from the task or milestone start snapshot, the callers of each changed function (fmmap, or graphify when present), facts (M7) for touched symbols, the criteria and evidence lines, and the lens's last 6 findings (exists). Reviewer agent definitions say: read the pack first, and use tools only to verify a specific suspicion. fm close (M9) runs the review headless on Sonnet, in parallel with the gates.
- first slice: Ship pack generation in fm audit prep and the reviewer prompt change. Compare the confirmed-findings rate and calls per review over 20 audits.
- bar/numbers: Measured: fm-reviewer ran 341 times (median 12 calls, 53.7M input-equivalent tokens); general-purpose subagents ran 51 times (87.1M). Effect: calls per review 12 → 3, and reviews ÷3 for the 43% of tasks in milestones. Together with M18: subagent spend −60%, from $0.45 to ≈$0.18 per finished task. Close-out is also faster.

### No-poll waiting: background jobs wake the session, and the drive works on something else meanwhile [R2 · Token economics; build; value 4, S]
Demo: Claude starts a 9-minute media gate. Instead of `until grep -q EXIT log; do sleep 30; done` holding the session, the call returns in 1 s: 'watching gate in background → you'll be woken'. Claude moves on to planning the next queued item. Nine minutes later a notification lands: 'gate EXIT 0 · 0 failures'. 'It stopped babysitting its own builds?' Foreground polling is the largest wall-clock sink measured: 64 h of until/while…sleep loops in 30 days, 32% of all foreground Bash time, plus `timeout 590` waits. Claude Code already has background Bash with completion notifications, and Monitor; Foreman makes them the only way to wait.
- mechanism: Sync PreToolUse(Bash): fmguard's parser recognises wait shapes (until/while loops containing sleep, sleep ≥ 30, tail -f piped into grep -q) and rewrites them (updatedInput) into a detached waiter (setsid + a waiter script that exits when the condition holds). The call returns 'waiting in background (id …)' with additionalContext telling the model to continue other work. When the waiter exits, the existing T-0145 background-shell handling in the Stop drive, or a PostToolUse note, wakes the session. Meanwhile the drive takes side work from the queue (fm next already offers it) or prefetches (M12).
- first slice: Ship the wait-shape rewrite for until/while-sleep loops and long sleeps, plus a drive note to continue other work. Test it over the 1,241 recorded wait commands, fm replay style: every one is recognised, and no non-wait command is.
- bar/numbers: Measured: 199.5 h of foreground Bash in 30 days = poll/sleep 64.4 h (32%) + tests 47.6 h + builds/runs 32.5 h + fm 23.6 h + other. 988 background Bash calls already exist. Per finished task, 3.9 of 19.7 min of wall-clock is polling; after the rewrite ≈0.4 min stays visible (when the queue has nothing else to do). Tokens are neutral, since a wait was one call, but waits past an hour also bust the cache (M5).

### Milestone fan-out: independent members of a milestone build in parallel cheap lanes while Opus judges diffs [R2 · Token economics; build; value 3, L]
Demo: Milestone M-0014 has 4 members, and 2 are footprint-independent. The pane shows two lanes running Haiku intents in worktrees, with daemon verdicts streaming, while Opus reviews lane 1's first diff. Total time: 14 min instead of 23. 'It's running a little team, and the expensive model is just the tech lead?' Lanes today run whole S/M tasks on full models, and round 1's contract-first swarm splits an L task by seams. Milestone fan-out parallelizes at the member level with cheap executors and a single expensive judge, while sharing one plan, one pack and one end gate.
- mechanism: fm milestone marks members whose predicted footprints are disjoint (T-0445) as parallel. Each gets a detached worktree (fmlanes) running Intent (M13) and the daemon (M10) on Haiku. Opus receives the diffs in arrival order and accepts or revises them. Accepted patches land in member order in the main tree, and the end gate runs once (M11).
- first slice: Ship parallel execution of two independent milestone members in lanes with Intent, landing in order with one end gate, tested on a toy repo.
- bar/numbers: Measured: lane infrastructure exists (fmlanes; 27 lane_new events); 43% of tasks fall in same-file clusters. Assumption: 40% of milestone members are footprint-independent, so 30% of their model + test time overlaps: 0.43 × 0.3 × 6.0 min ≈ −0.8 min per finished task. Tokens are neutral (same intents, same reviews).

### Self-tuning economics: every knob in the stack runs as a live experiment, and only what holds quality is kept [R2 · Token economics; build; value 3, M]
Demo: fm digest: 'This week the stack ran 212 tasks. Router threshold moved 100k → 80k (Haiku turns above 80k had 2× rework). Distiller span budget raised for Rust files (re-reads ran 3× higher). Intent drafting disabled for TSX (41% acceptance). Cost per finished task $1.38 all-in (−80%); reopen rate 2.1% (baseline 2.3%).' 'It tunes its own frugality against my quality bar, with receipts?' Token savings are easy to claim and easy to fake: cheap now, rework later. Every knob in the stack — clean-room boundary, distiller budgets, Ask nudges, router thresholds, effort policy, milestone size — is assigned per session and judged on cost per finished task, under quality guardrails measured from Foreman's own ledger: first-try green, reopens, escapes, review findings and user corrections.
- mechanism: state/econ/arms.json maps each knob to its arms. SessionStart picks an arm per knob by Thompson sampling on cost per finished task (fm cost already attributes tokens to tasks). Hard constraint: an arm whose quality metrics are worse than control by more than the noise band is frozen. Quality signals come from ledger events (escape, reopen, corrections). Knob changes are recorded via fm decide and reported in fm digest. This reuses round-1 'Live self-experiments' machinery, specialised to a cost objective with quality constraints.
- first slice: Ship arms for two knobs (clean-room boundary: task vs 40%; distiller on/off), assigned per session, with an fm digest line comparing cost per finished task and escape/reopen rates.
- bar/numbers: Measured base rates for the guardrails: 29 escapes over 729 finished tasks in 30 days (4.0%). Detecting a 1-point quality change needs ~200 tasks per arm, about a week at the current pace. Effect: it guards the stack's −81% against regressions and finds the last 5-10%. It is not counted in the stack arithmetic.

### The stack: Opus conducts, everything else is cheap or free — −81% expensive-model tokens and −55% wall-clock per finished task [R2 · Token economics; build; value 5, L]
Demo: 9:00: the user types 'work the queue' over 12 items. Foreman says 'milestoned 6 into 2 (shared files), reordered the rest by affinity'. In the pane, each task opens in a ~30k clean room seeded with a 6k pack, and the gauge never passes 60k. A violet/grey strip shows Opus planning and judging while Haiku performs. Ask answers arrive with line numbers. Intents come back as 30-line diffs already green under the daemon. Waits happen in the background while the next room is prefetched. Each task ends with one `fm close` verdict. 11:00: fm cost says '12 finished · Opus tokens per finished task 0.23M (last week 1.26M) · $1.40 all-in (last week $6.97) · 9 min per task (last week 20) · escapes 0, reopen 0'. A senior engineer: 'same gates, same reviews, a fifth of the spend and half the time?' No single trick gets past −50%, because Opus cost is (number of calls) × (context per call) + (output) + (cache writes), and the measured baseline is bad on both multiplied factors: 48 calls per finished task at a mean 335k context. The stack attacks each factor with mechanisms that compound, because each makes the next affordable. Clean rooms shrink context, which makes per-request model switching cheap. Ask, intents and the daemon remove calls, which makes clean rooms shorter. Milestones and the scheduler amortize what is left. The expensive model ends up doing only three things — decide, judge diffs, judge verdicts — and never reads raw files or tool output.
- mechanism: Build order (each step useful alone, each measured with fm cost before and after): 1. M1 clean rooms (−46% alone). 2. M10 test daemon + M20 no-poll waits (biggest time levers). 3. M6 Ask + M7 facts cache. 4. M9 fm close + zero-call bookkeeping. 5. M2 input-side distillation + M3 delta re-reads. 6. M11 milestones (+ M12 affinity/prefetch). 7. M13 intent edits. 8. M15 router + M14 surprise-priced effort + M5 cache shaping. 9. M4 lean prefix. 10. M8 compiled skills. 11. M17 budget planner + M22 self-tuning guardrails. 12. M16, M18, M19, M21. Primitives: PreToolUse updatedInput, PostToolUse additionalContext and updatedToolOutput, SessionStart(clear), foreman-ui turn.step (model and effort per request), $.model.fork, $.tool.register, $.prompt.submit, $.session.compact, clock.after; headless `claude -p --model claude-haiku-5-5` children; git worktrees; sqlite3; the advisorModel setting.
- first slice: Ship M1 (clean rooms with the model.fork handoff) as the first M task, then M10 and M6. Add `fm cost --per-finished-task` reporting E, $ and wall-clock per finished task split into N, C, O and W, so every later mechanism's share of the stack is proven on the user's own transcripts rather than asserted.
- bar/numbers: Re-measured (round 1's out/efficiency.json was not present), over 30 days to 2026-10-09: 1,518 transcripts (1,050 main + 468 subagent), 56,768 API calls, $6,861 at the list prices in the claude 2.1.295 binary, and 984 finished tasks (fm task finish/done in main sessions). All-in that is $6.97 per finished task; the main thread is 93.5% of it. Opus 5.5 prices: $4/M in, $20/M out, $8/M 1-hour cache write, $0.2/M cache read. Haiku 5.5: $0.1 / $0.5 / $0.2 / $0.01 per M below 100k prompts.

BASELINE per finished task, main thread, from the measured means (Opus input-equivalent E = input + 2·write(1h) + 0.05·read + 5·output):
N0 = 48 Opus calls (47.2k main calls / 984).
C0 = 335k mean context per call.
O0 = 48.4k output per task: thinking 43% = 20.8k; visible 27.6k = edit payload 12.7k + other tool inputs 12.7k + text 2.2k.
W0 = 108k cache writes per task (2.25k per call).
E0 = 48 × 0.05 × 335k (804k) + 5 × 48.4k (242k) + 2 × 108k (216k) = 1,262k → $5.05. Cross-check: measured Opus 5.5 main spend is $0.108 per call × 48 = $5.2.

A) CALLS N: 48 → 20.5 → 15.4 (−68%).
read/search 18.2 → 5.5 (M6 Ask + M7 facts + M12 packs; bursts average 2.31 calls): −12.7.
tests 4.2 → 0.5 (M10 daemon): −3.7.
fm bookkeeping 3.6 → 1.0 (M9): −2.6.
edits 6.6 → 3.5 (M13 intents): −3.1.
misc Bash 12.2 → 8.5 (M8 macros, M20 waits): −3.7.
fixed per-task residue amortized by milestones (M11; 43% of tasks in clusters of 3+): −1.7.
Total 20.5 Opus-decided calls. The router (M15) moves 25% of mechanical continuations to Haiku → 15.4 Opus calls.

B) CONTEXT C: 335k → 46k (−86%).
Prefix 28.7k → 21k (M4). Seed (brief + pack + handoff) 6k (M1, M12).
Growth per call 2.27k → 1.6k (M2, M3, M13; already raised 30% because each call now carries denser results), = 2.13k per Opus call once the Haiku-routed turns' additions are counted.
C1 = 21k + 6k + 2.13k × 15.4/2 = 43.4k; milestones' rolling rooms (capped at 80k) blend it to 46k.

C) OUTPUT: 48.4k → 18.2k per task (−62%).
Edit payload 12.7k → 3.2k (M13). Other tool inputs 12.7k → 4.8k (fewer, shorter calls). Text 2.2k unchanged.
Thinking: 1.5× the old per-call average on the remaining, decision-dense calls (650 × 15.4 = 10.0k), then −20% (M14) = 8.0k.

D) WRITES: 108k → 42.8k per task (−60%).
Room seed 10k (the stable 21k prefix is a cross-session cache hit) + 15.4 × 2.13k suffix = 42.8k. Mid-task busts ≈ 0 (M5).

RESULT: E1 = 15.4 × 0.05 × 46k (35.4k) + 5 × 18.2k (91k) + 2 × 42.8k (85.6k) = 212k → −83.2%, $0.85 instead of $5.05 of Opus per finished task.
Planner allowance (M17: 10% of tasks stay Opus-heavy at 2×): 0.9 × 212k + 0.1 × 424k = 233k → −81.5%.
Cheap-model cost added: Asks $0.017 + intents and drafts $0.016 + routed turns $0.015 + digests ≈ $0.05 per task.
All-in $ per finished task: main $6.52 × 0.185 = $1.21, + Haiku $0.05, + subagents $0.18 (M18, M19) = $1.44, against $6.97 (−79%).

SENSITIVITY:
Clean rooms alone: −46%.
Everything except clean rooms (C stays 335k): 15.4 × 0.05 × 335k (258k) + 91k + 2 × 32.8k (66k) = 415k → −67%.
C only reaches 80k: 238k → −81%.
N only reaches 24 (Ask saves 1.5 calls per burst, partial daemon adoption): 24 × 0.05 × 50k (60k) + 5 × 26k (130k) + 2 × 60k (120k) = 310k → −75%.
So the 80% target needs both levers, N ≤ ~18 and C ≤ ~60k; neither alone gets there.

WALL-CLOCK per finished task (active hours / tasks, measured 19.7 min):
Baseline: model 7.6, polling 3.9, tests 2.9, gates inside fm 1.4, builds/runs 2.0, other 1.9.
After:
- Model 7.6 → 4.2. Opus is 18.2k output × 7.3 ms/token + 15.4 × 2 s first-token time = 2.7 min; Haiku turns and workers add 1.5 min (assumes Haiku 5.5 per-token speed ≥ Opus — not yet measured).
- Polling 3.9 → 0.4 (M20).
- Tests 2.9 → 0.9 visible (M10; 70% overlapped).
- Gates 1.4 → 1.0 (M9 parallel close-out; M11 gates once per 3 members).
- Builds 2.0 → 2.0 (unchanged).
- Other 1.9 → 1.6 (M8).
- Fan-out (M21) −0.8 and prefetch (M12) −0.5.
Total 8.8 min → −55%. Without M20 it is 12.3 min (−38%): no-poll waiting is the biggest time lever. That assumes the queue has other work while a gate runs; a single long gate with an empty queue still takes its own time.
